The bells hadn't started down by the river. The city was bottomed-out silent, that deep night-silence in which Varanasi alone, of all cities he had ever known, did not feel empty — merely paused, like a singer between verses.

"You went to Manikarnika last night," Ram said.

He did not turn around. It was not a question.

Rudra's mouth opened with the first reflexive lie — and out of some deeper, wearier reflex, closed again. Lies were for things that could be hidden. "For an hour," he said. "Veer ke saath."

"The other two were the girls from the college," Ram said. "The quiet one, and the one who carries the notebook."

The city tells me things, Rudra thought. Somehow, the city tells this man things, or the man knows how to lean on the city until it talks. He had long ago stopped asking how. He said nothing.

"Stance," said Ram.

And the session began at double pace.

There are drills that condition muscle and drills that condition reflex and then there is a third category that conditions something neither body nor mind can name, and Ram moved briskly into the third category without announcement. Stance-hold at half-depth with the brass water-lota balanced upside-down on Rudra's crown, and when the lota found equilibrium Ram poured a slow thread of water into it from the jug until surface tension quivered and broke across Rudra's hairline — and then, instead of calling the count, Ram began to *move*, circling with strikes at waist height that had to be parried without spilling, without rising, without the knees unlocking. Four counts in, hold four, out four — breathe, parry, don't spill, don't rise — the lota's rim a cold ring of pressure on the skull and below it the drills doubling and redoubling: the eight-parry chain became sixteen; the sixteen grew footwork; the footwork grew eyes-closed.

"Again."

Pour. Parry. Breathe.

"Again."

By 4:30, water and sweat had made one river of him. His shoulders — the left one still carrying its week-old ache and last night's impact through the bamboo pole — began their bright, joint-deep complaint. His knuckles had split again inside the wrapping; he could feel the cotton going dark and warm. He did not look at it. Down by the ghat the first bells of the morning-singers trembled into the dark, and the neem leaves above the courtyard breathed a small pre-dawn breeze across his streaming face, and it would all have been bearable except that the brass lota was refilled, and the strikes became throws, and every throw put him on the flagstones — the broken, root-heaved flagstones, colder than the air, older than him —

The cold of the stone went through his palms and up his arms and arrived, like a guest that knew the house, at a door behind his ribs.

And then it was fourteen years ago.

---

Winter. The hour before the dawn's first grey. Fog off the river so thick that the courtyard walls stopped existing at shoulder height and the neem floated like the mast of a sunk ship.

He was five.

His feet were blue and then they weren't, because the blood inside them had opinions about the stone, and the stone won. Ram had taken him out of bed — gently, his mother's hands had been the gentle ones and even *they* had been gentle but firm, dressing him, buttoning him, her eyes saying one thing and her mouth saying nothing — and walked him into the fog, and pointed at the far wall, and said: "Run. Wall to wall."

The flagstones at the courtyard's eastern edge were broken. A root of the neem had heaved three of them up decades ago, and the shards lay in shallow-up and shallow-down angles, frost-filmed, sharp as pottery. His five-year-old feet, soft as bread, found every edge.

He ran. He cried. He kept running *because* he was crying, some crucial thread between effort and dignity having been cut at the first shard, and from the doorway he could feel his mother's hands around her own mouth, her whole body a held breath, but she did not come out of the doorway, because his father had told her — he would not learn this sentence for years, but it had been said, in the doorway, before the fog came down — *if you run to him now, the stones will have taught him nothing except that running from them works.*

"Appa, bas—" The word broke off snot and air. "Bas, Appa, *bas*—"

"Wall to wall," said Ram.

The fog sat down. His soles bloomed red. Somewhere beyond the walls a conch sounded from a house shrine and died in the fog before it reached him, and he fell the third time on the shallow-down shard, and this time he lay on his face and waited with the specific totality with which five-year-olds wait, confident that the pain had reached its legal limit and the adult world would now enforce the rules.

Boots came into the fog. His father knelt — *knelt*, to his eye level, so close he could see that the irises were not one colour but rings of them — and his voice was not angry. That was the thing no one ever believed later in the remembering. It was not angry at all.

"The ground doesn't care about your pain," said Ram, and touched him, once, between the shoulders, two fingers, flat, like a brand being placed. "Get up. Wall to wall."

He got up. He ran wall to wall, crying. Then wall to wall the other way, and the wall at that end was made of fog, and then his father said, "Now: falling," the way the drills always arrived, unannounced and unnamed, and he learned to hit the stones the wrong way until the wrong way stopped being wrong — the tuck, the slapping forearm, the flatness of the fall —

When it ended the sun was the colour of ghee and his mother came out of the doorway with water and cloth, and she did not look at his father, and his father stood at the neem trunk with his back to them both, and Rudra could see — though five-year-olds file such things where adults keep regrets — that his father's jaw was working, the muscles at the hinge knotting and unknotting, knotting and unknotting, like a man chewing something too large to swallow.

He had hurtled through school years after that with a body that remembered the stone. He had run gullies barefoot and not gotten sick. He had learned — his body had learned; his consent had never been consulted — that the ground doesn't care, and pain is weather, and weather passes.

Only once had it stopped, those years, only once —

---

"DOWN."

The present returned bodily, and Rudra dropped, forearms slapping stone, a hand-shift of cold entering through the split knuckles. The fog wasn't here. There was no fog yet. The lota lay on its side, its water racing toward the neem roots.

"Up. Again. Breathe."

"Why?" Rudra said.

The word sat in the courtyard like a foreign animal.

Ram had already turned to reset the staff. He half-stopped, head angled slightly back, listening to the acoustics of his son saying *why* at 4:52 in the morning — by Rudra's calculation, the first time in approximately six years that the word had been deployed during a session.

"Because the drill isn't finished," Ram said.

"Then *finish* it," said Rudra. His voice cracked. He hated that it cracked and pushed through it, the way his palms pushed through gravel. "Finish it yourself. What's it for, Appa? Tell me what any of this is for and I'll do double. Triple. I'll run wall to wall till my feet come apart like last time, just— say the thing. One time. Say what this is."

Ram turned all the way around.

The cold had made his eyes lighter, some trick of the season, some geometry of the sets and the iris. He looked at his son standing chest-heaving in the wheeled white dawn — bruised jaw, blood-dark wraps, steam coming off the shoulders in loose banners — and his expression did the thing it had done a thousand times across the last two decades, which was *nothing*, and behind the nothing everything: the arithmetic of a man who had spent nineteen years in a counting-house, knowing the ledgers were burning, telling no one.

"It is for the thing that is coming," Ram said.

"What thing."

"If you knew its name, you would think knowing the name mattered." Ram bent and righted the lota with his foot, and his voice came up from the foot-level and was colder than the stone. "Stand. The drill isn't finished."

"Kal raat Manikarnika pe—" Rudra's own mouth launched it before the lists-keeper in his skull could sandbag the gate: "*That* thing? That was the thing? You knew something was going to— is that why last night you— how do you *know*, Appa—"

"Ek sau," said Ram quietly. "Push-ups. Ek sau. For asking the same question twice."

It was the coldest register he had. Rudra had its whole piano by heart: the flat middle octave for drills, the half-tone down for mornings when the past was closer, and this — this quiet broken-glass register, which was not anger at all, which sat close to the lowest note the man possessed and which historically crept in exactly when the truth had gotten nearest the door and been met with a chair wedged under the handle.

And here was the thing — here was the thing Rudra carried to bed later and could not put down: the push-ups weren't punishment. He understood, with a small click like a step-stone settling, on rep forty-one underneath his father's unmoving stare, that they were *replacement*. The man had run out of drill to fill the silence with, and had reached for the nearest one. The answer was supposed to live where the push-ups went instead.

At eighty, his left shoulder announced infirmity. His father watched him catalogue the failure, compensate with hip, fail again.

"Bas kar."

Rudra rose on muscles of give-up steam. He looked at his father. His father looked at him.

"Kal raat kuch tha," Rudra said. Low, now; not a question. Not pushing. Presenting a coin. "Manikarnika pe. Main wahan tha. And you're not surprised. You're not *anything*."

"I am tired," his father said, "of seeing it coming for nineteen years, and being unable to say it until after." And then, as cleanly as a shut gate: "Breakfast."

Rudra unwrapped his knuckles on the threshold, balled the cotton, and walked out of the circle. His knees buckled once — traitorously, in plain view, on the third step — and he caught himself and did not look back, because if he looked back the whole structure of his walking-out would collide flat with whatever was still-thawing on his father's face.

So he did not see:

his father, alone in the circle, reach slowly for the abandoned staff, fail the first grip entirely, stand holding the staff in a hand that had begun to tremble from the veins outward, subtly, ruinously, like the ground shivering before the first crack shows. Ram pressed both thumbs down the staff's shaft to still the shake and the shake changed hands, went up the wrists, took the forearms. He stood in the steam of his son's heat and let the trembling happen the way one lets weather happen, acknowledging it with full inventory, two hundred and nineteen muscles public, and the trembling sank into the ground through the balls of his feet and was gone, his body once more the old building, load-bearing, cracked someplace nobody could see.

Ram shut his eyes.

The ember on the northeastern skyline of his thoughts was no longer a coal. It was a ring now, one ring of a stove that had been lit, and he knew the manufacturer.

Fourteen years since the last time the city forgot itself — the pyres lying flat under a wind that had not been wind. He had let himself talk about it that night for an hour in his marriage's lowest voice, and then he had buried the hour with all the hours before it, platformed his life over the trapdoor, and made his son a sword.

"Abhi nahi," he said, to nothing, and the nothing had a face now, a face on a pyre stepping down from the fire with rivers for veins.

"Abhi nahi," he said again, and the wall of fog — fourteen years retreated — leant briefly against the courtyard's east stones, listening.

---

Rukmini had watched all of it from the kitchen doorway.

She had been there for the water, the doubled parries, the why, the coin laid down and refused. She had been there with one hand inside the doorjamb's groove, the groove two decades of her hand had polished, her knuckles around its edges so pale and fixed the wood had slowly turned the colour of bone in the lamplight, one bone yellowing against another.

She was not frightened of *it*. Her fear was not the ordinary blue fear that any wife feels watching a husband dismantle a son with love.

She was frightened because under his collarbone, where the morning's wet chest heaved with the end of push-ups, *she had seen the lines*.

One glimpse, through cotton, door-shadow, twenty feet of dawn-cut steam — a thread-fine gold geometry sited exactly where she had set it nineteen years before with both hands and a name spoken inside her heart against the new red skin, a geometry meant to *close*, and last night something at the ghat had leaned its whole weight on the closing, and the lines had not opened but had *glossed* — the way hairline cracks in stone take shine before they take width.

The seal was being pushed. From the inside now, too.

Three days old he had been. She remembered it from inside her own elbows: the raisin-weight of him; his extraordinary silence during the procedure, a silence she had chosen to read as assent because the alternative was unliveable; her own thumbs against the new sternum, slicked with the boiled oil and something older than oil that she had carried out of a life this house was never told about; the forty hand-positions; the knot-count; the name she had spoken inside her heart only, against the skin, because spoken aloud names travel, and this name must go nowhere, ever, for nineteen years.

Nineteen years she had been granted. It had felt, at three days old, like eternity purchased cheap.

She had rebuilt its inner knots every morning of his life after that — sleep-side, breath-side, in the steam of dal — a mason-mother's daily unwitnessed labour — She had rebuilt its inner knots every morning of his life, in sleep-side, in breath-work, in the showers of the day — and the wall that nineteen years of mornings had built was now carrying load it was never laid for, and last night she had felt its joints jolt four hundred metres away standing in a kitchen doorway of her own house while four streets over the fires lay down flat on a pyre and a dead man remembered he had legs.

Rukmini turned from the doorjamb to the counter, and took up the water-thali to pour, and her own hands betrayed her then — the tremor that had moved through her husband's staff now moved through brass and water, fine and horizontal, the water crossing the steel like shaken lines on graph paper —

She shut her eyes. Her mouth shaped a wordless, one-line something, very old, very *not quite* this world's grammar humming against her lips, an air so brief it died before it could become song, and the water-steel steadied.

When the tremor had entered the brass down to the sleeves, she poured, cup by cup, for her son.

Rudra sat at the wobble-legged table for his paratha with his jaw too clenched to chew properly, so she pressed ghee with her palm's heel and made it soft. She wrapped the re-split hand with boiled cloth — tighter this time, she noticed, tighter than the daughters of her previous mornings had wrapped, and their wraps had already been too tight — and her thumb lingered at his pulse past necessity, counting. Not for heat. She was counting the steadiness of the ember inside him, down two rings since midnight, a coal deciding what to be.

He watched her thumb do this and thought, in the tilted morning light, *she's checking it. Like blood pressure. Like a girl's war-zone hospital books.*

"Ma," he said.

"Bolo."

"What was the thing at the ghat?"

She folded the cloth under. She did the fold as perfectly as she did all folds. Her eyes rose once, passed the doorway where Ram was retrieving his tea, and came back carrying all the weight of the kitchen — the yellow walls, the pots, nineteen winters of thread-heat — to lay every gram of it on his face.

"Khana khao," she said, and pressed his head with the flat of her hand, and bent down and put her lips onto the crown of it through the hair, very fast, like a seed being pocketed.

Which he had not had done to him since he was seven, and which left him utterly unable to speak.

Outside, in the lane, the city broadcast itself through the dhobi's radio hung from the handlebars of his bicycle — the eight-o'clock voices pressed flat: *...ghat par bhagdad mein gyarah zakhmi... officials ne gas-leak ki afwah ko sira se kharij kiya... vidhiyon mein jo viral ho rahi hai uski jaanch—*

The radio dopplered away up the lane, carrying its lies like medicine.

---

Ram sat at last on the courtyard's edge-stone with the tea going cold.

When it was cold enough, he rose and went into the storeroom, where haldi-collared jars and blackened karahis and his late mother's sewing-machine crouched under oilcloth. Behind them, in the masonry's throat, was a skin of oiled cloth about a forearm long, knotted twice with a fisherman's hitch against damp that never came.

His hand rested on it without picking it up the way a cavalryman's hand will rest on a blind horse.

The knot was as it had been for fourteen years — the oilcloth dry, the knot patient — and he unknotted it one hitch, and checked by fingertip the inner wrap, knotted it, set it back behind the stone lips of the wall where the house kept its unsaid, and didn't allow himself one single syllable of the name inside.

He came out wiping groundstain from his fingers like evidence.

Through the wooden rafters, over the courtyard's breath, a single cut neem leaf came down through the sun-quiet, and he caught it, and standing still in the ochre circle with the leaf in his palm, he wondered — in the total temple-ruined privacy of his mind where fear sat enthroned these nineteen years — whether, on an afternoon when the room was full of pyres, the fire at Manikarnika had also once laid flat for his father.

And whether his father had also said *abhi nahi*.

And whether the ground had then said: *nuh.*

---

Rudra took the long way to college, which was not long, and arrived by the lane that was not his lane.

The dhobi's radio-bicycle gossip had ripened overnight into the chai-stall's whole stock-in-trade. Beyond the Srinivas stall's steam, two BCom seniors had a phone out, its cracked screen aimed at the world like a talisman; the video on it had been taken by a mourner's cousin, forty seconds of shaking midnight, and even at a fourth re-watch the crowd's signature gasp made Ajit at the next table lean over without shame. Rudra heard what the stall heard: *—chal na, ye to editing hai—door se le, dur se—matlab raat ko itni bheed mein kya—*

Forty seconds, four lakh views. By breakfast, three of those four lakh links went blank. He noted the pace of their blankness with the part of him that was his father's son, and walked on, and hated the walking-on with a passion that startled him — the first-born instinct of his mother's son, who wanted to turn around and say: *it wasn't a prank, and it wasn't a stampede, and the man it wore was somebody's father, and you will all forget by Thursday, and I will not be able to.*

His friends' group chat, called — for reasons predating him — BHU MECH BATCH '26 FINAL (STRICTLY NOTES), lay in 14-hour silence. Above its unread marker, Veer had dropped a single message at 6:12 AM.

**bhai tu theek hai?**

Under it, three ticks were blue: seen by Ira. seen by Aadhya. Seen by him — the phone had clocked his opened eyes at 6:14 and published them.

His thumb found the reply box and waited over its emptiness, and the morning passed around him entirely: the paan-man slicing his betel into fans; the blind bridge-sweeper tapping; a troop of boys nobody would ever ask anything of laughing too loud at a joke about the stampede.

He typed: **haan. thoda fever tha. kal milte hain.**

He considered it. Deletion, edit, restoration — the thumb hovered with the tremble of a man deciding whether mercy received counts as mercy stolen. His knuckles, wrapped fresh and already damp, pulsed once against the phone's glass back, self-important as proof.

*The truth was that the seal in his chest had gone down one more ring in the night, and a dead man had walked uphill to visit him, and his father had known before the police had, and his mother had kissed his head like she was giving him into someone's keeping — and the four words he could send about any of it were exactly the four sent.*

He hit send. Put the phone away, face-down-deep, bottom pocket. Up ahead the college gate gaped, ordinary, forgiving, daylit; and in it for a heartbeat, wrapped tight around its frame as a garland on a photo-frame, he saw — and did not see, the way one does not see stars at noon — the whole of the last twenty-four hours hanging over the entrance like the smoke that would not bend.

---

# CHAPTER 6 — ROOTS

Aadhya did not sleep on the night after the burning ghat.

This was not unusual only in degree. She had slept badly for nine days — since the morning the banyan had announced its dying, since the cold had learned her name, since some sub-basement of her nineteen years had begun ticking like a wire. But tonight the insomnia had *content*. Tonight the dark behind her lids kept reopening on the same sequence: the flames lying down like grain under pressure; the grey filaments brightening, flowerless; the son's face as his father sat up out of the fire wearing something else's agenda; the crowd's avalanche; and worse than all of it, worm-quiet under the screams, that deep subterranean rustle — *like a garden at midnight heard through a wall* — the sound of roots moving in soil they had no business touching.

She had got off her mattress twice already. Once for water, once for the window. Her wrist remembered cold.

On the bed's far side, illuminated by the street-sodium suffering quietly through the curtain, her physics notebook lay with her half-finished elasticity derivation, and she looked at the neat equations — stress, strain, the young modulus — and they looked like a language from a country that had declared war on hers and lost. *Strain under load. Where was the elastic limit of a day? Of a tree? Of a nineteen-year-old girl who had watched the dead rehearse life?*

At 11:40 PM she accepted, formally, what her feet had decided at ten.

She dressed — jeans, kurti, the dupatta ravelled up to a scarf; thick socks; trembling hands making a report of her shoelaces — and took the family's old grey scooter from the shadow between parked autos where it lived like aloof furniture, key already cut smooth as a fossil from a decade of her mother's kurtas' pockets. Her mother was not in bed. Her mother was on the balcony in the wicker chair — the chair whose creak Aadhya had known since the apartment in Lanka, since the balcony of the sparrow, since all her life's past lives — asleep with wool over her knees and three rows of knitting frozen at the needle. Mouth slightly open. Even in sleep, even there, some watchman-part of the woman's face was *on duty*.

Aadhya eased the door to, the way one takes leave of a shrine.

In the stairwell she felt her own heartbeat in her teeth, and tasted — when she swallowed — the first copper, anticipated, like chewed foil.

The city at midnight was a text she'd read every year of her life and never once at this hour.

Lanka's fruit carts slept under their tarpaulins, stacked like the animals of a wooden ark. The paan kiosk's shutter was a cliff-face of steel, one magazine page of a film star's smile still stapled to it, staring. Somewhere a pressure-hose cleaned tomorrow; somewhere two dogs disputed an inheritance of fish-heads; the old roadside neems pulled the last of their thin leaves tighter into their branches as she passed, as if her headlight were wind. And the ghats — down the long incline of Saket Nagar, four careful lefts beyond the post office — the ghats were *changed*: no floodlights any longer, no ropes, but light-columns of the tv crews were all extinguished and the pyre columns had resumed, grey and unhurried and blameless, the city's appetite washing its face after the incident. She could not see Manikarnika from the overpass and was glad.

She parked outside BHU's lane-barrier on the Sanskrit wing side, which had been tied shut with rubber and apology since her first year, where the wall behind the derelict faculty had lost a panel of fencing to a bohar's root and no engineering section in eleven years had felt the need to remember it.

A watchman slept inside the glass gatehouse, earphones in, head arranged as if prayerfully upon the counter; she had rehearsed the lie all the way here — *my register, sir, exam tomorrow, please sir, two minutes* — but it turned out the campus at half-past midnight stood watch for saboteurs and never once for a girl with a nosebleed starting, slipping through a fence-gap like a thought through a comb. The rubber-bound barrier, the dew, the unmown council lawn stippled with the night's cold drip, the whole elephant-dark of the botanical dead-end behind the Sanskrit building: all of it held still as she walked into it, with the terrible tranquillity of a lung between breaths.

There was no moon. She was glad. She did not fully understand why she was glad until afterward: no moon meant no witnesses that reflected.

The banyan was waiting.

---

Nine days ago the tree had been dying.

Now the tree was *gone* — and what stood under the stars in its place was a thing that looked, from twenty paces, like a banyan drawn by someone who had heard of banyans once, from a witness, briefly, in the dark. The canopy's geometry still spoke its name in silhouette — the flying root-curtains, the trunks-within-trunks, the great wing-spread of the secondary canopy — but all of it was uniformly, exhaustively grey, grey as soap, grey as the slag-ash from the iron-smelters beyond Kamaccha, and even at this distance she could hear it. Not with ears.

*It* — the sound had no right word — was a dryness, a rustle with no motion at its source, like the song of a record needle in an empty groove. Silence had stopped being an absence, she thought, and become a texture under this tree.

She should go home. Her body explained this thoroughly — heartbeat in her teeth, cold-wrist memory climbing the elbows, little hairs standing on her forearms like field-grass pointing all one way under a thunderstorm —

— except there was no thunderstorm —

She circled the trunk once, careful, as she imagined deer must circle water-holes in tiger country. And found the leaf.

*Her leaf* — she knew which it was, would have known it blind. The one her hand had put green back into on the first morning, nine days ago. She had counted it that morning in secret, had irrationally disbelieved her own evidence, a dew-fresh apple-green handkerchief in a congregation of shrivel —

It hung where it had hung.

But in nine days it had gone the colour of pond-water at dusk — not dead, *deeper*, streaked now with grey-black filaments branching from the stem end — anchor-end, she thought wildly — outward. The black was in its veins. It was the same veining that had run rivers under a dead man's skin on a pyre platform. It had been there nine hours or nine minutes. And the leaf, being drunk on her gift, had opened a door for the drinking; the little green she had poured in through the bark was now a cup handle on the tree's ruined door — she understood with animal certainty and without recourse to language: *the thing below had found the tree by the same warmth she'd fed it, and she'd been feeding the tree and the tap below it as one dish.*

This is what I could not explain in the cafeteria, she thought, very tremblingly. With Rudra watching me. With his graveyard bruises. *The tree is not dying, it is being eaten, and it has been eaten for longer than I have known it, and tonight it no longer has any strength to fight, and I am the only person in the world who knows because—*

Because I can hear the difference between a drying-up and a drinking-down.

Because whatever is wrong with the tree — is *what is wrong with me.*

Her eyes were wet. The bare branches scratched a mild anarchy on the high clouds.

She put out her hand — the left one, the hand not yet on bark — and laid it flat against one of the aerial-root curtains, the thick corded strand that had hung in that exact gradient her whole university life.

And got — not memory, nothing so helpful as memory — but *weight*. The strand's dead fibre was a rope hung with uncountable small bells of experience, all rung silent decades ago: the forty years of children who had swung on this very cord and been sworn at from lecture windows; the night in 2019 when the Sanskrit roof had come down and the tree had taken half the falling masonry into these strands like combers into a net, and nobody had ever said thank you; the hidden daily congress of barbers, brief-men, napping mid-day electricians, amavasya-worshippers with their pinches of vermilion, the jamun-sellers' knives nicked into its ankle every July. The banyan had been the Sanskrit wing's second building; it had never charged fees; it was sixty years deep in the gratitude of the uneducated and the devotional habits of the department sweepers, and all of that was still here, banked under bark gone grey as soap —

And all of it was flowing out, grain by grain, down through the root-tips, into a vein she could now feel the shape of the way one feels a pulse through a quilt.

*It's going somewhere,* she thought. *None of this is dying. It's being carried.*

Her knees made the next decision; they folded her down onto the leaf-mould at the trunk's root-flare, where the ground was coldest and therefore — her whole body now knew the arithmetic — nearest the bore of the vein's mouth.

Down below, in the dark inside the dark under them, she *heard* the lines.

---

Once, when she was seven — the balcony, the brown bundle of warm feather weighting its beak open in her palms; the school-flood when she was twelve — the marigolds rucked and drowned and pressed back out of the mud by something that did not know it was a girl; the dog at sixteen — both times she had done it kneeling, with her *hands in the earth*.

So she knelt. Thank you, she thought, to nobody. Palm flat on the mustarded humus of the trunk's root-flare, sixty years of fallen leaf under her fingers, earth-cold and pleasantly ordinary for one dear second —

The second ended.

It did not come as images — it never had before, and she understood only then how little the other times had shown her. It came as *placement*, as if the night had been asked to make room for her. Her head lifted, her whole body listening, and behind her eyes the city unfolded a second city: *the ring*.

She saw — felt — knew — the pilgrimage circuit that Ira had sketched in red ink across her graph paper in the cafeteria. It was below her, fifty, sixty feet beneath the mud of the lawns, drawn around the old city like a ribcage around a heart. Fifteen-odd kilometres of it, the five kos of the old walking-route, and each kos a slow bright artery of *warmth* — she had no word that fit what flowed there; 'energy' was too small and 'power' too vulgar, so her mind did what it had always done and found it in inventory: the camphor-ash of a million evening lamps ground into river-silt; names whispered into water at first light, every day, for longer than the university's ledgers could count; flowers laid at thresholds by hands now dust; fasts kept by mothers whose granddaughters' granddaughters still kept them; the rope-smoke of ten thousand boatmen's mornings; ghee, jasmine oil, eleven o'clock bhajans leaking from somebody's window radio — all of it cached, gathered, sunk into the soil and old stone where so many generations had said thank-you that the saying-of-it had become a thing, a *substance*, circulating slowly like light through amber.

The city's savings. The account that faith kept.

*The reservoir.*

*The reservoir.*

And at one point on the south-eastern curve — where the map's circle had darkened in Ira's redrawing — the arteries bent out of the earth's argument and hooked *down*.

The black came up the line to meet her thoughts like the answer to a whistle.

It was not a line. It was the absence made technical — a bore, a throat, a wound with walls, running from down at the ghat-wards bedrock *through* the ring's rich artery, braided into it, the way the tapeworm braids into an animal's guts: tissue-native to its host. It was sucking on the ring. It had cut wells into the reservoir and stood in them, lapping: slow, scheduled, cumulative — a decade's trickle, nine days' gulp. It was on a ninth night now of geometrically widening the tap, and she could feel — as she knelt barefoot and gasping on sixty years of leaf-mould with her hand stirring faintly green —

She could feel that it was nearly *full*.

Far down the bore — so far her senses refused to give it shape, as a mouth refused certain words — was a *capacity*, a vessel whose outline she could just touch with terror's antennae: some great cup or tank or stomach, being carefully, patiently poured toward brimming.

*The cup is being filled,* said Ira's voice in her head from the rooftop, thirty-six hours ago. *The question is — at what point do you stop filling a cup?*

Aadhya sobbed, once, aloud.

The banyan's branches clicked above her. The dead leaf swung.

"Bas," she said, to herself, to the bore in the earth, the way a woman talks herself over the ledge. "Bas. Bas. Enough."

She was not, it is essential to understand, a soft girl. She was the daughter of a clerk who had never missed a day's honesty; she had fought the state school for a lab-key, fought the college for her scholarship, three boys in her second year for the microscope, fate for a fever — but she had never in her life fought anything that filled up like this: level rising nightly, shoreless, without a throat to break. There was no argument with it, no answer required. There was just ordinary here: a girl kneeling on the earth with the ground *eating* under her knees.

Her eyes made the decision her mind was otherwise engaged to make. They filled. Her hand shut harder on the cold bark.

"Stop it," she said. Uselessly. Out loud. Her voice wet.

And — like a key trying the lock the soul keeps on every body's furnace-door — from the centre of her, that old heat, the one that had been a flicker on the balcony, a candle in the flood, a torch for a broken spine —

— came up like someone had stepped on its accelerator.

---

The green came out of her palms the way sunrise comes out of a horizon: not projection but arrival.

In the material dark, it was the brightest thing in Lanka road-side — both her hands from wrist to fingertip now fully incandescent, bottled lime lightning, veins going up under the skin of her forearms like floodlit channels in an aerial photograph, and under them, through her skin, the tide climbing her arms, shoulder-wards, into the sealed ring above the sternum where her heart must be. Somewhere far above the ground her mouth made a sound that was not a word.

Light flooded the bole of the tree. She could see the tree-grain through the bark as if the dead banyan had become translucent — veins gone to black lace, grey flesh, hollows; she saw, and the seeing hurt, the pattern the emptiness had been knit into, a crochet of hunger decades in the making, spliced up the trunk, fisted in its root-tips — and likewise the bole of the dying tree *opened* and drank from her, accepted, steeping, like a grandmother's face into warm hands.

The trunk warmed. The grey admitted colour reluctantly, at the bark-level, as coals admit breath.

At her hands' outline, a ring of radiance ran outward through the ground — moss igniting green, the humus steaming gently into the cold — and the ring, wide as two tall men lying head-to-toe, blinked the lawn alive in its passing: blades of winter-browned grass stood up shuddering, went soft and complete as if the frost had been un-rung from their cells. One aerial root — the dry curtain-strand her knee touched — lay under her light like a rope being mended strand by strand, grey pith slipping to live fibre, cork to heartwood, out from her.

Above them both the canopy came on softly all over, ten thousand dead leaves remembering concurrently the texture of the undersides of their youth, and for the duration of her breathing — four counts? forever? — they lay *green-fleshed against the starless cold*, trembling, and every crow asleep in the municipal limbs beyond the fence woke up screaming.

She looked up at her own hands and the aureoles of them had simplified to radiance.

The taste in the mouth then — that was the cost waking up and presenting itself before the miracle had even settled its bill: copper, no longer anticipated, *delivered*; something rattled loose behind her nose; a drop of noseblood fell onto her wrist and sang sizzling in the green light, and the blood sang brighter, like matter agreeing to become energy, and—

The bore down in the earth below the ghat *flinched*.

She felt it as plainly as a horse-suddenly-halted is felt between the knees: the careful, patient, decade-long drinking down below — *threw a hitch*. The bore had registered an unprecedented *source* standing over the ground of its flow. A mindless something — ancient, full, near-brimming — oriented itself toward her, blindly, the way a bull orientates toward cloth. The sucking at the arteries hesitated. The walls of the bore flexed with attention. And where the siphon's current paused along the arteries, it *rushed* harder up the one tap it had already cut — her tree's tap —

— *took her taste* —

Aadhya tore her palm off the bark like tearing it off a hot iron.

She was already standing, or she never was. The dark came slamming back; the leaf-ring trembled hopeless around her like a lamp's halo after the lamp has gone; and she reeled through the canopy-curtain, blood tipping twin ribbons out of her nose and mouth, heartbeat crashing at the cage of her teeth, vision already swerving-side-black-and-concave.

Out of the canopy's dying light, off the Sanskrit building's lawn, through the panel-gap in the bohar-root fence she ran — clipping someone's generator pipe with her shoulder, sprawling once on the dew — stung palms, grass-wet knees, up again, *run, think later* —

— the Campus sentry's glass house empty now, empty of him —

She started the scooter with one foot like a curse and went out of Sanskrit-building lane at a velocity the tyres had not been purchased for.

The shuttered little streets of Lanka slid by, hazy yellow, deaf. One old dog that knew her from her morning feeding rounds — one-eared, ash-freckled, held together by loyalty and mange — rose from the municipal debris-point at the corner of the last lane, saw her face, and whined, and came lurching alongside her at its ancient amble; she slowed the scooter to a roll and put her hand out, half a second before remembering, and halted it a hand-width above its scarred shoulders —

"Aaj nahi," she said, and her voice came apart. "Aaj — nahi. Ja. Apne raste—"

The dog — nineteen generations of Varanasi gutter-keepers bodied into one battered pensioner — sat back on its haunches and let her go.

She drove on alone between its eyes in mirrors and the sodium lights stretched her shadow longer than the street at every lamp, until the scooter's shadow resembled less a vehicle than a straight *line*, one street-width thin, dragged south over ground that, under fifty feet of compressed rock, had never forgotten the feel of a ring.

And the city was dimmer.

She did not trust the observation and could not unhave it. Every small night-shrine at the crossings — the tile-faced Hanuman with his steady brass lamp outside the ration shop; the Shiva niche with its single oil tongue on the wall by the Lanka turn — every bulb and flame she had ever known by heart went on doing everything correctly and yet came up short, seven watts short, one tessera of gold short, like photocopies of themselves. The ghee-dark window-shine of the old houses above the shuttered shops held on stubbornly and still gave the impression of embers being hastily read. Nothing was wrong anywhere. Everything was *less*.

The reservoir felt skimmed — even through the panic, with blood detonating quietly inside one nostril, the part of her that could not stop measuring things saw it and logged it: tonight's skimming was deeper than last night's skimming, and deeper by *more* than last night's had been deeper than the one before.

Whatever's cup was filling had widened the tap by two.

The bridge-sweeper was gone. The blind beggar at the temple steps was gone; but his dog — not *her* dog; a different dog altogether, spotted — was asleep in his rags with its head over the tin bowl as if holding the city together in his place, and it had not occurred to the dog to be watchful, and something in the sight — a small faithful thing sleeping on the lid of an empty collection-tin, blessed and empty — anyway, that was when Aadhya first began to cry in earnest, shoulder-shaking at sixty kilometres an hour through the last empty lane before home, no longer able to tell the sobs apart from the wind.

---

At home, the balcony chair was empty, the wool folded. The stairwell held her like an ear. Her mother had left the living room's small bulb on, and it lay over the cane furniture like an unasked question.

In the hall she passed her parents' bedroom and its open door, and there was her mother — still in the chair-side shawl, awake after all, sitting on the bed's edge in the near-dark with her hands folded, facing the door as if the door had been the thing she was waiting up for. Their eyes met for the length of a held breath.

"Zoo se phone aaya tha," her mother said at last — mild, unreadable, the voice of a woman who had decided years ago that the truth, when it wanted to arrive, would take its own route. "Priya ki mummy ka. Pooch rahi thi tum theek ho. Maine keh diya haan."

A pause, precisely long enough to hold everything neither of them said.

"Kal college hai na," her mother said. It was not a question and not an instruction. It was a rope thrown across nineteen years of gap between them, thin as cotton and load-bearing as love. "So jao. Bahut der ho gayi."

"Ammu—" Aadhya began.

"So jao," said her mother, and lay down with her back to the door, which in their house had always meant: *I have done what I can with tonight.*

She locked the bathroom and stood in the hum of the ceiling fan she had known since her first board exams, running the tap, working the copper discolouration off her skin with both hands until the water streamed clear.

Her face in the mirror: blood-diluted, whiter than rice-water; her breathing staggered, stretched.

Beneath the skin of her raised arms, the veins gleamed the faintest drowned-and-silver leaf-green and as she watched with horror — far away, quietly, hands spread flat on the basin's cold rim — the lamp-light in them guttered once, embered, and went out.

"Kya ho raha hai mujhe," Aadhya addressed the bathroom in the whisper of the filament still cooling below her sternum.

The bathroom, being Varanasi's, knew too much to answer.

On the knees of her jeans, pressed indelibly into the porous fabric, were threads of drying humus and, catching the lamp, two grains of living moss — still faintly green, small enough to hide, bright enough to accuse her.

She cupped water, put out the remaining shine as a mother smothers coal, and wept with her head against the mirror until her forehead pressed cold enough that the glass began faintly to fog.

---

Far out under the drainage-wreckage west of the old library and the bohar-roots and the fence, beneath eleven years of absent engineering and fifty feet of brown earth and black rock, at the end of the bore-narrowed tap-line the parasite-field's three eastern-node filaments lay closest to the skin of the trunk-base where she'd knelt.

For an interval of minutes after she had left, they did not pull; did not drink.

They stayed curled mid-syphon, filaments vibrating faintly, like spiders tasting the silent air left by departed prey.

Then, wrapping themselves slowly around the soil-print of one leaf-heat left before it, they *hauled*: the whole night-dark woven mass leaned one degree toward the banyan's lawn and the campus above it; and the ring of glow-bleached soil around the trunk where her hands had stood began — not visibly — to be read grain by grain.

Stone, root, leafmould; the bore had picked up her flavour like a cup by its handle.

It returned slow seconds after to its arteries. But it had now *two* scents within the city limits: a furnace under a seal, and a garden under a girl.

And down the full dark length of the bore — all the way down past the bedrock, past the seam of old iron-stained strata, past nine days' deepening harvest of the city's slow-warmed prayers — a vessel that could not properly grow edges stirred its walls by one millimetre, listening, and upon the tenth night geometrically began again, two taps wider than before.

---

# CHAPTER 7 — THE LULLABY

Rukmini spent the whole of that day rebuilding what could not be seen.

This was her oldest labor and her most secret one, older than the marriage-photograph over the kitchen shelf, older than the wobbly-legged table, almost as old as her son. Nineteen years of mornings: before the dal, before the stove's first hiss, she stood at the kitchen window with her eyes shut and her thumbs pressed together and rebuilt the knots. Forty positions. Counted twice. The work took six minutes and was as invisible from the outside as a woman listening to a pressure-cooker; Ram had watched her do it, on and off, for nineteen years, and had never once asked, because in this house some threads were not pulled by mutual consent, and because he had his own versions of the same prayer.

But today the knots had resisted. Twice her count had slipped — not her count; her count was perfect, had been perfect for nineteen years; rather, the *knotting itself* had snagged, mid-position, like thread that's gone over the same stretch of cloth too many times. Nineteen years of stitch on the same seam. She had redone position twenty-eight four times and felt, in the snagging, a thinness that had not been there when he was ten, when he was fifteen, a gloss and give like rain-worn steps in the temple ghats.

At the stove, stirring the evening dal, she caught herself humming.

She stopped instantly — hand to her own mouth, ladle raised, the way you'd snuff a candle with two fingers. The melody had escaped. She hadn't hum it — not consciously, not on purpose, not since the first year. It had climbed out of her without permission, the way grief climbs out of old women at weddings.

It was the lullaby. The one from before. The one that had no Hindi words because the grammar it was built in wasn't Hindi's grammar, or Sanskrit's, or anything any wandering scholar would be able to write down in Devanagari. Eleven intervals, no taal she knew would hold them; when she was young she had tried to find them on a harmonium at a neighbour's and failed — the keys simply didn't go that way.

She stood quite still with the ladle going cold, listening for the house. Nothing. Ram's footsteps upstairs in the storeroom, inventorying anchors. The gate shut. The street outside ordinary.

*Don't,* she told her mouth. *It's a door. Open it a crack, and things notice.*

And she put it away — folded it down somewhere beneath the sternum where all the unsingable things lived — and ground the evening's garlic, and listened for the gate's iron sentence, and something vast below the floorboards — not a floorboard away, not a city away, but *under* — turned its hearing approximately toward the scent of her tune, briefly, like an old dog lifting one ear.

The flame on the second burner leaned once, sideways, in no wind at all.

She looked at it.

It straightened.

"Nahin," Rukmini said quietly to the burner, in the voice a mother uses to tell a child *not today*. "Abhi nahin."

---

The afternoon had been its own small campaign of avoidance.

Rudra had lasted exactly forty minutes of the thermodynamics lecture before the chalk's sssss-ing against the board began to arrange itself into the cadence of a count — *in, four; hold, four* — and he got up and left with the universal two-finger-to-chest mime of campus gastric distress, which the lecturer acknowledged with the universal nod of a man paid the same either way.

Veer caught him by the water-cooler. Caught is not the right word; Veer was simply *there*, folded small on the cooler's concrete plinth like a temple guardian trying to sit at a desk, waiting with two steel tumblers of Nimbu-pani, one of which he extended without looking up.

"Pee le."

"Vitamins? Iss umr mein?"

"Pee le, gadhe." Veer took a long swallow from his own. His forearm dressing had been changed; the gauze was hospital-white now, professional, no longer the torn kurta-strip of Manikarnika night. "Kal raat ke baad aaj sirf teen baar maara hai kisi ne mujhe mazaak mein 'hero'. Teen. Itni publicity ke baad college apna dharm bhool raha hai." He watched a first-year spill water, did not help, did not laugh, continued: "Ira ka matlab hai — jo usne kaha tha — weeks. Tu usko lekar kya kar raha hai?"

"Push-ups."

"*Rudra.*"

"Ira ka math aur mere seene ka hisaab," Rudra said, dead-level, "dono apni jagah perfect hain. Par dono ko yeh nahin pata ki jab jo Cheez andar hai woh kholne wali hoti hai, toh jo uske saath rakhta hai woh bhi khulta hai." He drained the tumbler. Set it down. "Mujhe us din ka aadmi nahi banana jis din sab khaata hai. Baaki sab normal rakhna hai. Samjha?"

Veer looked at him for a long count whose length Rudra measured without meaning to — *in, four; hold, four* — then clapped him once, softly, high on the back, the spot two men use in lieu of the conversation, and said, "Chal. Warden ko bola tha teri kharab tabiyat ka invent karta hoon — attendance bacha dega, drama karwa dega." And he went, shoulders filling the corridor frame, and if Rudra noticed that his oldest friend had not once met his eyes during the whole exchange — had looked at the cooler, the floor, the future — he filed it, as he filed everything unbearable, precisely, and away.

Near the library turn, three corridors later, he saw Aadhya.

She was coming out of the periodicals room with a stack of last-year bound journals held against her diaphragm — academic armour — moving pale and straight through the light-shaft, and there was, unmistakable even at thirty feet, the shadow of bruising at the edge of one nostril, faint as thumb-smudge. She took in him; he took in her. Data was exchanged in some half-second protocols of recognition: *you too; yes, me too; say nothing; okay.*

What walked toward him instead of Aadhya was the small crop of her face turning aside first, in a direction that would pass him two strides away.

"Aadhya—" he said.

"Not today," she said, not harshly — gently even, with the tired courtesy of someone detoxing an afternoon — and went past him smelling faintly, unbelievably, like wet humus and camphor, and put down her head and disappeared into the reading-room door's dim geometry, and he stood in the empty knowing and felt, absurdly, that he had been refused entry into a shrine whose sanctum he's spent three days desecrating.

On the Sanskrit wing side, as he cut through for the gate, the banyan's ruin stood behind two new bamboo-poles strung fatally with red cloth — the municipal world's entire arsenal against the inexplicable: a string barrier and a faded notice, *PRUNING SCHEDULED — DO NOT APPROACH — BY ORDER, GARDENS DEPT.* The crows were gone from it entirely. The grey had climbed to its crown's underside like banked water.

Rudra walked the last stretch home under evening skies gone the colour of wet dhoop-ash, and counted — in, four; hold, four — all the way to his gate.

---

Dinner that night tasted of ordinary things and of three silences that didn't touch.

Rudra came in late — near dark, knuckles rewound in their boiled cloth, jaw-bruise yellowing at its edges like old mango. He dropped onto the dining bench with the theatrical collapse of a nineteenth-year-old trying to impersonate exhaustion and landing, without wanting to, on the real thing. His mother set the thali down. His father sat at the other end with the old FM transistor mumbling devotional turns from the corner high shelf — *vaghera, vaghera, gyarah zakhmi, jaanch jaari* — the eight-o'clock voice pressed down flat beneath the bhajan reprise like a ballast stone under a rug.

"Ghat gaya tha kal?" Ram asked, not looking up from his roti.

Rudra reached for the dal. "Veer ke saath the," he said, and the sentence was so perfectly rehearsed in its timing — bite first, answer second, eyes to brass-plate, casual, exact — that it might have been a drill his father had taught him: *lie as stance, part four.*

Ram said nothing, and broke his roti.

The transistor said *—afwah mat failaiye—* and went back to the bhajan, and Rudra's mother set the katori down and watched her family not talk, her hands folded on her thighs, knuckles unbleached for once, ten quiet joints that had held a universe together at dawn and were, for these forty minutes, allowed to be a housewife.

"Ammu says the corridor light is flickering," Rudra said, after a while, to the silence. "Said to tell Appa."

"Mm," said Ram.

"That's the entire message," Rudra said. "Whole thing. Very informative. Zero lightbulbs issued." He chewed. The bruise made the chewing uneven. Forty seconds passed with the bhajan. "Can I have a lightbulb? The corridor at night looks like—" he hunted for the reference and settled, tiredly, on the truth, "—like a corridor at night, Ma. It flickers and I have to watch it flicker. It's a hazard."

"Ungliyan," Ram said.

"What?"

"Tumhari ungliyan," said Ram, still not looking up. "Split. Dobara. Kal subah re-wrap." And he folded down a cartridge of dal and continued eating, as if the entire ghat-shaped thing the son hadn't said had been fully, courteously acknowledged and filed.

Rudra stared at the top of his father's head and felt, not for the first time, the dreadful inverted vertigo of being this man's son: that every word between them had its whole underwater continent, and they swam on the surface talking about corridor lights.

"Achha," he said, and let it go. Too tired to push tonight. The seal-heat was a fine low banked coal under everything, tonight, patient and warm, as if it had decided to observe a rest day — *waiting*, some traitorous lower floor of his mind observed, *like a client.*

Rukmini watched him finish only half the dal and then the roti — and he had always finished all the dal, had done so since the age of nine, the boy was *famous* in three families for it — and her hand went out to his forehead before either of them expected it, flat and cool, past necessity, counting.

"Bukhaar nahin hai," she announced — theater, for the room.

"Ma—"

"Sar mein dard?"

"Nahin, Ma, I'm—" He leaned out of the flat of her hand, gently, the way you'd leave a temple. "Tired hai. College."

She didn't answer that. At the doorway she turned — silhouette with the corridor's flickering light behind her stripping her to edges.

"So ja jaldi," she said. "Main baad mein aati hoon."

He didn't ask what that meant. Some part of him had already begun to drift, the body's dumb untrainable wisdom lowering him toward the floor of the day.

He was on the edge of it — the first black terrace of sleep — when he registered, distantly, with the back of his attention, the small domestic cricket-noise of a chair being dragged in the corridor below his room, the charpai-creak of his father's weight going up it, the old dust-ritic bulb unscrewed, the new one — from where, in this house, at this hour? from some drawer of foresight his father kept stocked like spare spearheads — going home into the socket with a slow thread-whisper.

The corridor light came on steady.

Steady. First time in a month, his mother had told the neighbours, two Sundays ago, over their wall. Rudra lay in the blue dark with his eyes half-open, the gauze of his wound never tighter than now, the coal breathing its four-count breath, and the clean yellow line of that stable light under his door was, in a way no one in this family could have explained on paper, the first unbroken sentence anybody had said to him all day.

*Father speaks only with his hands.* It occurred to him, going under: *the trembling means something is wrong with the light inside him; so tonight he fixed the bulb.*

Half a dream rose to meet that thought and carried it away — the way the lane-sweeper carried before dawn what the city had meant to learn by heart — and the note of somebody's naval song, far off and eleven-noted and not Hindi, folded the whole boy's nineteenth year gently around its key.

---

By ten the haveli was dark except the kitchen, where a single diya on the shelf below the wedding photograph made the brass shine like a row of patient moons.

Rukmini heated the milk slowly, because haste in milk-making is a crime against tomorrow's tea, and because her hands needed a liturgy that belonged to nobody's history but this household's: saucepan, cardamom, the brief agitation as it threatened to rise. She was aware — more aware than she was comfortable ever being aware — of the melody pressing against the underside of her attention like fingers under a door.

*Don't.*

*He's nineteen. Sleeping badly hasn't happened since—*

—since never. Rudra did not sleep badly. That was the cruel joke of the entire machine Ram had built of his son: the body had been honed so fine that recovery was automatic, that sleep came down on him like a curtain at the same nightly minute, that his father had commissioned every night of boyhood against nightmare. And here he was: two nights in three, scraped awake at three, four in the morning, the coal under his chest lit to a low blue-white point —

— and here it was, pulsing at her attention like a knock from the basement, a rhythm she knew better than *Om*: the seal was doing its nightly thing. Expanding a knot, letting the brief tide through, resting again. Holding. Diminutively fraying at its edges, yes — but holding. She'd been repairing its edges at dawn the way street-sweepers repair the city: invisibly, assumed-permanently, no one watching, garbage of the gods quietly carted off before the saints wake up —

*Don't,* she thought again — and found, in her own mind, no further argument the boy's face hadn't already rebutted.

She let the first two notes out over the saucepan.

The milk shivered.

---

Upstairs, in the dark, Rudra lay on his back watching the ceiling-fan describe responsibilities he had never agreed to.

The lane gate had settled; the fm was off; Appa was on the roof — always, lately — doing whatever thing he did there with the northeast sky. Somewhere in the wall a gecko clocked. The coal behind his sternum pulsed in four-count intervals. *In, four. Hold, four. Out, four. Hold, four.* His body had begun running the counts without him again, like an auto running on the meter of a passenger who'd fallen asleep against the window.

Ma climbed the stairs with the milk — he heard the saucepan's small musical clink on the steel tumbler lid. The corridor light over his doorway flicked on one dying instant, blossomed, and quit. Then she was in the room, not saying anything, putting the tumbler on his table, pushing aside the calc-notes with the side of her hand and her free hand found his temple. The contact sank through him like a first rain into last year's soil.

"Peelo."

"Ma, I'm not six."

"Peelo." She smiled at his chin. "Drink it and shut the eyes. Aaj bahut lamba din tha."

He drank it. She sat on the bed's edge. The corridor-flicker apart from them now, the room was blue and still; even the fan had decelerated into sympathy. Tomorrow-and-tomorrow college; his knuckles to re-wrap in the dawn; father's questions on the dining table wearing wifi-password faces; Ira's estimates inside his skull: *weeks, possibly less*—

Something warm flowed through his hair. For the first several seconds he mistook it for his mother's hand moving there. It was not her hand. She was sitting entirely still.

She was humming.

It was so faint he caught only the bottom of it — felt it more than heard it, her chest making a second slower reservoir beside him that the hum rose from in thin dark threads. The melody — he surfaced, blinked once in the dark, listened properly —

— he knew music. Everyone in this city knew music; the ghats were flooded daily in it; morning-singers with tanpuras sat on temple steps tuning their throats into the river-noise; old men with transistor-stutters held whole worlds together with an alap. He knew music, was telling himself two bars in — and he didn't know *this*. The intervals rose and fell in widening and narrowing steps that slid off the grid of any sargam. It wasn't a raga. It wasn't any filmi thing his mother's kitchen whistled during festival cooking — bhajan, bhojpuri, monsoon-oldies. It didn't resolve — and he had been raised since one on the premise of resolution, breath in and breath out; *the interval was not trying to resolve*. It opened. It described something further out than the room. Further out than the rooftops, the streetlamp haze, the ghats' perfumed smoke. The melody travelled an eleven-note arc, turned over in a *seven*-count breath — seven pulses, seven pulses, a cadence that didn't jog so much as *walk*, patiently, through somewhere —

He stopped being able to think in sentences. A part of his attention, far below, was walking the inside perimeter of his chest and smoothing something — the coal. *It was singing the coal down.* The ember under his sternum — the low hum that had slept there at varying volumes since some unremembered year — was, for the first time since the ghat, *settling*, banking down, coiling like a dog allowed at last to sleep after three days standing.

The melody kept going, and each place it went, an image opened in his dimming mind like a hand unfolding.

The third interval: a courtyard much larger than theirs, with no walls, only mist for boundaries, and a woman — not his mother — standing very far away in it with something held against her chest that was too bright to see. The seventh pulse: water, black and endless, going under a stone; a sense, without drama, of someone whispering the way you whisper into a sleeping child's ear: *remember only this, only this.* The eleventh — the door-note, the one that opened — sunrise over some plain littered with small cool cooking-fires no bigger than listenings, smoke going up straight, *unbent*; and behind the smoke, the horizon doing something skies should not be able to do, being *more than one horizon at once*, a fan of silver dawns laid over each other —

He thought, crossing down through the last fathoms before sleep: *someone once stood in a place like that and sang like this and promised to come back.*

He could not have said who he meant. The thought was in him and not of him, like the working of his own heart.

Somewhere in the deep of him, rudderless nonsense rose, wearing his own voice but not his aliveness.

"Ye kya ga rahi ho, Ma?"

A long moment, in which the melody went on and gentler, and his mother's hand resumed as if nothing had happened, padding his hair in small slow tides.

"Old thing," she said eventually — soft, casual, a lie performed by a genius with the single weakness of lying only about this. "Purani si cheez. Mere ghar mein sabko aati thi."

"Words kyun nahin hain?"

"Words honge," Rukmini said. A bigger wave of pressure passed through his temple and left less behind. "Lekin is = not for your ears. Not yet."

*Not yet.* The two soft words towered enormously over the rest of the sentence, a gate too large for its lane. He wanted to ask. Instead what came out — and he could feel the melody coaxing it up out of him with a midwife's patience, and felt mildly outraged about it from a great distance —

"Ma."

"Mm?"

"Galat kya hai? Mere saath." Slurring now at the edges — a boy in the dark of his own bed with his mother's lap-warmth levelling the day into a flat place he could finally lie down on. "Appa behaves like a countdown, aur tum aise dekh rahi ho jaise kuch hold kar rahi ho. Kya chhupa rahe ho. Kal ghat pe—"

The melody faltered. *Once*. One note sat down hard, half a shruti off its path, before she lifted it gently back onto the quiet rails.

That stumble said everything his question had asked for.

He understood — still drifting, fingertips of sleep already up his wrists — that he had not made it easier by asking. He had made it visible. He lay feeling the melody resume stitch by stitch over his believing-heart, felt the dark warm pool of it widening at his elbows, and heard his mother lower her head very close to his — lips almost to the ear, breath moving the hair that the bluntness of his kurta collar made wiry — and say, in a voice that had forgotten all theatre, that was the smallest true voice he had ever heard her use, so low that even the walls, lifelong sworn to her, missed half of it:

"There are people who look for children like you, beta."

He blinked into the dark.

"Promise me," she said, and the melody held one long, open, door-like note under the sentence, "you'll never let strangers see what you can do."

Rudra stared at the ceiling fan. For a moment the fan and its pundit's blurred wheel spun above him in the dark of the room like a question-mark putting itself out.

"Ma," he said gently, in the tone with which you remind a parent the world is ordinary. "Main kya kar sakta hoon?"

The very limits of his nineteen years were in the sentence — the whole unconscious, grand, pathetic proposition of his life: *what can I do; I can do push-ups. I can hold stance until breakfast. I can win illegal lane-fights and get see-eight-weeks of "training." What can I do? I'm nobody's whatever. I'm the guy who loses balance at the third step.*

And Rukmini — nineteen years of holding knots inside her own two hands, eyes closed at dawn, subjecting creation's most volatile heirloom to a mason's patience — looked down at her son with an expression that would have broken the heart of any deity who had ever asked her to be braver than this, and drew her thumb once across the boy's forehead, warmly, and said:

"Sab kuch, beta. You can do anything."

And then, stilled and sustained by the last note, she finished singing him under.

---

She remained with her palm on the warm brow past sleep. She remained listening, temple-trained, to the deep inner tide of him, confirming knot by knot — *the seal had taken tonight's reinforcement beautifully, intake down to safe occlusions:* eleven closed, eight thinning, three translucent. Seconds of gratitude from a mason-mother. One thread let itself be eased, another was purpose-set for the dawn work—

At the bedroom door a hand knocked, quietly, once. Ram's silhouette was there as everywhere these nights: fully dressed, on his way up to the roof, pausing at the edge of his son's darkness like a pilgrim at a ghat who cannot decide whether he is allowed to touch the river.

Rukmini looked at the silhouette. The silhouette looked at the bed. From within the dark of her son's room Rukmini's expression was not readable — except that her hand had stilled.

"Gaa rahi thi," said Ram, finally. It was not a question. It carried the certainty of a man who has been *listening to the house* for twenty years and knows the exact second its gravity changes. He could not have heard a thing; the hum had been threaded so fine. And still he knew.

"Gaana bandh kiya," said Rukmini. Her voice was as even and as soft as the first thirty years of evenings. "So gaya."

Ram said nothing for a while. The moon washed a thin milk of light into the corridor and he was, for one moment, only a young man standing uncertainly at a woman's door. 

"Ram."

"Mm."

"Bahut kam samay bacha hai."

For a long moment the man who had never left the house without locking every lock twice simply stood there — big, tightened, watchful, beginning to tremble in the deeper furniture of his body where no trembling showed, the coal-fed furnace of an unbroken oath glowing dully through his ribs. Then he stepped backward out of the moonlight and the silhouette dissolved, and the rooftop took him like a loaded bowstring accepts its arrow-back.

Downstairs, on the counter, the kitchen-diya's flame leaned sideways for one breath — leaned, as if listening — and righted itself.

---

And Rudra did not dream, for the first time in three nights.

The training-ground fog of his sleep unfolded, and there were no broken stones in it; the courtyard was enormous, rose-lit, the neem-roots holding the whole house like a lap, and in the middle of it — far away, small as a child's hand — a circle of seven burning lamps stood around an eighth unlit one, and the eighth one filled with light slowly, like a cup.

In the dream someone was singing. Eleven intervals. The missing word of the melody, rising now just out of hearing — a word his sleeping body knew so intimately that the mystery of it did not trouble him; it had, after all, been pressed on him with both hands when he was three days old.

He slept on. Below the sill, the street's one streetlamp burned; the lane-dogs' night-check passed; the next door's lichened wall shone faint; and on the facing roof a small pink-faced langur crouched with its child and watched the house as monkeys watch a fruiting tree.

Far below, far below the pucca whiteness of Lanka, the bore in the dark took one second of unlatched attention and sniffed, up at the rim of everything, along the underside of the singing — and then lost the thread of it, exactly at the point where the seventh breath paused — and went on dimming the town.

---

# CHAPTER 8 — THE DREAM MAP

Ira's room rented itself by the facts.

Top floor of an old Assi house, landed on by a staircase external and iron, so that the landlady down in the courtyard — a widow in her seventies whose name was Mausi and whose hearing had sharpened with age like a debt-collector's eye — never once had to interact with her tenant's hours. The room was narrow, high-ceilinged, whitewashed to the vertical plane where cheap paint met religious patience. Iron bed. Two bookshelves improvised from mango-wood crates set on their ends. A chipped table, sixty years with the family downstairs, now rented out along with the room at no extra charge if polish was maintained.

Across one wall the map hung.

She had drawn it six times in three weeks and pinned all six versions up in sequence, spidery red-spoked webs of the pilgrimage circuit inked over candle-lit evenings by hand. Beneath, on a long nail, the schoolbag. On the opposite wall's hook, her grey windcheater — ignored — above a solitary ink-marked cross on the paint where she had adjusted a nail to hold nothing.

The evening's instruments stood on the table.

A brass bowl — water, precisely to a pencil-line on its inner rim she had scratched five days ago at 6:47 PM with a geometry compass. Beside it: an old mariner's compass in a paper sleeve, bought from a scrap-dealer near the godown for fifty rupees, oiled and re-magnetized with careful flicks of a speaker-magnet; its needle wandered nightly in a wake-route across true north like a drunk crossing a maidan. Beside that: three incense stick-ends weighted upright into grains-of-rice pyramids on glazed tile, their cones of ash cooling, measured — collapse-angle photographed hourly with her phone and logged into a notebook whose spine was wearing through.

Her body did not always need the instruments. That was precisely why she *had* the instruments: as corroboration. It was one thing to know things. It was another to know that the depths of one's own knowing were — as the orphanage matrons had worried — *entirely sound.*

Data first. Always data first. She had a hatred of unverified knowing deep enough to dissolve certainties.

The six pinned maps above the bed were the autobiography of three weeks' surveillance. Version one — pencil, tentative, the ring sketched as a circle like a schoolchild's sun. Version two — the ring corrected, misshapen where the old route bent around industrial colonies that hadn't existed when the circuit was young. Version three — the nodes first circled, after she'd noticed that her five fixed compass stations never wandered on the same night, except at the same hour. Version four — the intervals. Version five — the red redraw, when the lines went to India-ink and her landlady had asked over the courtyard wall whether she was "city-planning ka course" and she had said yes, and the widow had nodded in a meadow of total misunderstanding and given her two guavas.

Version six was three days old. It was the first one she had been afraid of.

It hung at eye height. Red thread connected its seven circled knots to seven index cards along the wall's bottom rail; each card logged crossings — date, hour, compass-drift, waterline-tremor, the note of what she'd felt against her two palms when it happened — and beneath each, darker, the card for what she'd *dreamt* the week before it occurred. The correlation column was full.

Twenty-one years, and she still put the correlation cards face-down every evening, like dishes put away.

Tonight the bowl held its line but its surface — she trained her phone-torch close, chin hovering — flickered with a fine ring-system seated at its centre like dust on a crow's eye. The compass had wandered approximately nine degrees east-of-north and stopped with its needle quivering as if at attention. Two of the incense cones had collapsed inward — into the pyramids — rather than out and off onto the tile, as always before.

She put all of it into the notebook in handwriting like pressed tin.

*Bowl: ring-groove interference, 8 sec interval. Compass: +9° E, held. Cones: imploded inward. Same signature as the 6th and the 7th. The system is warming. The ring runs 1.3% dimmer per twelve hours, cumulative. At this slope the pull at Manikarnika reaches saturation in—*

She looked at the result before she wrote it, then wrote it with the pen nib walking very precisely through the ink-territory already damaged by her much-larger handwriting, as if smaller letters might slow it down:

*thirteen to nineteen days.*

She thought of Rudra's wrapped knuckles. Of the chest-center he had clutched at the pyre ghat three nights ago, his face suddenly so very un-made-up: it has a seal, she'd said; it's his mother's; it's failing. *Even if he received an intervention,* the math said, *the thing coming up arrives first.*

She opened the windcheater to a night's cold that hadn't existed at nine. Closed it again. Switch off the torch. Lie down, Ira. Sleep. Numbers in the dark held in their banks of seven and collapsed.

---

She had the dream every or every-other night since the age of four.

At the home in Durgakund she used to climb out of the low cot after it and walk the paint-flaked deterrent corridors with her drawing-book and one blunt grey pencil, gently persuaded by the wardens to narrate her nightmare pictures like placid miniature evidence. *Corridors,* she'd point, aged six, with the pencil. *Dark. No roof. Here a chair. Here this man, with a sword. He says a thing.* The wardens — sisters of a minor forgotten order, all dead now or married off — grew worried over the years, tried the doctor in Sigra. The doctor in Sigra prescribed vitamin tablets and warm milk with some badaam and the diagnosis of an imaginative only-child's separation anxieties.

There had been an incident, once, that only she remembered. She was eight. The warden's sister-in-charge had asked her, kind-eyed, to draw the chair again — *accha, woh almari-si kursi, dikhao* — and she'd taken the black crayon with both hands, ready, fluent; and somewhere between the seat-line and the arm-line the crayon had begun to lay down the mineral-veins of the chamber — *her* veins, the light-threads, the only part of the dream she would permit herself to love — and when the sister returned with the register, the page was blue-white with branching light and the crayon had stopped, and Ira was staring at it with her mouth open as if watching the page drink her.

"Baaki sab safed kyun chhoda?" the sister had asked.

"White roshni hai," Ira had said. "Saara ka saara. Wahan sabse zyada roshni hai."

She'd been made to draw a park after that. The chair was never requested again. The register, she suspected to this year, recorded it under the column headed with the single useful word: *pretends*.

She had outgrown nothing. She had simply learned to say the word *dream* like a smuggler's stamp on freight — a disclaimer, a harmless label, so that listeners would relax and look away. Her waking life had become her own meticulous investigation into the dream's crime-scene. The instruments. The compass, the cones, the bowl: evidence, gathered nightly, to settle a twenty-year unpaid debt of certainty. Because she had never, since infancy, been taken at her word about any of it. Because somewhere underneath her disciplines there was still a four-year-old Ira knocking politely on the inside of a locked matron's door, with a drawing in her hand.

She read three pages of a translated monograph on Benares's ritual micro-geography — third-hand, spine perished, somebody's marginal underlining in dire class-V pencil — turned one page habitually to the back of the next without interest, discerned loneliness was the true medium of all empty acts of study, and laid the book down.

Light off. Sleep took her at 12:48 by the brass bowl's ticking bands.

---

She entered the corridor this time from *inside* its blindfold.

The transition was not a transition; it was occupancy. In the half-syllable after dreamless sleep, her dreaming self stood in the black stone passage with the draft moving through it, and her dreaming eyes were — *not her eyes.* She knew, clear as drowned bells, that she stood inside the skull of the kneeling one — looking out through *his* dark — while at the same time her witness-body hung above them all like dust.

Three escorts. Footfalls — *heel-toe, heel-toe, drag, heel-toe* — the patterns synthetic in their repeated placement, rehearsed down the hours — and she keenly, with the special anguish of the dreaming archivist, strained to look up and see their faces and documented the failure: no faces. Smeared. The dream's gesso had clouded their three visages like spots on old film.

*It doesn't want me to see their faces. Something, in here with me, knows I collect them.*

The chamber bloomed. Cathedral of black stone, seams of mineral blazing cold luminous like veins of frozen lightning lighting the walls from within. The pillars rose to a ceiling lost in pressured white dark — its air nautical, dense, bearing down past her collarbones like standing under a well. A throne — one cold piece of obsidian, no cushion, no arm-rest carved; *the least comfortable seat she had ever seen, and then a stranger recognition laying itself gently over her skin: a seat designed by someone who hated the thought of sitting easily.*

On it, the gaunt man. Ashen robes drinking the light. Sword across the knee — blade habitual, companion-lodged, its handle so much a growth of his hand that the fingers didn't so much grip as *surround* it.

Spoken without malice:

*"I hope you performed better."*
