And then the eyes of the kneeling skull she stood inside lit red-gold, and the light went out around her like dawn pulsing through a slit in a blind.

The man on the throne leaned forward.

And the dream's pressure surged — something mounted in the room that was *mostly* not in the room — and she became aware, with the coldness of a compass needle flexed over by iron nine degrees, that the chamber was being *filled* — that an immense decanted dark was pouring into the air of this place at a rate equivalent to — the metric lived directly in her palm like a wet weight:

— 1.3 per cent per twelve hours —

— of an invisible reservoir's contents.

"Cup," whispered her witness-mouth in the dust above everything.

Far away — *down*, it was *down*, the whole thing was beneath the world and this was why her compass had spent its whole love harrowing east — inside the hollow bowl-side beyond the corridor-terminus, a black intake-mouth rhythmically spasmed, swallowing slim glowing flotsam.

The corpse-glow of Varanasi — its lamps', fakirs' tapers, noon-votive clay, its weeknight brazier-lights, the red smoulder of a husband taking a last beedi on his dear balcony — all of them, drained to grey smoke-streams, combed like sand-streams into the pit's noiseless throat — passing first through seven smaller distribution channels around a ring, seven small drinking-mouths set in the earth's rim like cisterns on a ghat, arranged like sweets around a plate — each marked with a sign — *and her sleep-standing seeing body felt them now and then she knew them: seven nodes, one central cup, the cup at Manikarnika, and a BORE that carried the sucked prayer down past rock's skin into nowhere.*

And the voice again — not the gaunt man's — a wider one, voiceless, arriving as her own held breath coming loose:

— *two taps wider* —

and she woke with her palm already on the notebook, in the dark, writing.

---

She didn't remember sitting up. She didn't remember switching anything on. The load-shedding had taken the neighbourhood at 3:04 — some drowsed corner of her sleeping mind had registered the fan's slowing whine — so it was the phone torch, gripped between her teeth, that lit the room: she cross-legged on the bed's edge, the notebook pressed flat against her bent knees, dried blood smeared under one nostril to the lip, her temple-veins leaping — and her pencil tracing, as fast as recognition could guide it, lines she already knew should not be producible at this hour, by these eyes, from this mind:

— seven nodes circled tight around a centre stroke — ring crawls north around the old city, comes back down the far side, closes — every node labeled with numbers because she didn't *have* their names yet, just designations she'd given them years ago: N-1, N-2, N-3 —

— and then down. *Below* the map. Not metaphorically but spatially: stem reaching past the page's lower border into the notebook's gutter, squeezing into the paper-loss margins with the pencil-tip pressing hard enough to dent under-sheets. Four descending levels. The horizontal ruled lines she'd never noticed represented levels, calibrated. Then the cup, the mouth, the pit —

— and at the bottom edge, irrational, her pencil drew the one thing it was wrong of her to have seen: the gaunt *seat* crosshatched into the crust, and from its seat-base two lines, like antennae, entering the ring and passing into the bore.

Her pencil stopped. Her heart in her mouth.

The phone-torch light whitened the sternum-line of her grey night kurta to a hard horizon. Under it all her chest still counted off the ghost rhythm of the dream-pressure: cup, cup, cup.

She got up; floor cold; and found the old top-loose leaf-map — version number five, she knew without flicking, she had all six on one nail — laid her dream-sheet over it, torch pressed side-light, held her breath—

— every line coincided.

Every one. The red-inked research-and-pendulum map of the last three weeks; the walking route; the ancient five-kos pilgrimage circumference maintained for centuries by *barefoot faith*: the dream merely laid onto it a seventh node she had consistently missed (east of the university, faint), the descending stem — and the truth her waking methods had banished as an unmeasurand:

*not just below. Below the below.*

Her fingertip rested on the seventh node — east of the campus — which under the pencil was showing a faint crosshatch infestation she'd ink-downloaded from the dream, a braid-thickening, a *diverticulum* — and beside it her dreaming hand had scrawled three numerals that her waking mind could now read:

**1 . 3**

She knew this weight: bowl, compass, cones. The ring runs 1.3% dimmer per twelve hours. The dream had found exactly one route for measuring what her table had measured for days.

Collapse: she sat down hard on the bed; the notebook shut against her sternum on a human tremor; breath nasal with iron.

*What visits me nightly is not the memory of a place. It is a place. And it eats the city.*

She held still until the room stopped going in and out like water. Then — because she was herself — she turned to the next clean page and wrote her work-notes about a thing for which no field stood printed in any ledger:

*1. The colour around the kneeling figure at the — I have no name. Red-gold. Coal-in-draught. I have seen that exact thread-colour once already this week: under a collarbone, on the rooftop, in the pyre-light.*
*The pencil stopped long enough for her chest to become entirely aware of itself.*
*2. I saw it in a dream. I am seeing it outside dreams. The two records are the same record.*
*3. Therefore: either what lives ahead of us visits sleep, or what sleeps under us walks ahead.*

She underlined nothing. She never underlined; emphasis was for people with someone to convince.

*4. Blindfold*, she wrote. *They had gone to the length of a blindfold. It is possible to be on both sides of a blindfold and not know it.*

She turned the page and, breathing through her mouth, made the verification table — because even here, especially here, the discipline was the door through and not the wall:

*Slope from instruments: 1.3% / twelve hours.*
*Slope from dream-pressure: "cups," unitless, on the same interval — call it equivalent.*
*Convergence of independent methods: match.*
*Forecast, ring-side: eleven days.*

Eleven. The number sat in the looking of it with the particular stillness of cobras. In eleven days the ring under the city reached zero; in eleven days the word "shift" would have done its last gentle work and whatever came after would no longer admit the vocabulary of *shifts*; and the seal over her friend's chest — N-1, *the furnace with the heartbeat* — was, by her last rooftop arithmetic, five to six weeks from failure, and even with every adjustment she could make for a mother's repairs, which her arithmetic had learned to fear as a variable —

Eleven was sooner.

Eleven was *much* sooner.

*The math is saying: the plugs give out in two months. And the cup fills in eleven days.*

She wrote it without shaking. She had, ever since the warden at Durgakund had taught her with a slate, believed that writing a number was the one way not to be entirely at its mercy — that a figure on the page was the figure removed from the chest. "Measurements are exorcisms," the old woman had said, drunk on religious scrabble, over crows. "Daain bhagane ke liye nahi, beti: pehchaanne ke liye."

The lines wobbled, then found iron.

And then she did write the last line, in a hand she barely recognized as hers, and closed the notebook over it as if the page were a window opened on winter:

*5. Do not tell them until verified.*

She read the fifth line back and felt the strange vertigo the sentence deserved: that she had written *them* — three specific faces, freckled with knuckle-wraps and chai — three years' friends — and that by *verified* she no longer meant the data. She meant verifying what happened to three human beings the morning the bottom of the world raised one lion's head and read its surroundings.

*The blindfold*, she re-read. *At the end of a corridor. Under the earth. Among my night-things.*

She put her forehead down onto the open page until the graphite's greasy shine cooled the skin. "Verified," she told the empty room, in the same voice her childhood had used to say *dream*, "matlab cheezein aur buri hon."

And laughed once, alone — data-keeper's solitary bark — and the sound held neither humour nor despair, which is the specific laughter of archivists.

---

She knew that she ought to wake someone — that protocols of any sane community science would be: diagram + anomaly + prediction → inform. She knew whose numbers she'd written on it: N-3 was the banyan by the Sanskrit wing; death-cries of a flock of crows at midnight from that side alone, a newest braiding where two nights' earlier notes showed thin webbing — *Aadhya had been there tonight, somewhere in it, hours ago; if the girl's leaf was real and the thing took taste, it's aether' winding is one wheel-tooth away from her address.*

She knew all this.

And then there was the rest of the rest. Below the map's descending stem — squeezed into the gutter by a sleeping hand — lay a crude *seven-ringed sigil*: concentric rings, a single point at the centre, exactly the proportions of the embossed ornament on the back of the old notebook — the one the sisters at Durgakund had found with her.

Twenty-one years ago, on a January dawn, a cane tray lined with coarse cloth had been left at the home's back gate with a five-day-old girl-child in it — breathing, stranded, quiet — and nothing else from her beginning except one small leather-bound object tied to her left wrist with a strip of cloth: the notebook. The sisters had, with the prudence this city masters early, declared it a birth-mother's diary of verses, or some widow's bhajan-copy, and shelved it like a little alcove-god of no provable cult. On Ira's seventh birthday it passed to her by house custom: *any object tied to you when you arrived remains yours forever.* One khaki envelope of tagged belongings, carried across six hundred small lives.

Since then she had carried it nowhere without her. Wondering, rarely, half-joking, what its worth might be as a prophecy. Rebinding its spine once, at thirteen, with a glue-gun and an old dupatta, in tears. Memorizing every dent. And never once in all her life laying the dream's shape beside the cover's shape —

She opened it now to the back cover.

Seven rings. One centre-point.

Her thumbnail measured the buckled emboss to the width of the Dream Map's gutter scrawl. Identical.

*Twenty-one years I've been carrying the plant drawing. All this time the map has been a map of what — seven* —

She closed it, palm flat on the leather, heart sprinting.

*The cup exists. There are seven drinking-points and one intake. One node in the east is freshly fattening. The next shifts are countable. I could give this thing an address. I could stand at the black mouth of the pattern with witnesses —*

In the dark, the phone torch burned a white spot into her lap, and all at once the twenty-one years of her filed themselves into one single folder: every measurement her days had taken and every image her nights had deposited were the same catalog, folded through each other like pages in an uncut book —

The second sight settled over her seeing like a sheet of glass. No fire, no glow. Just — *addedness*. In the torch-scrim she could now perceive the room doubled: the compass on the table quivering *not* on east but pointing inward toward her; the bowl-water shivering with small opposed rings colliding; both incense cones sunken into their rice; and — very faint — a bright thread of disturbed air trailing down the staircase, as if someone had just walked out of a house that had slept through everything.

*— either the boy's sealed furnace had left that trail here days ago. Or tonight something else had stood at the bottom of her stairs and left.*

She did not decide about it. Instead, precisely, she hid the map.

A notebook is a kingdom she could fortify. Between the envelope of tax receipts and the orphanage's khaki document-file, the dream-page went in, folded flat to nothing, thumbed down until its graphite slept inside the seam. Then the notebook went back cover-down under three library-returns, so that the seven-ring ornament touched bare wood. Then she performed the household ritual of safety — the shelf straightened, the bowl re-calibrated with an almost-steady hand — because ritual is what the frightened use when armour is unavailable.

Nobody would see that page. Not yet. Data unshared was data unsacrificed.

She pulled on the windcheater.

---

Later — 4:26 AM, the river below the ghats asleep like a black pelt, the load-shedding cradling Assi in radio-dead peace — she stepped out onto the little landing above the courtyard. Mausi snored gently on her khatiya somewhere below. The morning-singers at the far ghat were clearing their throats into the cold, tuning the city by hand.

A dog sat alone under her window — the spotted one from the temple-rags, folded asleep onto himself — who raised his head once at her coming, as if to ask whether she had seen whatever it was that had visited the staircase.

She stood under the naked bulb and looked down at him until comprehension arrived.

"Kyun dikha rahe ho mujhe?" Ira asked, very quietly. "Why are you showing me?"

The dog, being Varanasi's, knew too much to answer.

She climbed back up the spiral iron with the notebook held against her ribs like a second heartbeat, set it into the wall-tin shelf and slid the tin shut — the way the house's older residents had once hidden their gold —

— and beneath her feet, down the ancestral stone staircase with its century-old mason-marks worn smooth, the house listened for one hairline-long moment, and the earth below it carried on drinking.

---

Mausi was awake when she crossed the courtyard in the first grey, sitting up on the khatiya combing the night's frost out of her hair with oiled fingers, the comb's discipline so regular it was almost a clock. The load-shedding had ended. Somewhere someone's inverter thudded forgivingly on.

"Chai pi ke ja," the widow said, without looking up — the phrase the house issued every clear dawn, part blessing, part tenancy payment.

"Kal chutti hai. Pi lungi lambe."

"Aaj." Mausi's hand found the lota. "Tere gaal pe raat ki main lakeerein hain. Barish ki bhi aek shruti kabhi shunya se shuru hoti hai uski, jaanti hai." She looked up, then, with eyes the colour of old silver coins. "Neend ka kharab mausam hai. Sabko aa raha hai. Pi ja."

Ira took the chai in both palms the way one receives at a temple, and standing there in the courtyard's up-and-running dimness — the tulsi pot, the crow-bath saucer, the laundry-line sagging between two eras — she drank it, hot and over-sweet, and for one false-brave second considered telling this oldest tenant of the city everything.

The widow reached out instead, unprompted, felt Ira's forehead with the back of her ruined-articulated hand — a grandmother's triage older than any nursing — nodded at the verdict, and returned to her comb.

"Kambaal de rahi hoon," she said. "Room mein. Kuch dikhe to gaana ga lena, kuch bhi."

And Ira — recorder of tremors, cartographer of cups — went back up her iron stairs carrying the chai's after-warmth, and did not stop shaking until the door shut.

---

# CHAPTER 9 — DASHASHWAMEDH

Ira called it at half past four in the afternoon, in the language of people who had decided, without ever once voting on it, that they were going.

The group chat — BHU MECH BATCH '26 FINAL (STRICTLY NOTES) — had been a cemetery for three days, since Rudra's *fever* lie and Veer's blue-ticked silence and the video links that kept dying four hours after they spawned. Then, at 16:31, a message from Ira, who had never once in three years of friendship started a chat thread herself:

**Aaj shaam aarti. Dashashwamedh. Chaaron. 6:30, phoolwale ke paas. Bahana mat banana.**

Then, eleven seconds later — the entire rest of her relationship with punctuation, delivered as a second message, with a photograph attached: a compass needle lying across a sheet of graph paper, hard-east, quivering.

**Kal raat +9. Abhi +14. Kuch aaj shaam kheenchega. Main jaa rahi hai tum log aao ya na aao.**

Veer's reply took twelve seconds: **aa raha hoon.**

Aadhya's took forty minutes and said, in its entirety: **Idiot log. Theek hai. Lekin agar kisi ne mujhse kuch 'feel' karwaya toh main gaandhe nadi mein dhakke maar dungi.**

Rudra read the thread standing at the gate of his lane at dusk, with the smell of his mother's tadka behind him and his father's storeroom being audited above, and typed his one-word complicity with a thumb that didn't trust itself:

**aaya.**

At 17:40 he told his mother he was going to the aarti with Veer. It was the most legal sentence he had produced in a fortnight, and it was almost true, and Rukmini received it standing in the kitchen doorway with a folded towel over one shoulder and for one second — one only, the width of an intake — something crossed her face that was not relief and not permission but the specific stillness of a woman recalculating all her clocks.

"Jaa," she said. "Par Rudra."

He paused with his slippers half-on.

"Bheed mein rehna," she said. "Akela mat jaana. Aur—" the towel folded itself again under her fingers, "—agar ghar jaldi aana pade toh der mat karna."

From the roof came the sound of his father's small hammer against the water-tank's stand, regular as a second-hand. Rudra looked at his mother's dark eyes and understood with a drop of cold that she'd just handed him three sentences and one indictment, and that the indictment was for the father upstairs: *she had heard the count of that hammering all afternoon, and she had counted on him having heard it too.*

"Aata hoon," he said. "Jaldi."

---

They crossed the last of Godowlia as a unit, in single file, because the lanes had ceased to be lanes and become breathing.

This was the festival-old part of the city — the zone where the street was exactly as wide as two elbows and one bursting cardboard carton — and tonight it was full to its ancient rafters: marigold strings drooping from every balcony like the city's own aarti-trays mirrored upward; paan-stalls doing the year's best business off folded silver leaf; a bulb-cart grinding its generator through a crowd that stepped around the noise the way the patient step around snoring dogs. Underfoot, packed puja-sindoor and ghee-drip and the dust of three millennia conspired into a paste that took impressions well: the tread of pilgrims' sandals, the neat little circles where the businessmen put down their baskets, the drag-marks of the wheelchair wheels of the very old — all pressed into the lane like glyphs of a script written only by feet.

Aadhya walked at the middle of their little line, inside the crush, with her dupatta's edge up over her mouth — not out of propriety but because the press of human intention had become physical in this lane; she could feel it arrive, the way you feel a wave's lift before it reaches you; the crowd was *straining* upward, all of them together, without knowing— *stop it. Focus.*

She was better at it than she had been the night at the banyan, and worse at hiding it. Twice Veer glanced back when her breathing changed, each time ready to lay against the crowd the leverage of his shoulders, each time met by her flat wide *bas kar* eyes. She was learning, in this lane, something she had no intention of passing on as a skill: that the pull from below went up past the ghat's steps to somewhere past Godowlia; that the lanes' little temple-niches — the marble Hanuman halfway down with his vermilion face, the seven-stone cluster under the pipal root where a woman's old offerings of eleven-years-running were finally losing their shine — that these were *arteries*, all of them knotted somewhere under her feet, over-visited and over-loved, the savings of narrow lanes held in low vaults.

She could *hear* the vaults. Emptying.

Her mouth was dry by the time they reached the main stair. "Paanch minat," she told Veer's returning stare. "Bas paanch minat."

Rudra, three bodies ahead, turned; he'd heard her through the din — he did not know how — and she had the weird clarity of realizing, with the exactness of fear, that he hadn't *heard* her at all: he'd felt her feeling it, the way a finger dipped in water knows the water without touching it. For a half-second their eyes met across the flower-vendor's bargaining haggle and something passed between them — some weak shock, some electricity of kinship — and they both looked away from it as from an embarrassing contour; and the lane bore them down, together, right and down, into the great-lit churn of the ghaat.

---

Dashashwamedh at six in the evening was not a ghat. It was an organism with a heartbeat you could stand inside.

The broad steps fell to the river in terraces alive to their last inch: flower-sellers with their pyramids of marigold and rose; the leaf-bowl men with their wick-and-ghee merchandise balanced on forearms like waiters of the devout; photographers menacing families with instant prints; chai-boys threading shoulder-first through the crowd's frozen admiration; a colony of pandas in white, hereditary and territorial, arranging mats and dakshina-boxes along the front like ministers of an empire older than any flag. The loudspeakers — four of them, tea-crate sized, hoisted on poles like secular rakshasas — had already begun the warm-up: bells, a conch, the tannoy's soft feedback whine, a woman's taped voice saying *manavta ki raksha* over and over into the dusk.

Below, the river had gone the colour of bronze dust. Boats stood on it in slow brown rows, people silhouetted from the waist up, every head turned to the ghatside platform like iron filings to a magnet.

And on the platform: the seven priests, drawing into formation in their crimson and gold, lifting the first tier of lamps.

Ira was at the flower-stall rendezvous five minutes early, which for her was late by twenty-three minutes. She had on a kurta whose pockets visibly mattered to its structure: from the left came the squared-off weight of the old mariner's compass; from the right, her phone; and pinned inside her sleeve against the wrist, folded to nothing, a strip of thermal paper pale and purposeless as a bus ticket — the kind of receipt-like evidence she kept issuing to herself because paper did not argue back.

"Lo," she said, without greeting, handing each of them a small brass safety pin. "Yahan." She tapped her own collar. "Phenolphthalein nahi hai, gurdware ke bhandare ki chai nahi hai, kuch gyaan ka item nahi hai. Bas ek pin. Agar milte hain ki cheez galat direction mein bah rahi hai, haath mein le lo. Pin thanda hai. Cheez garam lagegi. Farq."

"Ye bhi science hai?" Veer said softly, pinning it with his good arm. The bandage had moved up out of sight under his sleeve two days ago; what remained near the wrist was glossy, fractal, and faintly tan, like oil on teak. He wore it like a watch he was ignoring.

"Ye *safety* hai," Ira said, and fell into step.

A couple of facts they exchanged on the steps down, because facts were the only sugar Ira trusted:

— the Pancha Koshi ring's whole circumference ran, on her figures, at one-point-three per cent dimmer per twelve hours, had done so for nine nights, and tonight's rate had changed;

— the change was *not* a rise or a fall, it was a *concentration*: flows along the larger ring were shallowing while one location fattened, as if somebody had pinched all the tubes of a city-wide drip to fill a single syringe;

— and the syringe's needle, on the map, ended here. At the ghats.

"Toh ye Aarti hi hai," Aadhya said quietly, behind Ira's shoulder, before anyone could say it. Her voice had gone flat in the particular way of a person keeping a hand over an instrument so it doesn't flow. "Mera matlab — aarti hi toh hai. Itne log, roz, itna *bharosa* ek saath…" She did not finish the sentence. She was looking at the crowd — thousands of faces burnished a uniform gold by the lamp-tier's first raising — the way a loan officer looks at a bank.

"Pure reservoir ki ATM queue," Ira said.

"Don't," Aadhya said, softly and with feeling. "Ye ATM mat bolo."

"Fine," Ira said. And for a second the two of them stood together at the edge of the light in something that, in the accounting of later years, would count as the moment the four became *the four*: two girls at the edge of the oldest show in town, watching faith behave like weather, frightened together.

Then Rudra said, "Udhar," in a voice none of them had heard him use before, and pointed.

---

The three men stood at the ghat's north edge, in the shadow of a shuttered ticket-office, and they were wrong. Not drama-wrong. Not villain-wrong. *Continuity-wrong*: three figures in the ghatscap that the eye kept apologizing for and stepping past.

Wrongness one: the aarti was to their left, four hundred feet of gold smoke and devotion, ten thousand pairs of lungs beginning the aarti-breath together, and all three men were facing the other way. Not the river. The buildings.

Wrongness two: a lamp-flame crowd gust went sideways over their heads — aarti-smoke, camphor, six hundred wicks breathing out at the same time — and around the three of them the smoke didn't go sideways. It slipped around them as if around a stone. Aadhya saw it first. She stopped walking so abruptly that Veer took one step past her, reversed, and planted, and the pause ran down their skirmish-line like catching a thrown glass.

"Kya hua?"

"Wo teen," Aadhya said.

Wrongness three — and this one Ira's eyes caught between one breath and the next: all three were dressed enough like surveyors on a contract to disappoint inspection — grey windcheaters, hard-hat cases at their feet, laminated tags on lanyards — but one of them, the lean one in the middle with a grey face like an unsharpened pencil, held in his two hands, at waist height, gently, like a brass lamp-tray, an instrument: a U-shaped frame of dull metal, palm-sized, with a glassine coil basking in its crook, and along its arms a row of small vents that breathed.

The coil was glowing. Faintly. At a rate that Ira's wrist-strip was already answering: the thermal paper had taken its first centimetre of black.

"Sab idhar," Ira said, and they closed in behind pillars of moving people under the poet's-desk shadow of the steps-side building wall. "Bheed ka smoke unki taraf jaa raha hai— nahin, *smell* nahi, kuch aur—" she dragged her own vocabulary up like a bucket from a dry well— "garmahat. Jo lamp mein se nahi uthti. Log de rahi hain. Wo le rahe hain."

"Yeh kya bol rahi hai tu," Veer said, smiling without cheer, not disbelieving, asking for terms.

"Theek tarah se bol rahi hoon," Ira said flatly. "Wo teen aarti kyun nahi dekh rahe? Kyunki wo yahan bhakti ke liye nahi hain. Wo *collection* ke liye hain. Dekho—"

The middle man turned his instrument four degrees minutely, as a radio-operator tunes. The coil's glow climbed. And in the front row of the aarti — sixty feet away, one of the crowd, an old woman in a green sari who had stood up on tired knees to see the lamps over the shoulders in front, alone on her own personal pilgrimage — the old woman sat down suddenly.

People around her mistook it for devotion, tiredness. A granddaughter bent to her. Twenty feet on, a man pressed two fingers to his temple and leaned on the barricade. Somewhere behind them a child started crying out of nothing. It was like watching sunshine being collected off water by a blade — from a distance, everything glitters in the same direction and nobody inside the water knows who's drinking.

Aadhya's hand had gone to Ira's wrist. Under her nails the thermal strip blackened another centimetre, silent as rot.

The aarti went on. That was the worst of it. The very geniuses of the evening — the priests — sweated under their tilting crowns and went on hitting their marks out of will and habit, the way old singers hold a note past their breath: but the whole show had taken a fraction off its shine — a wattage only farmers of the invisible would notice: the lamp-tiers' flames burned half an inch shorter; the smoke, which in all the history of this ghat had always climbed straight through all the noise, a good high devotional ribbon, tonight flattened at eight feet as if meeting a ceil­ing and rolled sideways down-river, hugging the faces of the crowd; and on the third terrace a six-year-old on his father's shoulders, who had been bouncing two seconds ago calling out the lamps' names, laid his head abruptly down on his father's bun and went to sleep with the violence of a wound.

The father never noticed the theft. He thought the child had surrendered, sweetly, to the evening.

"He kaisi chor-gali hai," Veer breathed, watching the rooftops do nothing wrong. "Bheed se aise le rahe hain jaise — jaise dukaan se chawal taul rahe hon."

"DM wale hote toh Aarti rok dete," Ira said. "Yeh DM wale nahi hain."

"Ye kaun hain phir?" Rudra asked.

Nobody answered. It was the first time all four of them heard the question arrive in its full uniform.

*So that's the cup,* Aadhya thought, and tasted blood where none was — the banyan's taste, its sixty years of gratitude carried off; *that's the cup, and tonight it's drinking the whole congregation.*

"Band karna padega," Rudra said.

Voices of reason assembled — Veer's mouth was already open with the sensible frame of *bhai, police…* — and all of them knew, all four, in that moment, with the maps and the measure-keeping and the title-deed of the last ten days in their faces, that this was the thing they had come for, that the weeks of inexplicable dread had purely been *warrant-serving*: the training exercises, the banyan, the dream, the seal — all of it had been the irrelevant source-package of this exact decision, *band karna padega*, the we-don't-know-how, and Veer shut his mouth and nodded, once, taking his own prior objection into private custody.

"Pehle pakad ke dekhenge," Rudra said. "Bheed mein kuch nahi. Ghat ke peeche, masonry wali side — udhar kheenchna hoga. Log pehle. Cheez baad mein."

---

What broke the procedure — the polite first-minute of it, in which Rudra and Veer approached the three with the loose, uncrackable bonhomie of two college boys asking about a missing phone and could you gentlemen please sir step this way, there'd been an incident sir, police sir, tanhai mein — was the instrument.

The lean middle man turned it toward Rudra at fifteen feet.

And at fifteen feet, the coil in its crook — the little basking glassine creature — went *white*. Went white and started to vibrate in a visible blur, and its row of breathing vents forgot all their meter and began to suck, and the man's grey face did one human thing all evening — it changed. Confusion first, the operator's confusion of a man whose dial has just scammed him. Then fear: of the instrument, the instrument that had never still'd in his hands before and was *still not still*; then something past fear, an old allegiance look, as if a voice had swum up behind his eyes and told him about a fifteen-year-old fishing trip on this very river decades ago; a look of *receipt.*

He said one word to the man on the left. He said it quietly. Neither of them was wired with visible comms; the word went from mouth to ear the old way.

The word was: **"on-site."**

"Survey kis cheez ka?" Rudra asked, level, slower than his own heartbeat, stepping into the fifteen feet like a man who had been taught from before memory where the centre of a courtyard was. "Kaan ka department? Gas ka, light ka, ya shraddha ka?"

The lean man's insured smile deepened by one respectful notch, the smile of a man who had been briefed for every kind of interference and was delighted to find the species with flair. His laminated tag swung as he adjusted the coil's frame by one more precious degree. The letters on the tag meant nothing. The self-assurance meant everything.

"Beta," he said. "Janta ki seva mein kuch log raat ko bhi kaam karte hain. Tum jaao. Aarti dekho. Photos lo. Apni umar ka maza hai wo." His eyes went once, lazily, past Rudra to the other three — cataloguing them, Raghu-mugger style, the quick inventory of a man pricing crowd-risk — and came back. "Bachpan mein suna hoga: jo cheez samajh na aaye, uske paas mat khadi ho."

"Aur jo cheez chori ho rahi ho?" Rudra said.

The insured smile did not move. But the coil hummed a quarter-tone higher, and the lean man's head tilted, birdlike, and for two full seconds he looked at Rudra the way locksmiths look at safes.

"Gas leak hai," he said gently.

And then, because events had agreed to stop being deniable, the left-hand man reached under his windcheater and pulled down over his right forearm a thing of struts and mesh — a scaffold of black alloy that locked wrist to elbow with three clicks, had a hose to nothing, was more brace than weapon, plumbing as jewellery — and came forward into the space between Rudra and the crowd with his arms relaxed and his eyes doing a street-sweeper's job: moving the audience out of his work-zone.

"Chhote," the man said to Rudra, in a voice of bored municipal kindness. "Hatt ja. Building department. Gas leak hai."

His padded fist, swung casually mid-sentence at the stone newel post beside Rudra's ear — a sort of *tap*, a *here-is-a-demonstration-for-your-imagination* — sheared the post off at its foot. The stone toppled half a tonne into its own dust. People did not scream; people did not even see: the aarti had swollen to its first full mantra, eight thousand voices on *Om Jai Jagdish—*, the loudspeakers peaking, the river answering with claps of boat-wood; the post's falling was an entirely private collapse within a public ecstasy.

"Gas leak hai," the man repeated, smiling. "Hatt ja."

Rudra did not hatt. Nineteen years of mornings came to attention in him like a battalion: heel placed, breath drawn into the drilled count, left shoulder quarter-turned — and the coal under his sternum woke, banking red and glad. *In, four; hold, four.* Down two rings, light at the cracks, the seal his mother had rebuilt that very sunrise; he could feel the whole architecture of her hands in him, and he could feel, with a sprinter's dread, the want behind it: the thing the seal was holding wanted to make itself *shapeable*, wanted out through his shoulder into the lane where it thought fights were proper fuel—

*Abhi nahi.*

"He's going to break concrete," Veer said evenly to nobody, stepping up alongside, one bandaged fist already balled in a way he'd sworn to his mother not to test. "Toh phir todne de. Mera plan hai plate pakad leta hoon."

"Plate?" the left-hand man said. He was having the best day of his career.

"Idiots," said Ira, from completely elsewhere: she had both palms flat to a wall ten paces away. Not on them. *Reading them.* "Rudra — wo jo coil hai — wo *tune* kar raha hai tujhe! Ye log aarti se hi nahi khinch sakte — ye *territory* mein khichte hain — tujhse bhi! Bhaag nahin — *door ho jaa* usse—"

The lean middle man smiled an insured smile, teeth square as windows. The coil's whine, heard now by everybody, slid up a fifth. And the instrument's glassine heart, warm and still thinking with a year's savings, pulled one thread straight through the banquet of the crowd, through the brass lamp-smoke, through the layers of evening-smeared air — and laid it on Rudra's breastbone like a hook.

He understood two things at once. Both arrived voiceless.

First: that his mother had been asked by life, nineteen years ago, to become a mason of the invisible, and had done the job with both hands, and the coil had just read her joinery like a thief reading a lock.

Second: that a thing with no eyes had just picked him out of ten thousand people at ten feet. Specifically him. By his *lock.*

He thought of the pyre. Of the mouth on the horizon cupping its quiet thunder. Of his father's staff trembling by four-and-a-half millimetres.

*The cup is drinking Varanasi,* Ira had said, in the beige light of a cafe, three days ago. *It is exactly as simple as that.*

The fight made its paperwork first — the way all real fights do, in two and a half seconds of logistics.

Sharper than the blow was what preceded it: the crowd to their left was creaming slowly north beneath the loudspeakers, all eyes claimed; to the right, the shadow-yard of fallen masonry beside the ticket-office wall, floating in ghee-smoke and deemed poor shelter by ten thousand correct decisions. The filing-cabinet man — the third scout, the one built like furniture — had already drifted on silent feet to situate himself on the rubble's rim, cutting the boys' lane to the main stair. Veer faced him. The two big men regarded each other across nine feet of cracked flagstone with the eerie professional smoothness of two doors recognizing a doorway.

"College ka ladka," the filing-cabinet man said gently.

"Sarkari daftar ka coolie," Veer said back, equally gently, rolling his bandaged wrist once, and the crowd roared behind them at some peak of the aarti and neither of them moved.

Then Rudra stepped forward. Breath four. Heel set. Shoulder set. Jaw set. The coal in his chest hissed like a stove being turned _up_. His knuckle-wraps — rewound fresh that morning with his mother's goddamn mule-like patience — bit into his palms exactly where they'd been dried twelve hours before. Something in his father wanted out of him. Something behind that wanted *more* out of him. Somebody's promise of nineteen years and a locked name. All of him went on the ball of his left foot and he swung, low, rising, the body's entire honest curriculum delivered into one unsparing frame —

His fist met the coil-held chest of the lean middle man squarely over the breastbone.

The man staggered. Not from the strike — he took the strike, all of it; it shuddered down through the strut-braced composite of his gear and came to nothing; the man staggered from what the strike *detached.* The coil, white-blurring, was flung against his sternum with the pilgrimage hordes' saved vapour dragging through it, for one half-second his meter reversed — got confused — stuttered — the man cried out as the flow hiccuped back down the line, and the whip of the returning taste nearly took Rudra's chest off with it —

Behind and right, Veer went in under the filing-cabinet man's first methodical reach, got both arms around a torso built like a safe, and lifted. The man let himself be lifted — that was the terrible part — let the boy's shoulders do three-quarters of the throw and then *settled*, somehow, mid-arc, heavy as a dropped blame, and came down planted, and palmed Veer sideways into the ticket-office wall with one flat hand, once, the way a toll-booth barrier dismisses a cycle. The wall crunched. The plaster came down off its bricks in fans. Veer hit his knees with his vision swept flat, heard distantly through the cotton of it a woman's voice — Aadhya's, quickly, quietly, close:

"Chachi — Chachi uthiye — bas yahan paas aaiye, sans lijiye — dekho, dekho Chinki ko, jaag gayi na, dekho —" — the old woman in the green sari — being walked, held upright by Aadhya's forearms trembling under the bones of her, out of the smoke's flattened ceiling into the lane's open air, away, while behind Aadhya the little one — Chinki — rode out on a stranger-uncle's shoulder, whimpering with the epic offended astonishment of a sleepy child; and it would have been a good moment, a moment with something human defended in its teeth, if Ira's wrist had not chosen it to go ice-cold:

"Rudra," she said, and her voice had lost its last civilian register. "Wo coil ab sirf *tujhe* padh raha hai. Sirf tujhe—"

—and Rudra heard the crack.

—and Rudra heard the crack.

It had no business being among the combat noises of the world. It belonged to glacier-photographs, to temple-bell bronze flawlines, to the internal sound-works of great ships at dock. It went *off* inside him — a *snap* and then a dragging velvety *give* — felt not heard, heard not felt, an event of frequency his skeleton absorbed before air could carry.

*The kavach,* his mind said clearly, from behind the shield-wall of his fathers' drills, in a voice that was almost his mother's. *Cracked.*

On his feet, his face gave away absolutely nothing. His mouth stayed a fight's mouth. But in his chest the coal lifted suddenly off its measure and began to stare out through a place no knot covered—

— out. *It can see out.*

And far above them, where the aarti's first mantra was only gilding the edges of the rising night-air of Varanasi — eleven small fires raised at a signal in perfect unison, the priests' lamps wheeling through their ancient arcs, the river's far side collecting faintly scavenging eyes — something at the edges of sight assembled no alarm.

Very far below the ghats, the seventh part of the ring's cup drank.

---

# CHAPTER 10 — THE PROTECTOR'S FIST

The fight, when it properly began, lasted eleven seconds before it started breaking things that didn't belong to anybody.

Afterwards — afterwards each of them would privately demolish and rebuild the eleven seconds their whole lives, would disagree about order and direction and who had stood where, would agree on nothing except two facts: that something under the ghat had been drinking when it should not have been, and that the fight had ended at a stone pillar with a sound like a mountain coughing itself loose; and that in between there had been *a light* — and that this light had come out of Veer Sawant's right hand and gone back into it again, like a guest who realizes he has the wrong address.

The eleven seconds went, as best as witnesses who were also bleeding could reconstruct them, like this.

The left-hand man with the strut-braced arm — the building-department smile, the dented newel post, the voice of bored municipal kindness — took one interrupted look at his superior, who was still bent over the coil that had mistaken Rudra for a harvest-row. Confusion read upward from operator to operator the way weather moves between fields. The third man, until then silent at the base of the pillar of the ruined gate — a man built like a filing cabinet, with a lanyard-tag that said nothing in grey laminate — moved to close the bag off behind them: eight casual paces, positioning a body the size of a sofa between the boys and the crowd.

Ira moved first. She broke — not toward the fight, not away from it, but across its mean grain, cutting behind the ruined gate's tilting masonry on the diagonal she'd been occupying for forty seconds — the paces between her wrist-strip blackening by the centimetre. She wasn't armed. She didn't intend to be. She had a different idea entirely: kneel at the lean middle man's feet, at his case of brushed steel laid open like a doctor's bag — she had memorized which gear was being fed into what with maps of her own — and throw the brass-and-coil idiot *scavenger* into Ganga six steps away, downstream of ten thousand prayers.

She got both hands on the case.

The left-hand man — who had never once turned his head — moved without turning it.

Later she would try to describe the next two seconds to Rudra on a roof where words would go, and would have to settle for: *it was like watching a train decide to stop.* The man's arm came across through empty air toward her — an arm with a machine's sleeve of alloy mesh, braced about the forearm like a dark puppeteer caging the bone — coming on a line for her face with the detachment of a ticket-stamper —

— and then there came from around the gate's ruin, at a run that wasn't running, a shape.

Veer took the blow on the bandage.

It made a sound. Everyone in earshot — however few of them, inside the midge-cluster of roar the aarti had become — understood that sound as the end of something human. His whole forearm from wrist to elbow went white-hot agony; the skin of it moved visibly sideways and came back; and under his feet the flags of the ghat's old charity-flagstones were station-pegged with something that went out from him through the arches of his soles and came back to him *as load*; and he stood, exactly as he had promised his mother one midnight, small, aged eleven, feverish, waiting with a tumbler of nimbu-pani in service beside her matches — *stand, son, you are a post; your mother is the tent* —

and the strut-braced arm, strong enough to shear a stone newel, *stopped on him.*

Stopped.

And in the stopped one second — held in the palm of that halted blow like the hush after a mis-hit thunderclap — Veer had time for one whole memory, the kind a drowning man is rumoured to receive free of charge. He was nine again, in their lane in Sigra, and the fruit-seller's cart had gone over on its axle-pin at the corner by the temple tank, pinning the old pony; and the men — his uncles, the shopkeepers — had all been arranging to lift, talking it into committees, when nine-year-old Veer had quietly gone under the cart-edge himself and stood up. The cart had risen off his back like a basket off a hook. The pony had scrambled free. The lane had cheered. And he had run home without waiting for the sweets, run home crying, not from pain — there had been no pain — from the *goodness* of it, the sweet, smooth, terrible rightness in his spine when the weight was the most and he was the least broken by it; and his mother, flour-handed at the door, had taken his wet face in both hands and said what mothers say when they are translating God in real time: *Beta, isliye toh strong diya hai. Rakhna. Baten mat.*

He had never told anyone that he had gone back to the same corner, three evenings running, secretly hoping the cart would fall again.

The filing-cabinet man paused mid-stride, something wrong addressing his instincts. The bored kindness drained out of the left-hand man's face. For one breath the two of them — appliance and post — were eye to eye over the halted forearm. The bandage on Veer's forearm was smoking faintly at every seam. And Veer — who carried no groan aloud, who had given the night so far only a couple of closed hasty prayers and one rude joke about plates — felt the thing he had been reporting since the pyre-ghat, the drag of darkness he had been paying for in pharmacy bills, decide that the arrangement was concluded: that whatever had been *transferred into* him at Manikarnika that night — as he kept bed, trembled, and tried to read his palm under tube-light — had come into him not as injury but as deposit; was here now as calling; and stood up out of the small of his back like a tenant given notice, and opened the windows of his right arm.

His right fist caught the light.

Not reflected it. Caught it the way a lamp's flame lights from inside — the knuckles became four small suns behind his own skin; the forearm's old burn lines shone suddenly through the dressing in seams of gold like a paper lantern held to a bonfire; the whole weapon he had dreamt of himself as — the colonial power of him, the peacekeeper of six-feet-four and two hundred and twenty honest-born weights — kindled up in a single position, and every person present, down to the three scouts, understood the difference between a punch and *will* instantly, the way everyone understands the difference between a grocery list and a psalm.

Veer punched.

The lazy kindness had not yet finished leaving the left-hand man's face when the gold arrived at his jaw's angle and undoubtedly completed something in it. The man left the ground. He left the ground with the completeness of a man lifted by carpet — cleanly, wholly, his pivot never found — travelled three body lengths through the October-night ghat-smoke, struck the waist of the great gate-pillar of the fallen shrine — a column of chunar sandstone as old as the first maratha repairs, four feet across its flats, base sunk past Ganga's own memory — and the pillar, which had stood through the flaying of two empires and the appetites of four centuries of river, *cracked through and carried him onward*, crown shearing down.

The falling of it took two seconds exactly.

In those two seconds, the aarti's second mantra was climbing its wheels of flame at the platform; the river answered with its clapped choir of wooden hulls; a woman's conch-armed exhale completed itself — and within the walled noise of all that devotion, inside the little leased privacy behind the gate-ruin, gold light drained out of a fist and nothing in the world noticed, because nothing in the world had been looking — except, sixty feet up the ghat's flank, the third-floor window bars of a sleeping pilgrim-lodge, behind which a very old person sat up out of no reason and put one palm flat against the cold glass, and blinked, twice, at Dashashwamedh, as if a grandchild had called a name in another room.

It took the rest with no sound at all — as if the ghat had considered denying the fact and then thought better of it. The crown of the column went into the river forty feet down with a sound entirely disproportionate, a bloop of stone; the man went into the masonry works below the gate and lay there, his alloy scaffold sparking once in a little electric desperate dying twitch; the remaining two men, operator-surgeons with nerves of calibrated lunch-room steel, did their sums on the spot and found that the locals had acquired manager-grade assets.

The lean middle man looked at the coil — dead now, drained into the blowback; looked at Rudra, at what was written there that he could not see; looked at the fist of the pillar-breaker and did some addressing of reports in his head.

He said one word. Not *retreat* — it was not a soldier's word — a word, rather, of technical scope: *abort.*

The two of them were gone down the northern steps within eight seconds carrying their cases, and by the time they were gone the crowd behind the ruined gate was swarming down the first terrace like water finding its bowl, public opinion rapidly assembling forensics — whose building, whose pillar, whose boys — and the machine of Varanasi rumour, whose opened floodgates no ministry could close, got its head into its hands.

---

Veer stood looking at his own right hand as if someone else had been wearing it.

The gold was gone. It went back down the seams of the old burn like water abandoning the roots it had lifted up; the knuckles were bare skin and blunt ordinary blood, one split across the fourth knuckle, the fist of a boy who had punched something once and remorsefully. He turned the hand over — palm up — and the light there was sodium, and nobody's gold; and something in the way he turned it over, slowly, like a man reading his own suspension-order, made Ira stop from a dead run — eyes burning brine of two careful days turned to molasses in one second — and say,

"Veer."

He put the hand behind his back like it was stolen goods. The trembling had begun in both forearms at once, the kind of deep structural trembling that the body issues when a thing has been asked of it that it never budgeted for. He was whiter than anyone in that crowd seemed entitled to be at three ghats past midnight.

"Maine aise—" He tried his voice out. It failed by a syllable. Tried it again. "Maine *aise kya kiya*—" He looked, for purchase, at the pillar's sheared stump. Pieces of the man he had felled were climbing out from the rubble under their own power with the alert, affectless economy of insects. Out of the smile of the fight the left-hand man's jaw was wrong in a way that looked systemic. His eyes were open. He moved his fingers — one — two — they worked; the scaffold on the arm sparked in sympathy.

"Th-Th-THE—Rudra—"

"Sambhal ke!" Rudra was there — shouldering past the first glance-down faces of the pressing public, catching Veer by the upper arm below the ancients-of-Ranjit-colony tattoo: hauling him round towards the ghat wall where the dark angles of gate rubble would take them all. The crowd behind them broke over the ruined column like a fast tide over a wreck.

"Ladke—!" a panda shouted after them, pointing — "IDHAR AAYA THA NA PATTHAR—"

"Civil wall gira!" Rudra shouted back, turning his head enough for the voice to carry, his face absolutely at peace underneath: seventeen years of good-son training on parade. "Poorana hai! Yahan dekho — sab baras se gira hua hai! Aap idhar mat khade raho—" and he pointed helpfully at the leaning masonry of the ticket-office wall, a standing source of authentic menace, year round.

Public opinion, rudderless, swung its hundred heads to the harmless-seeming structural unsoundness and surrounded it, donating theories freely.

"BMC ka kaam hai—"
"BMC yahan hota hi nahin hai, Nagar Nigam hai—"
"Earthquake tha chhota sa? Maine feel kiya—"
"Beam gira hoga — aarti ki vibration se—"
"Vibration se *pathar* girta hai?" — the oldest man present, offended to his bones — "Beta, pathar KABHI nahi girta. Hum hain toh Banaras hai—"
"Mujhe lagta hai flash-mob tha—"

Somewhere beyond the terraces a police whistle began its long administrative argument with the evening, two sharp notes, then three, then a whole grammar of them, and the crowd performed the mandatory dispersal-shuffle of people who want simultaneously to help and to never be named in connection with anything. A boatman in the second row was already telling a stranger the definitive account: a landslide; no, a cracker; no, a politician's protest; his cousin had seen it personally — all three versions delivered in a single breath with the serene promiscuity of an eyewitness who has seen nothing and will therefore miss nothing.

Behind the corner of rubble Rudra stopped Veer with a hand he didn't know he was using yet. Pulled him back against the wall, braced him face-to-crowd like a product display, and said — casually, loudly, for the ten citizens on the other side of the stones to hear —

"Thoda behosh hai yaar, kuch zyada hi garmi thi na—" the voice of a boy comforting his taller classmate — and into the private breath-space between their sternums, dropping every register out from under his throat: "Chup. Chup reh. *Kuch nahi hua.* Ira — ghabrao mat bas— hato— usko seedha, seedha— Seedha khada karo—" — Veer's knees were another matter, were finding their own independent line of descent down the wall — "—Aadhya— pani— ungliyon pe—"

Veer put his face into the angle of the wall above Rudra's head and said into the stones, very low: "Mujhse gaadi palat jaati toh mujhe pata tha kya main kaun hoon. Pillar nahi palta tha. *Meri muthi palat gayi.*" He paused to reassemble himself. "Ladke se lekar police tak sab pooch rahe rahenge. Jhooth bola maine. Abuse bhi kiya. Thappad maar dala aadmi ko. Par pillar, Rudra — pillar maine giraya. Muthi se. Cheez jo mein hoon usme aisi cheez kahan se aa gayi?"

"Ruk," Rudra said.

"Rudra—"

"Ruk." Something in Rudra's chest — the thing that had lain under his mother's knit-boned morning work and his father's uneasy hammer-nights and now stood slightly more visible than it had yesterday, not even three hours ago — gave a seam's fine rebus of pain. Under his kurta collar, where the cloth until that night had been empty pavements, a hairline — a thread of warm geometry, silver-dark and straight — flexed awake for exactly the duration of one heartbeat, and banked down, and Rudra, whose body had been raised all its life to read the inner furniture of training, knew what he had just been informed of.

He kept his voice. Kept it the entire way down to even.

"Aaj raat tu zyada kehta hai toh sab pakda jayega," he said. "Kal baat karte hain. *Kal.* Aaj: bhaago. Bheed mein raho. Kuch nahi hua. Hum aarti dekhne aaye the. Sabko yahi bolna hai."

Through the gravel underfoot, beneath the week's collected dirt and the reverent spill of ghiya from a hundred boats' shoulders and the conch-thrum changing its speed toward second mantra — dark water filled the city's bucket, counted by ten gold things and two hands over an elbow. Four city lads and lasses with a blackening receipt stood in the crush of the oldest evening on the river, in the smoke, receiving the crowd's elbowing benign oblivion like taxis. None of them could have said what they'd seen.

Below them all, at forty-five feet under the platform's flagstone, a strand of the ring the last of the coil's salvaged leak ran rewoven — snapped — and the drain beneath it opened its little black ear, half an inch wider.

---

They stood for a while where the north lane took them — in the dark passage with the broken streetlight two ghats above the aarti — each of them facing out, a box-formation without anyone meaning it.

The last chai-stall of the lane was down to its embers; the chaiwala, a vertical skinny man dozing upright against his own kettle like a bird on a branch, sold them four glasses anyway — not because four glasses is profit, but because four young people standing silent in a broken formation at eleven at night, dust on three of them and one of them white as paper, is a sight that Varanasi chaiwalas process without comment, and with extra adrak.

Nobody said anything for the first two sips.

Then Aadhya took Veer's right hand, and Veer let her — and that was a new thing, that letting — and turned it, palm up, under the stall's weak bulb. The skin of the knuckles was split in two places, ordinary split skin, ordinary bone-colored bruises coming up under it, ordinary boy. She looked at it a long time, fingertip hovering just above the fourth knuckle, not touching, because out of superstition or science she was afraid she'd be able to feel the thing through the skin if she touched it; and behind her eyes, unsaid, the arithmetic of her own night three days ago at the banyan's roots — measured now against *this* — and the estimates she came to made her shut her eyes.

"Garam tha?" she asked finally, very low.

Veer thought about it for some time. "Garam nahi," he said. "…Accha laga." His voice went small in a way nobody had ever heard from Veer before, the voice of a man confessing to a diary. "Ek second ke liye. Sabse zyada accha jo maine kabhi feel kiya. Yehi dara raha hai mujhe. Pillar nahi."

No one said anything. The kettle sighed like the long-suffering matron of all of them.

"Kal college mein," Ira said, "sabko lagna chahiye ki hum aarti mein the aur kuch gadbad hui aas paas, hum ghabra gaye aur ghar aa gaye. That's it. That's the whole story. Jo bhi udhar hua —" her eyes went once to Veer's fist, once to Rudra's sternum, once, by accident, to her own blackening wrist-strip still folded in her sleeve, "— chaaron jaante hain. Kisi paanchve ko nahi. Police ko nahi. Ghar pe nahi."

"Parents ko nahi?" Veer looked up. I've never lied to my mother in my life, his face said, with the authority of the genuinely blessed. It was a real question.

"Tere ghar pe bhi nahi, gadhe," Rudra said, and then stopped, because the memory of his own father standing on a roof with a staff across his knees had come up in him like a wave and he was no longer sure what he was arguing; and started over, gentler: "Baad mein. Jab pata ho kya bolna hai. Abhi bolenge toh... wo log sabko door kar denge. Ghar samajhna."

And there it was — out in the air, in a broken lane, over four glasses of adrak chai — the pact. Nobody signed it. Everybody did.

"Lekin ek baat," Veer said, looking down the lane toward the river-glare. "Woh teen — wapis aayenge."

"Hmm," Ira said. "Log hain. Ladke nahi. Organization hai unke peeche. Loudspeaker wala din nahi tha aaj unka — trial tha. Instruments test ho rahe the." She finished her glass and set it down on the counter with the exactitude of a seal on a letter. "Aaj aaye the toh kal aayenge. Aage se milte rahenge."

"Toh hum taiyaar karenge," Veer said.

"Tum teen taiyaar karo," Ira said flatly. "Main ginti karti hoon."

They drank. Somewhere on the river a lone oar creaked against its pin.

"Ek cheez aur," Ira said at last. "Steel wala case. Maine dekha. Side pe stamped tha."

The top of Veer's head, against the wall, did not move.

"Aur—?" Aadhya said.

"Do shabd. Aage A. Pichhe M." Ira didn't take her eyes off the north steps. "Angrezi ke do words. *Aegis Meridian.*" She spelled both of them, in case the dark had misspelled them in air, and then said the two words aloud a second time, flat, without hatred or fear, in the manner of a notary sealing a document — and the alley, its drains and drains' disagreeable godhood and all, the commercial radio up the lane issuing *Jaanch jaari*, took the two words into its understory with the obedience of all Varanasi.

Rudra closed his eyes. In the dark of them a hairline — half an inch long, on the inside of his sternum, under his collar, where sweat cooled — could not quite be banished.

"Ira," he said.

"Hmm."

"Note kar lena. Kal subah chai pe batana mujhe. Kahan se shuru hota hai, kitne din, sab."

"Kitne din ka, main pehle se jaanti hoon." Iris-thin light from a far lamp-shrine moved across her expressionless face. "Gyarah. Bas gyarah bache hain."

---

At home — a little after one — Rudra stood before the bathroom mirror with his shirt off and the light off, and read his chest with his eyes shut.

With the eyes shut it was clear — clearest, in fact: the body as a country lit from beneath by the road-work of its own power lines, all the wire-geography he had been born with, the dark's accurate survey. Where the light ran it ran to the measure drilled into him since two; where it dampened it held the seventeen-year-old plaster of his parents' impossible marriage of intents: one road at the throat, a ring of heavy gates at the breastbone, and now — hair-fine, the length of half a breadstick, exactly honest as all his father's damage had ever been — a seam.

He put the heel of one wrapped hand to it, and breathed, and counted, and felt his mother's lullaby come up in him on its own — wordless — its eleventh note hanging like one arm raised at the door.

*Main kya kar sakta hoon, Ma?* he had asked her, three nights before — and she had looked down at him with nineteen years standing in her eyes, and said:

*Sab kuch, beta.*

---

One floor below, probably for the last hour, two people who had not slept were pretending they had.

Rukmini sat with her back against the kitchen's doorframe, on the floor, in the posture of a woman on a train platform, the folded towel still over her shoulder like a field scarf. She had been there since the gate's iron voice announced him at one bell — had stood to come up, and sat down again — because she had heard her son go past her door and there were ten things wrong with the walk and she had known them, all ten, knot by methodical knot, the way you count rosary: *left shoulder low; heel-strike soft — favouring the ribs; breath on a count of four he'd set himself because his own wouldn't hold; energy* — and that was where her counting stopped being anything a mother in Varanasi could say aloud.

Up the stairs, on the rooftop under the cold standing garnets of November, Ram sat with his staff across his lap and the night x-ray weather flowering slow over the northeast — where, if the mind's instruments were trusted, the river-town was trending midnight-blue and hungry — and he had been sitting exactly like that, changeless, since eight. He had heard the gate at one. He had heard his son's ten wrong things on the stairs. He had not gone down, because a sentry does not leave a wall and because the particular kind of wrong that was in those footsteps had, once, long ago, been in his own.

"Neeche aa ke so ja," Rukmini called quietly from the terrace door — she had come up after all, with the water lota the worst excuse in the world. "Barah baj gaye."

"Tune suna?" Ram asked the horizon.

She came and stood beside the parapet, near enough to be merciful, far enough to be obedient. "Suna."

"Fight," he said. It wasn't a question. "Ghat pe kuch hua. Ladke ka chalna badal gaya. Ek kadam aisa tha jaisa—" He stopped. For a moment the two of them stood looking at two different counties of the same dark.

"Khud se lad raha tha koi?" Rukmini asked, still quietly, eyes on her own folded fingers — and the question was a hundred-pound wager she placed, offhand, like a loafer's coin at the tea-stall, as if mothers didn't bet their thoraxes on just such turns of phrase.

"Khud se nahi," Ram said — very slow. "Khud ke andar waale se." And then he said nothing more at all, and he kept the staff's weary companionship, and somewhere over the city beyond numberless roofs a cloth-banner hauled over a two-lane intersection — *DASHASHWAMEDH AARTI — COMMEMORATIVE* — shivered in the faint night-offshore as if the words themselves had overheard something through the wall and wanted to be taken down.

She stayed there with him without going any nearer until the watch-feel of the hour hand moved her. She did not ask when it would happen. And he didn't tell her what was said to be coming, and the years of not-telling wore all the preliminary faces of his love.

Outside, the lane dog finished its night-check of the thresholds and climbed onto the newspaper stack outside the shuttered paan kiosk and arranged itself to sleep. Over the river two districts away, the ghat's prayer-smoke roved in the dark — losing its edge, thickening slowly, settling like a closed hand.

---

# CHAPTER 11 — CRACKS

The morning after the fight on the ghats, Rudra got dressed the way a fugitive gets dressed.

The bathroom was dark — the bulb above the mirror had given up sometime before dawn, leaving the small room to a corpse-cold tube over the sink — and he did not reach to switch anything brighter on. Shirt off, he performed the new liturgy in the dark: cotton medical tape, the wide kind his father kept in the storeroom's third drawer for wrists and ankles; six strips, overlapping, laid in a neat band above the heart, flat against the hairline seam that had opened under the skin above his sternum sometime during the last seconds of the previous night.

It did not hurt. That was the thing he could not get anyone to understand, because there was no one to tell it to: the crack in his mother's work did not hurt at all. It *radiated.* Laid his hand flat against the taped band and the warmth that came back up through the palm was the warmth of a clay tandoor photographed from a respectful distance — banked, interested, faintly cozy —

In the corridor, his mother's footsteps passed once — deliberately, noisily, the way she made steps heavy when she wanted occupants of rooms to know they were occupied — and did not stop. Nineteen years of supervised dawn-drills, and she had decided in the making of this morning's dough that today the surveillance would be performed by trust. He heard her go down. He heard, one room over, the storeroom door's bolt — his father's — unrenovated ancient note of it — not slide at all. Nobody anywhere was doing inventory.

That was how he knew they both knew.

He tore off a seventh strip, sealed the ends, and pulled the kurta over the whole business in one movement, checking twice in the mirror the kurta's collar-line, holding the collar's edge away from the throat so he could look down inside, from above, like a man checking a well.

Tuesday. There would be a lecture at eleven. There would be his mother's questioning palms at breakfast. There would be, alone on the porch railing, his father's small hammer, beating out its daily judgement on something.

"Rudraaa—" from the kitchen: the long, three-note call of a house about to feed someone.

"Aaya," he called back, and completed the tape's ragged mummery with a shrug, and went out to be a good person's ordinary son.

---

Breakfast: parathas, left-hand side of a square of old newspaper; the FM transistor declaring the ghatside *jaanch jaari* between two turns of bhajan; his mother, attentive, furiously ordinary, her glance touching his collarbone twice; his father, silent over his own steel plate, so untalking today that the silence took a chair.

"Kal raat hawa tez thi," Ram said finally, without looking.

At the doorway, the rolling pin stopped for one half-second and then exactly resumed.

"Haan," Rudra said. "Aarti ki wajah se shor bhi zyada tha."

"Hmm," said Ram, and folded the newspaper, and said nothing else for the rest of the meal — nothing about the pillar that was, by now, on the front page of the late-edition city supplement under the heading *GHAT PE PURANI IMARAT GIRI, EK GHAYAL* — nothing about the black-shuttering video links that had spent the past two days blinking out of the world like streetlights along a road at switch-off time. Nothing. He ate with the even attention of a man executing something.

It took Rudra until the bus to name what had felt strangest about it: the newspaper had been folded *inward*, its photograph section folded to the inside, by hands that did that every day of the year except today.

The bus did the city's morning reading for him.

It crawled past the numbered series of Varanasi's ordinary Tuesdays — a shuttered cable office; three women in white-at-Assi pilgrimage dress arguing about e-rickshaw change; the municipal van with its loudspeaker asleep in the lap of a clerk — and at every signal the conductor's paper money slapped against the old rolled ticket-spine in his satchel like a small drum-major of currency. At the Lanka crossing the city opened its daily parenthesis of dust, and through the bus window Rudra watched two schoolboys in the standard khaki-half-pants livery of their class act out, in the safe margin of the pavement, the entire ghat fight of the night before last.

"Dekh — *aise* pillar gira tha! Ek ladke ne aise maara tha—" the elder one demonstrated atop a waiting scooter — "aur fit pitthar se phatke bahar aa gaye —"

"Jhooth!" said the younger, worshipfully, both hands over his mouth.

"Sach! Mere mamu ke chacha ke ladke ke dost ne dekha tha—!"

Outside the window, Varanasi rolled on with the whole cargo of its morning — bhajans from a speaker-shop being tested in bursts; peasants' sacks of onions crossing under rope-bridges of wire; a short convoy of saffron prayer-flagged tractors — and inside it, inside his kurta's tape of concealment, the seam above his sternum lifted once against the cotton, enquiring, and was denied. The boys' pillar-myth had already run twenty-eight hours ahead of the paperwork. *This is how it happens,* he thought. *The truth is assigned to your cousin's cousin's friend; the newsprint fact is buried near the achaar recipes.*

"Chal — aarti waale din ka hindi homework!" the conductor said, rapping the pole; and the two schoolboys snapped to; and Rudra looked out over both their heads at a city that worshipped stone and iron and would go on doing so, and did not let himself wonder what else it bowed to when no one was around to invent a cousin.

---

The lecture was in the old gallery-style hall — Heat & Mass Transfer, the third-year core — with its bench-tiers rising to the back and its warm woolen dim: a hall built in an era that chose teak over window-light, with the ceiling fans turning somewhere overhead in valedictory slow motion and the projector's blue-white cone cutting down through the dust-haze to slide after slide of conduction equations.

There is a specific mercy the institution extends to the frightened: timetables. At eleven o'clock every educated boy in that hall settled into a groove that had been worn by fifty consecutive batches of the frightened, slid notes open, abandoned their private load-bearing terrors, and became, for one hour, the audience of conduction-through-composite-walls. Chalk went on with its pale arithmetic. A ceiling fan completed a slow bureaucratic orbit. Someone's tiffin, illicitly opened three rows down, released the suddenly planetary redolence of pickle into the teak-dark air, and fifty noses inhaled at once with total forgiveness — the collective animal function of a class that has agreed to survive thermodynamics together.

Rudra sat mid-tier with his notebook open. He was, as always, a page ahead of the lecturer. This was why lectures were dangerous for him lately. Prepared meant free, and free meant counting, and counting meant listening — listening to the warm little seam under the tape, which today had a pulse like a second small throat.

He was on the count's twenty-fourth loop when the projector flickered.

Only that. A power blip — the cone shuddered, threw the Fourier slide sideways for one glare of over-white, went momentarily cavernous — the hall's two hundred faces lifting from their notebooks into a shared half-second of dark. Two hundred people exhaled their stock "lo" of the power cut, and the tube-lights gate-crashed awake along the wall-fans —

— and in that one borrowed second of dark, before the lights took, Rudra's eyes burned.

Not hurt. Not flash. *Burned.* The coal behind the tape, a bed of ember under its own patient's ash-keeping, rose against the crack like a dog rising in the dark when the door-chain slides — and in him, effortless, as if it had always lived in his own two mirror-lenses, the red thread opened behind his eyes and filled them: no drama, no pain, just — *red-gold*, the colour of the bottom of a lamp-lit well —

He clamped them shut in the same breath. The lights came on. Another boy two benches down said "Fuse gaya kya" to nobody; the lecturer, who had been writing with his back to it, turned around forgivingly to the hall, chalk poised: "Ho gaya—" and went on writing.

Rudra kept his hands over his half-lowered face as if rubbing tiredness away. Time passed like spilled water.

When he opened them slowly and deliberately for vision like a man re-emerging from swimming, nobody was looking at him.

Except Aadhya.

Three benches down, two columns left, in profile to the hall — she was not watching the board; she was watching him, motionless, notebook still open to a clean page with its last line of writing abandoned mid-word, her whole face leached to the colour of bone-white paper: because she'd happened to glance up in the same half-second, by mischance, by sympathy, by the small sixth sense with which she had begun to map his weather — whatever the reason, she had seen it plainly: his eyes, in the dark, burning like two new coals.

She had seen. There was no way she had not.

He gave her the only armour he owned. He looked at her one full second — flat, ruined-casual, openly *satisfied* that he'd caught her staring — and turned his head away forward to the board like a man grandiosely uninterested, scribbling three garbage words on the page to support the act, feeling her not-leaving glance settle on him like five grams of rain.

Both of them spent the remaining forty minutes extremely visibly not writing anything down.

Around them, the hall returned to its covenant of chalk. The lecturer filled in, on the blackboard's rightmost corner, the little boxed inscription students had learned to watch for — *IMP for ESE* — and a hundred and something pens resumed copying the universe's instructions. Veer, three benches back and one tier up, who had attended none of today's class so far and attended precisely none of what followed, had lifted his head once at the flicker and put it back down. By anybody's casual inspection the episode had filtered into hall-lore before the bell even rang: one second of voltage sag, one boy rubbing his eyes, two hundred people permitted forever to pretend no one had seen anything at all.

---

He had the escape lines prepared: he had them before the class bell released the two hundred, he had them by the time his bag was over his shoulder. He had excuses arranged in order of plausibility first-impression — projector glare, hay-fever, piya hua nahi hoon wala joke — like a man stacking ammunition along his steps.

It helped nothing. She fell into pace beside him at the lecture-hall landing like someone who'd taught her legs the whole evacuation plan three corridors up: not running, not hurrying, simply *there.* Which was, in its way, infinitely more terrifying than pursuit.

"Wait," she said.

People were flowing past them down the staircase — the college's standard animal — and in the crowd the two of them stalled outness, occupying a detergent-smelling piece of landing; she lowered her voice by one degree as the bottom stairs thinned. "Tumhare aankhein—"

"Projector," he said immediately — too immediately, the word coming out machined. "Screen se direct seat pe light aayi. Aadhi class ne toh —"

"Class ki nahi, *andar se* thi," she said. Very plainly. No tremor in it at all: that was the frightening part. She was not asking. After all — she had armed herself with the same thing he had: she had spent seventy-two hours since the ghat-fight collecting her own incident-details, cataloguing every impossible thing in Braille. She read his face the way he'd taught others to read the counters of his opponents: by the sound it made under the wrist. "Us light mein tumhari aankhein wahan se *de rahi thi*. Main janti hoon screen reflection kaisi dikhti hai. Ye wali nahi thi."

He had prepared nothing at all for that.

The landing was empty; somewhere on the floor above a door was kicked open, metallic, dismissive. Three seconds went past. Through everything he kept the faint smile with which his childhood's first trainer — his father's repeated cold disappointment — had fitted him, like armour changing its voice twice a minute.

"Toh tumhe eye-checkup," he said, with a long string of patience. "Discount de dunga. Srinivas ke peeche wali lane mein ek doctor hai — naam likh ke de deta hoon."

"Rudra."

"Agar tumhe logon ki aankhon mein time-pass dikh rahi hai toh problem meri nahi hai—"

"Uss din ghat pe," she said. Man to man. "Jab woo pillar gira — uske theek pehle — tumhare *haath* pe bhi yehi tha." She tapped her own sternum, once, gently, the way a botanist touches a known plant. "Main peeche khadi thi. Maine dekha. Wo — bhi — andar se tha."

Under his collar, taped flat, the seam seemed to pulse once, happily, *at her touch from three feet away.* He hated his own body with a quick white clean flare of a hatred he had only ever reserved for the coaches who'd called his father cruel.

"Aadhya." Even; extremely clear. "Meri aankhon mein kuch nahi tha. Mere haath pe kuch nahi tha. Lecture chal raha tha aur main so raha tha. Jab main sota hoon toh puri duniya mujhe miraculous lagti hai — par mujhe chhodo."

"Jhooth," said Aadhya, softly, and did not even make it an accusation. She let it stand as the room's temperature. For a second the two of them regarded one another with the elegant mutual devastation of two negotiators who each know the other's card-count.

The hall went on breathing above and below them.

"Theek hai," she said at last, and stepped back — one step, two — giving him back the layout his face was begging for; her voice fell half a note into something older, a register she hadn't used on him in three years: "Jab tumhe batana ho, tab bata dena. Kal wale — woh log — teen the. Unka koi bhai-bahan toh nahi hoga jo wapas na aaye. *Teen ka matlab mujhe bhi pata hai kya hota hai.* Main—" she changed course with immense private effort, visibly, like a truck changing lanes; smiled, one-sided, ruinously fond — "—bas. Lecture miss kar rahi hoon. Bye."

And she went down the stairs one flight exactly as a friend, and did not turn at the bottom to look up, leaving him alone on the landing with twenty-four counts of breathing and the tape's small tandoor warmth beating up through his kurta like something saying *yes, yes, keep talking like that, harder.*

---
