He found Veer in the canteen's last bench, with his right sleeve rolled to the elbow — attending to nothing at all in particular and doing so immensely, a colossus meditating over one cold samosa and a steel tumbler of chai gone wrinkly with standing.

Rudra sat. Put down his bag. The seam-pulse under his collar matched, by hundreds of metres of air and the arrangement of old brick, the scorch of the exhaust fan above the vendor-back; the college noon went on; nobody was out of the ordinary but themselves. They did not look at each other for forty seconds. Veer turned his fist over once — palm open, palm closed — the split knuckles dark with Mercurochrome.

"Kal raat main soya nahi," Veer said, quietly.

"Hum dono ne," said Rudra.

"Hath jala nahi. Garam tha bas." A terrible awe had entered Veer's voice — a thing looser than fear, attendant to fear the way a junior priest attends on a ceremony. "Jab jal raha tha, us waqt, Rudra — mujhe darr na tha. Gussa nahi tha. Kuch nahi tha. Sirf — ek cheez poochna thi main tumse." He glanced sideways. "Tum pehle se jaante the. *Hum mein kya hai.*"

Rudra said nothing. In the steam off the chai, under the ripped sleeve, the old pyre-burn on Veer's arm — the one from Manikarnika, fractal and glossy — was tinged a very faint tan-gold under the flesh, the way old pottery shows salt where the water dried.

"Achha tum dono ne nahi bataya toh main samajh gaya," Veer said, with the loyalty of the unanswerable. "Par — ek baat. Kal woh jo mujh mein tha — *woh mujhe nahi lagta bahar se aaya hai.* Pehle se pada tha. Katora bhara kar chhupa rakha tha kisi ne. Didi jab school mein pit-ti thi mere liye ooncha sada hathiyar uthane par—" he finally tried a grin; it fitted him like dog-tags; "—tabhi se shayad." He flexed the fingers open-shut, once, the size of small garlanded idols. "Par kal — pillar ke age main *roka nahi jaa raha tha*. Samajh? Kuch naye aaye the aur unka hisaab se nahi, galti se pillaron ke darmayan tha. Mujh mein aur koi — *hai*, Rudra. Tum dono — hum chaaron — ke paas kuch hai. Aur tumhein kya karna hai usse? Baithne do ya nikalne do?"

It took Rudra a while. He looked at his own wrapped knuckles. He heard, through four closed boxes of ribs and tape, the coal saying *out, out, out.* He thought of his father fitting his forearms with the corrected bone-bruise of ten thousand mornings, and of his mother's voice singing *children like you*, and of Aadhya standing three feet downstairs forty minutes ago reading his glare like a map.

"Baithne do," he said. "Abhi. Jab tak samajh na aaye kya hai. Baithne do."

Veer nodded, slow and sad, the nod of a man whose mother had told him he was God's particular weapon and who had always suspected otherwise but never wanted it proven.

"Sasta nahi hoga," he said, at length. "Baithne dena cheezon ko."

"Kabhi sasta hua hai?"

"Pata nahi. Main toh sasta hi khareedta hoon sab." The grin came back then, his own, taking the bench with him into motion; he stood, stretched to a size the canteen's plastic chairs sincerely doubted, and saluted the canteen-boy for the bill. On his way past, cotton-sleeved shoulder an inch from Rudra's collarbone, he stopped.

"Ek kaam aur. Tune tape lagaya hai gale ke neeche — dikh raha hai. Mat karo yeh. Dudh ka doodh karegi Ammu. Aur woh badi wali —" Veer lowered his voice, all veneration, "—dhyaan rakhti hai. Chhodna mat."

---

The second confrontation of the day happened at the notice-boards near the girls' common room, administered by Ira, method one.

"Baki sab jhooth bol rahe hain toh tumse expect karti hoon kam se kam *informed* jhooth," she said, simply, and handed him a ruled sheet torn from a bound register. "Movie-wali baat nahi hai. Saansein, pulse, temperature, hum dono ke haath ke daag, woh pillar, wo telescope-wala coil — meri ek theory hai. Ghar pe padh lena." He glanced at the paper; beneath its blank top half, the drawings — tiny, diagonal, unmistakable — of the cup-and-nodes chart from beside her bed, made from memory in double-battery light. At the bottom, in her surd's handwriting: *11 days till zero. Tumse door kuch nahi hoga. Samet lo.*

"Itni si baat sach bol do toh yeh —"

"Na," she said gently, already turning away. "Sach de dene se toh tum dono uso banaate ho jo ho. Main uske liye taiyaar nahi hoon abhi." A corridor-moment; she halted, did not turn around. "Woh ladki Aadhya sahi keh rahi hai. Batana tumhe. Par tum dono ke saamne ek naya asli aadmi khada karna zaroori hai pehle — jo *raaz nahi* ho. Mummy baba nahi. Koi aisa jo bole agle hafte ki lottery mein tum sab ya tum sab nahi hote. Uske baad tum khud ke bhi malik banoge."

And she nodded once — to herself, probably — and left him among the notice-board staples, beside a climbing-club poster, a lost-ID-card plea, and a Sanskrit-seminar schedule curling like birch-bark, off all of which his face — whole, untorn, his mother's tap-tap joinery holding at rumour-grade — waited silently to be noticed by the world.

---

That night he did not go to the ghat. He did not go to the roof. He ate two rotis less than usual, informed his parents of an assignment of ampleness, and went up to his room and locked the door and waited until the house conducted its nightly descent into stillness.

Only then did he move the lamp.

In the dark — the corridor bulb having one of its flickering centuries again — he undressed to the waist, carried his study lamp to the wall's long mirror, angled its small hard gaze across the glass, and stood before himself. Shirt off. Tape off — peeling slowly, adhesive refusing, detaching in protesting small increments, until it came away with the last of the medical smell.

He looked.

On his chest, over the sternum's keel, the hairline was no longer a hairline. Over the last thirty-six hours it had unpacked itself, gently, into a geometry: a ring of small angular fractures — a small mandala of hair-thin **lines**, laid out in symmetrical spokes across his breastbone, something deliberate and pre-structured and diagrammatic, the way a tile cracks along any pattern of glaze-stress it was *kilned* with. His mother's nineteen-year-old joinery — he knew that now with a certainty that arrived voicelessly in the stomach, the way the names of old wounds arrive — had never been spread over him like a blanket but built over him like architecture, and the architecture was coming down *along the blueprint, beam by named beam*, a temple whose dome was about to know sky.

And in each thin seam: a fine gold-red line. Open like a kiln door. Looking out.

His eyes caught his own attention in the mirror — where the eyes of most boys meet them with the flat human soft-brown of all childhood, these were still burning red-gold — *still*, ten hours after the lecture hall — a deep, steadily banked, majestic ember that did not go out no matter how many counts he ran, and the face above the burning looked utterly, pathetically afraid of itself —

—and he saw it.

And he did the bravest, and the most terrible thing of all: he stood there and looked at himself. Two hands' worth of breaths went by. In the un-astonished dark, the reflection faced him down with the first and last teacher's patience of true disaster: there was no other side of this; there would be no day the other side of this; it would happen or it would be described, and in either case it was first, last and always his.

"Hello," Rudra said at last, quietly — to the construction — in what he did not know was the way exactly his father used to address silent weapons in storage.

It did not answer. It was not there to chatter. It was not there because someone had hidden it in him; it was there because it was *him* — vaulted, bricked-in, re-floored, re-painted, nineteen years deep in masonry — and it was merely coming back to the surface, where it had never not been.

He pressed his forehead to the glass and breathed — *in, four; hold, four; out, four; hold, four* — and behind his lids, deep in the architecture of the chest, the seam flexed like a door testing its hinges, twice, inquiry, patience — *not yet.*

He thought of Veer, three roofs away probably, lying wide awake with his arm outside the blanket, terror-smiling at the memory of how *good* it had felt. He thought of Ira feeding tomorrow's alarm-gates into her machines across town with the dry tenacity of someone keeping time for a universe that didn't ask. He thought of Aadhya on the landing — *"teen the; unka koi bhai-bahan toh nahi hoga jo wapas na aaye"* — how she'd fooled the whole landing conversation into being about lectures and had in fact been delivering, softly, the obituary of his version of events; how she had stood and smelled of the banyan leaf-mould even now, three nights later, like anyone who has brought back too much of a shrine on their skin.

He owed all three of them something; the exact denomination had not yet been decided.

In the morning he would tell none of them. He would bandage and joke and run with one-tenth acceleration in lane jog that nobody could measure, he would let his mother tap his pulse without changing his own, keep his head above his own assurances, and he would, absolutely, go to the mela if his mother later ordered one into existence — because it was exactly because there was no distant repairable frame anymore that the arithmetic of concealment was suddenly boring, like a game everyone had played as a smaller child.

But that was morning. Tonight, now, glass to forehead, he permitted himself the other kind of honesty available to a nineteen-year-old, the out-loud variety:

"Darr lag raha hai," he told the seam — first person, singular, present tense. "Theek hai? Bol diya. Darr lag raha hai." He breathed. The fog bloomed and shrank. "Ab tum bhi bolo."

The seam did not answer. But it banked, very slightly, like a dog that hears its name and — correctly, per contract — does not get up.

His mother hummed two rooms away, very faintly, a tune with words no one wrote down.

The tape lay on the floor where it had finally desisted. Rudra switched off the lamp, and stood in the red-edged dark and looked at his own eyes until he could no longer see them, and then longer than that, and then he dressed and put on the smile behind which he had spent the day, so that no one tomorrow would have to know what the house of his body was doing behind its locked, ordinary door.

---

# CHAPTER 12 — THE INTERROGATION

It came for Rudra at dawn, the way everything in this house came: through the courtyard, on a count.

He had slept in two ransacked fragments — the first till midnight, then nothing, then a leaden too-late second sleep from which his father's voice removed him at five forty-five with the traditional economy of the garrison: not a call, only the sound below of the staff being taken down off its pegs, the ironwood haft ringing once against the courtyard flagstone as it settled into waiting hands.

He dressed. He taped. He went down, wrapped knuckles hidden up his chewed sleeves, heart already on mark.

The sky over the lane was the colour of skimmed milk. In the courtyard Ram stood with his back to the neem, the staff upright beside him like a visiting pandit, and he watched Rudra come down the steps the way clocks watch men approach them, all the while doing one unprecedented thing: nothing at all. No warm-up. No walking of the courtyard's perimeter. No inventory. Standing. Waiting.

Inside, the kitchen's single diya still burned from earlier — stood and lit at five by hands that needed the fire to exist for the rituals to have somewhere to be returned to. Rukmini had spent those forty minutes before dawn not cooking. She had stood at the window with her eyes shut and her thumbs together and re-laid the boy's joinery, knot by knot, knowing that she was caulking a ship with the sea aboard already: eight fractures where yesterday's count had been cheating at four, one of them long as a finger now, shaped like nothing she could un-see — the crack had forked; cracks fork when they have discovered the building's original fault-lines. She had laid down position twenty-eight four more times and had the same snag-thread feeling, worse, and around position thirty-three — where the old weave *looped* eastward through the heart — she discovered that the seam there had *already chosen its first re-route*: the light inside her son was finding new borders to the east while she still laboured at the lost old ones —

She had finished anyway. Masons finish.

Now, with the pressure-cooker's first amicable hiss answering the morning below her like an all-clear, she washed her hands with all sixteen motions and dried them on the towel from last night — folds memorized, silent as bread — and went to stand at the inner door's curtain, two rooms from the courtyard, a wall away from her whole remaining life. She did not trust herself to go outside. She did not trust herself not to.

"Staff nahi chahiye aaj," came Ram's voice, flattened, beyond the wall. "Haath se karenge. Paanch minute mein shuru."

She put her forehead against the cool of the doorframe and listened, unmoving, proprietor and hostage of the same house.

So there was to be no session at all. There was to be a *fight.* In the courtyard, Rudra's throat went dry with a dryness older than yesterday; he said "Theek hai" softly, set his feet, and got up onto the balls of them.

They began, as all father-son wars begin, without a whistle — and with every instrument in the house, every diya and tap and pressure-valve, standing witness at attention, inventorying the noise of it into the family's permanent, private accounts.

---

It was not a beating. Everyone will tell you those are different and everyone is wrong: in the courtyard of a silent man, abuse and education wear the same leather gloves. The truth was that it began properly — drill-proper, a spar like the aarti began per schedule — and it disintegrated.

Before it disintegrated it was, for nearly eleven minutes, the most complete conversation they had ever had.

Sparring is the family's real dining table, and this morning they ate enormous helpings. Ram opened with the old counting-gate drills: palm-heel probes that demanded the lota-parry and got it, twice, crisp, earlier than the beat — the boy was *ahead*, today of all days, ahead of tempo the way a hundred-metre man slightly beats the gun — and Ram escalated by increments he no longer permitted himself to call cruelty: inside-reap, hip-turn, both hooks, then the close-range shiver-work his son had never weathered full-strength, and Rudra absorbed three clean bursts without breaking the count, breath low, eyes wide — wicked with discovery — and for two exchanged seconds the courtyard was what it had dedicated two decades of mornings to: two adepts, speaking perfectly in a tongue made entirely of their hands, the son finally marginally quicker than the father and the father finding, for the first time, that he could not catch up—

It disintegrated at eleven minutes, on a low inside leg-block of Ram's that came in harder than the day's protocol — pressed Rudra's forearms down and inward with the weight of thirty years of withheld mornings — and Rudra, answering, sheeted one elbow up across the opening: textbook; and on the passing of elbow past his father's temple the two men froze, because in that passing the air *changed quality around Rudra's elbow* — seasoned suddenly, gaspingly warm, like a bakery door had opened inside the courtyard at shoulder height — and a thin thread of gold-red came drifting off the boy's forearm-burn scars — three fading seams — into the morning.

They both saw it. The thread unspooled a hand's length, wound around nothing, and folded back into the skin.

"Ruko," said Ram.

Rudra stopped. That had always been the contract; the rule of every drill since the broken-stone runs was that a command halts a body the way a brake halts a cart: instantly, totally, on faith. Rudra stood with his back half-turned and his raised elbow getting heavier in the air, and for the first time in his life he did not want to turn around. And then he did turn around, because he was his mother's son too, all the way down.

Ram was standing perfectly still at the courtyard center. His chest moved as chests move after the word "archery," and not otherwise.

"Yeh kal bhi tha," he said.

It came out of him flat as a nail being indexed. Not a question.

Rudra considered, for the remainder of one breath, the armory of his prepared lies — *dhund; bulb; tea-stall ka photoflash; lecture mein sab dekh rahe the* — and his mouth, which had never once lied successfully inside this quadrangle, voted by absence.

"Haan," he said.

"Usse pehle bhi." Again flat. He had it all sequenced. Rudra could see the inventory: Ram had arrived at the interrogation with the account-books already balanced —

"Ghat pe?" said the father. "Veer ke saath?" — the little drop-pause of arithmetic — "…pillar?"

Rudra did not answer by nodding or by lying. He breathed, once, deeply. So Ram read to him, in a low voice, the charge-sheet of a man who had spent the night not sleeping:

"Raat ko main gaya tha. Do baje. Dashashwamedh." Flat — no theatre in it, which made it worse — all theatre belongs to people still hoping to be believed; Ram had abandoned even the wanting of belief. "Khambha dekha maine. Chunar ka. Aisa pathar jo hamare purane ghaton ke neeche dabbe-nakshon ka kaam kar sakta hai, baarish mein bhadega nahi, chirag se bharega nahi — *aisa.* Toota woh pillar stone-dust mein nahi toota, Rudra — *slice* hua. Chopper se todti hui cheez ki tarah. Aur neeche, teen kadam peeche, ek nishaan tha. Mitthaa hua pair ka nishaan nahi — jo cheez uthti hai toh wo dhool ko seedha khol deti hai — main woh nishaan jaanta hoon. Chaalis saal pehle —" he caught the sentence with his teeth, re-sentenced it: "—main jaanta hoon. Jhooth mat bolna ki tum ladke bheed mein the aur patthar gir gaya. Pillar. Chunar. *Slice*. Aur tum dono ke chehre pe subah — jitna maaza aata hai, utna *sannata.*"

The syllables fell, even and laid, like dressed stones. Long after, it was the total lack of raised voice that Rudra found impossible to forget. His father stood there without his staff — an old turtled paleness rising from under the collar of his vest — and read the city aloud like a walking ancestors' stone.

"Kal ka akhbaar," Ram said, "maine andar moda tha subah. Usi mein photo tha ghat ka. Tumhare peeche ek cheez hai Rudra jo tumne aaj subah apni balaiyon se jo chahe tape barrel kar li ho — mere andar bhi boli aayi hai wo. Maine saari umar us cheez ko ghar se bahar rakha; tum usse — *kheench* rahe ho, apni or, jaise kanjar-kushti mein kunda." His voice dropped a last rung. "Kisine kaha tha mujhse — woh jo ghat pe thi tumhare saath, ladki, lambe baalon wali — usne jo kuch bhi lagaya haath, woh *seedha tumhe dhoond rahi thi*. Coil. Cheeni-miiti light ki. Aaye hain Rudra. Aa chuke hain. Aur tumhe — unki light mein — tum — mera beta — ko *jala hua* paya jaata hai."

He waited then. His chest moved once. And Rudra — the son, the defendant, the boy of the broken-stone years — whose whole front had been assembled on the shelf of *one kal-ki-ladai karne wala beta* — understood, suddenly and without pride or shame, that the man before him was not angry: he was *petrified*, and had been petrified for nineteen years, and had learned to hold the posture of stone so constantly that the stone had begun passing for the man.

Rudra breathed, once, deeply, and the seam under his tape answered the morning's fine cool light with a glow restrained to below the visible by one strip of skin; he waited.

Only in the small of his back, dripping down the spine, came an understanding of how long this conversation had been waiting for both of them.

"Do din," said Ram at last, the iron voice gone into something more broken than anger — something *procedural*, the tone of a mahant reading out the list of maintenance-works for a condemned temple — "do din se is ghar ki deewarein bata rahi hain. Do din. Main apne kamre mein let kar *sunte* aa raha hoon. Ek maa apne bete ke andar ki cheez ko roz seedha karti hai, subah chaubis ghante, aur ek baap apne kamre se ginta hai ki aaj ki phati hui lakeerein *kal se do zyada hain.*" He stopped. His jaw set into the continuity of a man who realized he was already saying too much and would therefore say all of it. "Ungliyon pe gin sakta hoon, Rudra. Kavach ki phati lakeerein. Kal subah chaar thi. Aaj subah *saat* hain."

The word landed in the courtyard like a staff on stone: *kavach.* Rudra had never in his life heard either of them say the word. They had needed forty names for nineteen years of silence and they had never needed one, and now his father, standing alone in the rosy-flecked dawn without his staff, let the word out of the bag without asking permission from nineteen years of policy.

"I don't know what that is," Rudra said, in English, not hostile but suspicious, out of words, child-quickness, already apologizing to the morning for what he was about to say next—

"Jhooth," said Ram.

"—haan theek hai, jhooth, *jaanta hoon main kya hai* —" and the nineteen years that followed had scratches and were dry and unlubricated: "—jaanta hoon ki andar kuch hai kyun ki ROZ subah yahan khada hoke usi ko dabaane ki training ho rahi hai, aur main *pooch bhi nahi sakta* kyunki yeh ghar pe poochne pe rishtey tootte hain, jawab pe nahin!"

The wall-lizard, overhead between the living-room rafters, stopped ticking.

"Main jawab de raha hoon," said Ram — quiet, cyclonic. "Poore jawab ke liye tumhe pehle woh cheez bujhani padegi jo tum log gaye khinch kar laaye ho. Ghat ke peeche. Aadhi raat. Do naukri-walon ki jeep ke saamne." His eyes left his own thought and simply, entirely, terrible in their precision, fastened on his son: "Tum jaante ho un logon ko? Laal topi nahi lagi thi unko, Rudra. Laal topi nahi lagi thi *kyunki unhone tumhe dekh rakha tha.*"

"Appa—"

"Fifteen years," his father said, and all the grey mechanics in his voice broke loose and bared the raw steel underneath. "Main tumhare saath kya nahi kiya? Main tumse dosti kar sakta tha. Main tumhe akele chhod sakta tha. Maine tumhari hadiyan todi hain is aangan ke patharon pe, roz, subah, mazboot wali dubaara — not because I enjoyed it—" —he stopped, re-registered the past tense he had admitted —

"I didn't break your bones for fifteen years so you could let this power eat you alive."

The courtyard breathed.

"Maybe I didn't ask you to break my bones."

It was as if the sentence had made the sun come up while all these years the sun had been someone else's rumour.

Ram's face crumbled.

It crumbled exactly the way things in him would crumble — strictly privately, deep in the load-bearing rooms — with one tremor passing through the lower jaw and eyes unchanged, the face of a man being handed back his own entire curriculum as a bill his son was refusing to ever pay, and it lasted one second, one entire second that Rudra would replay for years without being able to remain present for it during the replays —

And then it hardened.

Set. *Re-sealed* — the same inner masonry his wife performed with thumbs and counted knots, executed in one stiff muscular summary. The jaw-set acquired its twenty-year policy. The eyes, two dim furnaces too far gone for mere anger, came up, and stepped back away from him:

"Toh phir samajh, Rudra. Agar todna tumhara faisla nahi tha — toh *bachana* bhi tumhara faisla nahi. Jab tak tum mere ghar mein ho, tum iss cheez ko khule mein nahin nikaloge. Din mein. Raat mein. College mein. Ghat pe. **Nahin.** Us pillar ke saamne, apne hi doston ke saamne, police ke saamne, un teen aadmiyon ke saamne — kabhi nahi. Mere saamne nahi. Apni maa ke saamne *bhi nahi.*" His voice did not rise. It *lowered*, which was infinitely worse. "Kyunki ye jo cheez hai — ise dekhne wale aayenge — ye vardan nahin hai, beta." He stopped carefully, the way he stopped his staff before striking. "Ye dhabba hai. Dhabba. Tujhe pata nahi kis waade ki jageer hai tu. Main jaanta hoon. Aur jeete ji, zamaane ko pata nahin lagne dunga."

"Dhabba," Rudra repeated, level, bloodless. "Keh rahe ho — mujhpe dhabba —"

And that was when the courtyard's back door opened.

---

Rukmini had not watched the whole thing. Years later, assembled in a room of sons-in-law Rudra would claim that she'd heard it all through two closed doors; and only she, who knew that the house's walls had been enlisted into service years before the boys could spell, would narrow her eyes listening to that boast. She arrived, in any case, precisely at the crest — the curve of the fight's unobtainable apex — with an expression on her face that Rudra had never seen on it: the expression of a mason who turns a corridor corner to find both her husband and her son standing *inside* the wall she has been quietly taking down at dawn for nineteen years, their hands black with mortar.

In one hand she held a steel bowl of breakfast — halwa someone's condolence-sweet, halwa from Mr. Srinivas' stall delivered nineteen minutes ago. She set it on the courtyard's washing-stone with a sound of metal settling.

The men of her house did not look at each other. Two silhouettes with arms hanging empty with the spent ferocious knowledge of what an unfolded night in the world did to the morning.

"Chalo," she said. Not *don't fight.* Not *enough.* Just: *chalo.*

Neither of them moved.

"Rudra." She turned halfway to the courtyard tap and said, over her shoulder, at the courtyard wall, in the easy everyday machinery voice: "Upar jaa. Nahale. Tere notes cushion ke andar hain, maine theek kar diye."

He stood looking at her. Then at his father's back — the incomprehensible changed country of it, the north of his father he'd been born too late to emigrate to. Then he walked past — tapped his father's arm very briefly, two fingers, neither contact nor absence, his mother's finger-tap — and went in, and up.

Mother and son heard, ten seconds later, the water going on behind the closed bathroom door, high and cold and deliberate.

***

Rukmini took the water lota off the tap. Turned it down. Filled the brass bowl for the birds. When her hands were done tidying they went to the small of her back and stayed there, like hands standing outside a dispensary.

"Uske saamne," she said, at last, with the tender uninflected flatness in which this household kept its Holy Articles, "kabhi phir se yeh mat kehna. Dhabba-wala."

Ram did not face her. He faced the courtyard-wall, or the lane beyond it, or something beyond the lane whose headcount was rising every night, at eleven, across the city's ringed drains. He said: "Sach hai."

"Sach kehne ke tarike hote hain." She poured. The water said its small hallmarked song onto old brick. "Pandrah saal tu ise todh-sikha raha tha. Ye bachcha tumse aaj tak ladta nahi aaya — poochta rahaa. Aur tum dono ab jhooth bolte ho ek dusre se, aur dono ko bilkul theek pata hai." She looked up. She was not crying; a manufacturer of tears does not have the budget. She was taking inventory, arranging his face along the dawn, memorizing which regret had stayed and which had emigrated. "Jaante ho — jab kisi ko mitti lagti hai na, toh mitti zyada dikhayi deti hai."

When he did not speak — when it was quite clear that all the fortifications of his day were sand-construction — she acquitted him of the helplessness by changing the subject to the only one she had left:

"Mela aa raha hai." Pause. "Dev Deepawali. Teen din. Nau ghanton wala." She came up beside him with the empty brass bowl under her arm like a commander's folded paraphernalia. "Usse jaane do, Ram. Doston ke saath. Jhoomne." She paused, organized her voice to the weight of an ultimatum written on the back of a ration card. "Is ghar mein ek din bhi aisa hona chahiye jisse usse yaad rahe ki is andar kuch hone ke siva bhi zindagi hai. Tumhe nahi pata. Tumhara kaam hi alag hai. Lekin *main jaanti hoon bharosa kaisa dikhta hai — aur abhi usko humse bharosa uth raha hai, beta hote hote isse ghar ho raha hai. Aaj hi.*"

He looked away first. That was his concession; that was his surrender; in twenty years she had earned, perhaps, four of those.

"Peon," Ram said to the doorpost, sourest ancient joke, name of the boy's first coach, the name by which he had called his son from four. His whole back vibrated once.

---

It wasn't the water that answered Rudra upstairs. It was the door.

She came in with a dry kurta folded over her wrist and the household towel over her shoulder and did not knock — this was the house's one room where knocking had always been considered a foreign luxury, a boy's bathroom being, in the taxonomy of Indian mothers, roughly equivalent to a verandah. She set the kurta on his bed. She did not flinch from the steam curling off his chest, or ask whether the steam was his. She stood inside his door with her folded hands and looked at him — whole, tall as her now — for a moment that belonged to twenty years ago and to the moment after this one with equal injustice.

"Tape hata do," she said. "Geela ho jayega. Aaun?" — an old joke from teething months; the geezer-rope humour of babyhood.

"Ma."

"Kab tak chhupayega?" She made a small business of folding the bed's coverlet edge straight. "Meri taraf se lad. Baap ki taraf se mat lad. Ek hi kaafi tha is ghar ke liye." The words came out of her it seemed from very far away: from the old country of wives' kitchen-collisions, where women learn that the way to make two angry items not break the kitchen is to place oneself exactly, physically, between the stove and the shelf. "Woh jo usne kaha — dhabba — bhool dena. Uske muh se nikal gaya. Uske *andar* nahin nikla." A pause, long and straight. "Tu uska dhabba nahin hai, Rudra. Tu uska nirnay hai. Log —" and she did a thing with her chin, a small brutal shake — "log waade nibhaane ke liye ladakpan mein bahut chhoti si hadiyan todkar bade karte hain dusron ki. Tumhe samajh aayega. Kabhi."

"Kab?" Rudra said. His voice had found the tube from the age-five courtyard with no address change. "Kab samajh aayega, Ma? Kal? College ke baad? Shaadi ke baad? Jab woh log mujhe *le jaane* aayenge?"

The seam under his sternum, deep and golden and watching, answered her before she could: it stood out in the steaming skin like one slender line of script, and she looked at it, and he looked down at it, and in their shared look was the entire walled history of the house: the morning she had placed him like a raisin into the knotted architecture; the door she'd made inside a door she'd made; the small mason-mother agreements which had kept the roof on —

"Jab main aur tera baap na bach paayenge," she said. "Tab nahi bataungi. *Usse pehle.*" A stone moved into place somewhere under the sentence; she said it the way towers are talked about — *when we can no longer protect you; before that.* "Abhi nahi. Beta. *Abhi* aaja. Pooja-ghar ke diye mein tel khatam ho raha hai — bhar de."

And she went back down the stairs, taking her old shoulder-towel and her nineteen years with her, and Rudra stood in the steam of the opened bathroom door with the dry kurta over one hand and discovered that he did not, in fact, know what any of the four inhabitants of the house allowed themselves for lunch.

---

After that, behind the closed bathroom door, under the high cold water, Rudra stood with two palms plastered against the tiles, head bent — letting it run down his whole history, the nineteen years of him, the one day of him that had just unfolded in the dirt below. Cold water found the taped seam of his chest and stood aside from it like a crowd from heat; the steam rose off the red-gold seams of him in a fine, private plume, in the way a lamp is said to bow without extinguishing when it prays.

*Dhabba,* he thought. *I'm a mark that belongs somewhere against someone's debt. The house knew. It knew before I did; the house was built around it; my whole name is an accommodation the world made to this thing and then fooled me with.*

He stayed there till the water went cold all the way around. Then he made one strong unshaking motion with a wrapped hand and stood straight, chest to the merciless tile, and let, at last, one short English sentence — the most expensive kind — climb out of him through the water-noise, spoken southward, spoken like an unofficial pact with himself:

"Enough hiding."

---

In the small back storeroom off the dining passage, where the house kept its entire unproud history — sky-blue paint flaking off the inside ribs, an almirah older than the marriage, two steel trunks with their painted initials whiteworked by decades of ghee-smoke — his father stood alone with a table between his hands like a parapet.

On the table, the oiled cloth bundle lay where he had set it a long forearm's worth of him ago.

He did not open it. He crossed the room and came back, twice. Outside, the lane's municipal radio-bicycle went past playing someone's recorded Aarti tone-deaf and off-schedule, and in the yard behind his head Rukmini mixed the morning fresh flour for the boy's eleven o'clock, and the house protected none of them any longer from what it had always known; and he stood there above the oilcloth — the old herringbone wrapping, the sailor's hitch his fingers had tied and un-tied and tied for years now like the instruments of an honest penance — and he let his palm rest on it, once, flat, the way a dying man's hand finally blesses a forehead.

"Baad mein," Ram muttered to the bundle. Voice of a general forcing the front to hold by pure failure to accept its collapse. "Abhi nahi. Abhi — dekhna hai mujhe, bache hue dinon mein, kya kya tootta hai — aur kya kya — *ruk jaata hai*."

He retied the hitch with shaking, deliberate fingers: reef knot; fisherman; then the knot he had learned from a dead teacher thirty years ago in a city that did not have this one's name — the knot with the wrong little loop, the one only three arrested histories in the world could have taught him.

Then he turned out the small lamp-oil of the storeroom's dusk and went back out into the house, to stand.

The neem above the courtyard leaned against the house listening with its hundred bittersweet leaves, disapproving, furious, afraid — for even a tree knew better than to give a father permission to cry, and knew with the same certainty that under the house, forty feet down, in every direction at once, a great patient cup was being filled.

The day was required to continue. It did.

---

# CHAPTER 13 — THE MELA

His mother issued the orders at breakfast, in the form of a statement about other people's children.

"Sharma ji ke ladke log mela dekhne ja rahe hain raat ko," she said, sliding the third paratha onto his plate with the inexorable authority of a state granting citizenship. "Dev Deepawali pe. Itna bada din hai aur mera beta ghar mein baithega toh padosi log kahenge kuch baat hai."

"Kaunsi baat?" Rudra said, mouth already half-betrayed by paratha.

"Yehi — ki kuch baat hai." She turned back to the stove. "Veer ko bhi bol dena. Woh pilla panch-din se apni maa ke saamne bhi tape laga ke ghoom raha hai."

From the dining end of the table, behind the newspaper (today folded normally — a diplomatic signal whose significance was not lost on either of them), his father made no comment at all. Rudra, who had learned at six years of age to read this household like a field officer reads weather, interpreted: *permission, granted by one government, unopposed by the other.* Dev Deepawali night. Three nights hence. The ghats would be a single unbroken necklace of fire from Rajghat to Assi, which was a thing that happened every year and felt, this year, less like a festival and more like the city arguing its own case to the sky.

"Mela hum log saath mein dekh ke aayenge," said Rukmini, to the stove, the room, and the sky. "Usi din."

---

---

Dev Deepawali on the ghats of Varanasi is not a festival; it is an argument that the city makes, once a year, with the dark.

The argument goes like this: that fire, when it is small enough, multiplies. That a single brass diya holding one tablespoon of ghee and one cotton wick is nothing; that a hundred of them are a temple; that five hundred thousand of them, laid step by step along eighty-eight ghats in the keeping of volunteers and priests and boatmen and chai-wallas and grandmothers and children and thieves, become a shore of gold fifteen kilometres long — become, from the river's side, from mid-current where the tourist boats rock at their anchors, a second city that burns alongside the first, a Varanasi of smokeless flame laid down for one night only, for the gods' own stairway down to the river on the night of the full moon of Kartik.

They had arrived, by custom, by the lanes' oldest choreography: e-rickshaw to the point where wheels give up, then the walk — past the pooja-thali sellers triple-shifting, past the cane-furniture godown woken out of a decade to rent its frontage to a toy importer, past the municipal loudspeaker broadcasting *savdhaani* to no one available — and out at Assi's slope where the temporary fairground had assembled itself over the week like a travelling republic: the giant wheel turning against the charcoal sky in a spine of naked bulbs; the wooden drum of *maut-ka-kuan* exhaling dust and engine-scream every forty seconds as two young men orbited the horizontal world on second-owner motorcycles; the toy street with its year's trove of inflatable spacemen and paper horns; mehendi women along the mats, patient as monks, drawing onto new girls' palms the same paisley drawn at this spot for decades. A thousand smells conducted themselves without any sense of municipal authority: roasting corn, diesel, cheap attars, gunpowder praline, hot sugar breaking.

Veer acquired the pink bear balloon approximately here. Documented later as costing forty rupees, defended by him as *free complementary* with the purchase of roasted peanuts by means no one could reconstruct, it rode above them the rest of the evening like the expedition's own small blimp.

"Log dekh rahe hain," Aadhya informed him, once, accurately.

"Toh dekhen," said Veer, with the total inner peace of the innocent. "Mela mein sab apna level dikhate hain."

The four of them stood at Assi's top step at six-fifteen and looked north along that shore of gold, and nobody said anything for eleven seconds, which — for these four — was a historical epoch.

"Aath lakh diye," Ira said at last, very softly, giving it the dignity of data. "Kam se kam. Poore sheher ne aaj raat ke liye apni poori sena bitha di hai."

"Grammar book mein likh lena," said Aadhya, laughing, and hooked her arm through Ira's elbow — a motion so unearned and so specific to women's friendships that it briefly startled the years' worth of arm's-length vigilance out of both of them. "Chal. Jalebi pehle. Phir jaake dekhenge kya disaster tum log plan karke aaye ho."

---

The jalebi stand was old, and holy, and its holiness resided in a vat of bubbling ghee wide enough to bathe a small deity, tended by a man whose family had fried batter on that same corner since before the corporation had painted the corner yellow. He fried in the dark between two electric bulbs, with the fat light coming up off the ghee and illuminating his face from beneath like a portrait of contented damnation, and the queue bent for thirty meters.

"Moti jalebi," Rudra said, at the counter finally. "Ek plate. Sirf moti wali."

"Patli," said Aadhya, immediately, from beside him, without consultation.

"Tu tasteless hai," Rudra said. "Patli jalebi pehli nazar mein jalebi lagti hai aur khaane pe plastic. Moti jalebi ke andar syrup ki gaanth banti hai — *banti hai*, na ki baith jaati hai—"

"Logo, logo," Aadhya announced to thirty metres of queue, one arm out like a traffic constable, "isse dekho. Ye jalebi ke andar gaanth ki baat kar raha hai jaise iska ghar isi mein ho. Patli wali crisp hoti hai, ek dum kadak — moti wali sirf soft aur budhiya hoti hai —"

"—aur yahi donut-wali soch ne is desh ko aaj tak pahunchaya hai!" Rudra informed the queue, which had begun, in the ancient democratic manner of all Indian queues, to weigh in from both flanks — an old man condemning patli as "instant", a bride in new sneakers explaining to her husband that the difference was the *ghee-to-chashni ratio and that he would never understand anything in his life*, and a small boy on his father's shoulders, inexplicably and passionately, chanting *moti! moti! moti!*.

The jalebi-wala, beaming, listening to his life's philosophy refought for free in front of his own vat, fixed it: "Aadha-aadha," he decreed, and made two cones from newspaper and tore the difference clean. He would not accept money for the theory; this was his offering to the evening.

They ate the compromise at the ghat's edge, in the forecourt of the fire, while the loudspeakers somewhere on the main Dashashwamedh platform played old film bhajans between announcements and the river received its diyas from a hundred hands every second — boat-men launching the little offerings from mid-current, children lowering them from the steps with dazed liturgical care, the whole mirrored ghatscape swimming with small lights sailing south like a migration of benevolent insects.

"Dono achhi hai, tum dono bakwaas log ho," Veer announced through his mouth and both elbows, four cones' worth of judgment already gone in his cheeks.

The giant wheel took them next, because from it, Ira announced to nobody's surprise, one could see the drift-patterns of the whole crowd. They loaded in pairs — the natural mathematics of it executing itself without any of the four acknowledging there had been mathematics: Veer and Ira ahead, gondola swinging under his registered mass like a coin-purse on a nail; Rudra and Aadhya behind, entering to the patient's attendant lurch of ferrous furniture, sitting, the bar coming down.

Up they went. Varanasi arrange itself again below them — first ghats banked in gold, then the gilded roofs, then the overhead ornamentation of wires adorning the colony lanes, and last, as they hung at the top, the whole view: the town like an instrument case of jewels stirred by a black river. Aadhya forgot about height-fear because she'd never had a chance to cultivate one. She put both hands on the ridiculous safety bar and said, in a voice from before there were evenings like this:

"Yaar."

Rudra said nothing, in a manner that meant *haan, dekha maine bhi* — and that was the entire conversation until they went over the top and came down the far side, at which point she said, laughing into the wind's mouth, "Agar gir gaye toh jalebi ka case family court jaayega," and it was probably the best minute of the week for both of them, which neither then nor afterwards was ever going to mention.

At the toy-shooting gallery midway — a stall of tin ducks rowing left on a wire track under posters disowned by copyright — the stall-man offered five shots for twenty; Rudra took the spring rifle, checked the bent front sight against the tin with a quarter-second's adjustment learned with air-dried rags and pebbles back, way back, in the courtyard. He dropped four ducks in four shots and handed the rifle back.

The stall-man stared at him, then at the ducks, then in the bin where the prizes waited, and said, quietly and with conviction, to the world: "Koi toh aata hai achhe ghar se."

"Usse de dena," Rudra said, taking the sad little stuffed dog off the hook and handing it to the girl without looking at her, meeting the eyes of the midway.

Aadhya took the dog and looked at it for a long second. "Isko main kya karun?"

"Yaad rakhna jitna banduk-paper training mere liye teri taraf free mein rahi," Rudra said, gravely, folding his arms.

"Ha. *Ha.* Fine. Aata hun," and she walked in front to carry it, with the light from the wick-lines catching her shoulders, letting five unseen bystanders conclude, inaccurately on fifty counts, that the two of them were celebrating an engagement.

"Jhoola dekhega aakhiri mein. Pehle woh phir se maut ka kuan dikhane le jaa raha hai —" Veer tilted his head at the fairground bowls of noise above the Assi slope, where the barrel-cyclists' riders were even now defying municipal physics inside their wooden drums — "whoever's scared of the balloon, you know where my lap is."

---

Veer's arm-wrestling defeat of the mela's reigning tables began, as such things always begin, as lunch-bet idiocy and became, within half an hour, the whole evening's greased downhill slide.

The arm-wrestling table of this particular fairground — a module of the mela circuit that concerns itself with honour, shirts up the bicep, and a electricity of excited small-wagers — was reigned over by a soot-sweat goliath with the build of a municipal water-tank, who was popularly known, all along the Dev Deepawali circuit, by the nom de guerre of **Bhainsa** — standing, unstated, for buffalo. He had, by tallies chalked on a nearby wall by a promoter employed purely for the purpose, won one hundred and nine consecutive competitive wrestles at mela after mela, and he received challengers the way pianos receive moths.

Veer sat down opposite him for a ten-rupee stake and a rising ring of spectator noise.

"Chhote sahab," said Bhainsa, kindly, meaningfully squeezing a coconut of a fist, "deewar pe dekh raha hai mera record? Ek sau nau."

"Haan ji," said Veer bashfully, sitting, planting his elbow, settling his grip — and Rudra noticed, with the dry ice of dread, the way his friend had, entirely by instinct, *set* himself: elbow vertical, shoulder neutral, spine carrying load the way the courtyard drills from a different world had taught his spine when they were fifteen and being trained, neither one by the other but by the same merciless arithmetic, remembering a boyhood of staff corrections none of them knew the origin of —

Audience howling — *khao khao* — lock-grip — *ready—* —

"Chal—" said Bhainsa.

The arm went down backwards through all of Bhainsa's expectations with the irresistible momentum of a suspension bridge deciding to lie down. The tent's benches — twenty-two spectators — went up with the collective *oooooooo* of one single body. The promoter, chalking at his century-scored wall-board, froze mid-wicket, then realized, blinking, that One Hundred And Nine was about to revise. In the stands, people were shouting the whole Mahabharata with their bodies.

"Rematch," said Bhainsa, in the civilized and slightly horrified tones of the giant men that all tall tales are required by law to feed. "Ek aur."

Veer, who had not meant to — who *knew*, Rudra could see him knowing it in the exact instant after, with horror, colour rising — had fired one careless push of whatever the ghat-fight had mixed into his forearm, and had mistaken it for nothing at all. He stood up out of the chair backwards, smiling — people-pleasing, horrified —

"Bas bas — ek hi game, bhaiya —" and he dispersed the storm by clapping his own big hands together and thrusting the prize — the hundred rupees and a paper cone of roasted chana — back among the riot of his brand-new fairground legend, declining rematch with both palms raised in surrender, and Rudra got a shoulder around him and walked him out of there, laughing furiously, saying everything but the thing:

"Mazaak mein bhi itna daraya kyun karta hai yaar tu — ek wahi table hai poore mele mein jahan sambhalna zaroori tha."

"Maine kuch nahi kiya," Veer whispered, ducking through the tent-flap into the open fairground lights, almost pleading, almost laughing. "Rudra — sun raha hai? Maine *kuch bhi nahi* kiya."

I know, Rudra didn't say. Yehi toh problem hai, bhai. Kuch kiye bina yeh haal hai.

---

The fortune-teller's stall was two tarpaulin roofs away. Its owner-operator — a Bhojpuri-weight patriarch of a forecaster in a saffron turban gone coral pink with sun — had deployed the traditional constellation of props: a deck of worn "question-cards," a wheezing collection of hand-drawn zodiac charts, and a blue-plumaged parrot in a cage the size of a shoebox whose divine occupation it was, twelve hours a day, to pull cards out of a deck for ten rupees a prophecy.

Ira stopped in front of this establishment with the expression of a statistician beheld by a shrine.

"Dekho," she said. "Data ka mandir."

"Ira—" Aadhya began, who knew.

"Tum log uncertainty ki maa-behen karke maze lete ho, par yahan—" Ira gestured one open hand at the parrot's cage. "—ye hai. Reference standard. Mujhe dekhna hai ki prakriya kaise chalti hai. Paanch minute. Bas dekh rahi hoon—"

"Ira, _don't,_" said Rudra, hoping against twenty-two years of the city's physics.

She was already doing it. The patriarch, with the princely adaptability of men who have been asked by the decade for directions, conversions, job consultations and once — he reminisced weekly — a certificate for a wisdom tooth, registered her standing there in her kurta and her dry annihilating poise, read her ratio of devotion-to-spectacle in half a second, and without a word vacated the stall's one stool and presented it to her with the little consular bow men reserve for calamities or other proprietors.

"Beta, ek customer de do pehla," the patriarch requested patiently to both heavens, as Ira sat down. The parrot, sensing career change, practised breathing on the cage bars.

The first customer they drummed in was a fiftyish government-accountant grade passer-by whose question, shouted down from the porch of a nearby matar-chat stall, concerned his daughter's UPSC attempt. Ira took the man's hand the way she'd take a compass bearing — professionally, by the wrong end, left hand because Table A said left hand — told him to ask the parrot for a card — declined the card — closed her eyes exactly once, for the length of a wrist's cooling —

"Teen attempt ho gaye uski," she said, eyes still shut. "Problem Math nahi hai. Pakka nahi. *Paper* se pehle bhi wo blank ho jaati hai. Behan ko bolo poora sirf revise karaye ek baar, taking *notes out loud.*" She opened her eyes. "Is bar ho jayega. Shakht mehnat waali ladki hai. Daily sote waqt fan bahut zor ka chalti hai — wo band karwa dena. Neend se aadha paper banega."

The accountant stood blinking with ten rupees in his fingers. Then he put them down on the deck — folded twice, reverent — and said "Dhanyawaad beti" in the tone used for buses caught in time, and went off at speed. The patriarch watched him leave.

"Chauthi mein laga jitenge toh aana phir," the patriarch called after him, one professional to another, and then held the stool for Ira in silence, like a man signing over the bar to a better bartender. The parrot, watching the changed tenancy of its little stage, produced one ribald syllable of cage-opinion and went about its nuts. It had decided she was one of its own. The patriarch had decided she was one of his. Neither was wrong.

"Ye kya —" Rudra started, horrified-delighted.

"Seekh rahi thi bas," Ira explained, dead-eyed, as if detected in theft. "Main toh free mein li rahi thi information. Uska naam, bache, sab visible the. Main bhi dekh sakti hoon logon ko." But when the second customer sat down already — an anxious young mother with two toddlers and green mehendi fading off her ring finger — Ira went very still for two seconds, exactly the stillness of someone who has found a fish-hook under the water where a lily should be, and then gave the woman two entirely conventional sentences about patience and hidden blessings, and the woman went away exalted, and Ira would not meet Aadhya's eyes for ten minutes.

Aadhya, who knew precisely the dimensions of the look, took her elbow at the ninth minute and steered her out to the river.

"Kya tha wo?" she said quietly, when the fairground was half a diya behind them.

Ira looked out at the kneeling dark water. "Ghar ka address dikha mujhe," she said at last. "Kaunsi khidki, kaunsa lock. Bata deti toh kal raat koi wahan phir se ghus jaata — ya kuch. Main nahi bolti aisi cheezein." She folded her arms. "Problem ye hai, Aadhya — mujhe dikhta hai. Sabka. Bina pooche. Jaise mela mein wo photo lenewale hain na — log aate jaate rehte hain, photo ban jaati hai. Aaj tak maine kabhi kisi ka 'nakli' nahi bola, kabhi kisi ka asli matlab nahi bataya. Par aaj raat —" she stopped, and there was, for a heartbeat, a girl under the archivist — "—aaj raat mujhe wo aadmi bhi dikha jo parrot wale ke peeche baith ke diagram copy kar raha tha. Hamara nahi. *Apna.* Doosra jo kheench raha hai."

Aadhya put her arm around her — one arm, the temple-touch of shared ordinance — and let the river answer instead: the waves, the lights, the boats of gold, the little diyas sailing out of their makers' lives like errata.

---

They were all four still together at eleven, and nothing had exploded, which set a Book-of-Acts precedent.

The mela had softened into its final form by then: the vendors tearing down, lights coming off poles, the crowd's noise settling from human weather into the machinery of closing — bench-planks stacking, ropes unhooking, the giant wheel giving one last ceremonial rotation for nobody. The four of them stood at the water's edge, in a lane of matted grass where a thousand feet had passed, each of them about to say their goodnights; none of them saying them.

On the river before Dashashwamedh, out of some custom old beyond photograph, a last boat moved along mid-current throwing lamps in slow handfuls to the waves; and along the ghats, from Rajghat's far end down to Assi's forsaken scholars, the great dial turned: half a million flames going out one by one, extinguished not by wind but by intention — by hands that knew the hymns for the hour a song is over.

Before the last of them went, they did the thing themselves.

It was Aadhya who announced it was happening — went down the wet steps and bought five leaf-bowls off a boy half-asleep at his heap, paid him double for the impertinence of staying open for them, and loaded each bowl with its wick, its marigold, its thimble of ghee. And they stood in a row at the river's stone hem, the four of them, with the stranger's fifth — "kisi ke liye," the boy had said, unspecified — and lit them off each other's hands, and were quiet then, each with their pinch of flame, while the crowd-light went down like a stage around a closing act, each of them visited briefly by one private thing before setting it afloat.

*Veer* — holding the leaf-bowl with two fingers the size of small bananas, wanting as always to laugh and not daring to — who made the wish the way he had always made all important transactions, with zero negotiation: maa ke pair ka var. Uske andar sab aa jayega. Nikalte hue cheez sab aa jayegi.

*Ira* — who negotiated: *ek hi proof de do. Ek. Bs ek. Koi aur bhi dekh raha ho toh dikha do.* And held the flame; and in the flame, two rings deep, she caught herself staring at — wonder of wonders — the map: its seven rings, centred not below her but above. She put the bowl in the water with great care, as if it contained a very rare illness.

*Aadhya* — who wished, first, automatically, for her mother's health, the way you renew insurance; and then, out of nowhere her own, with an ache under the ribs she twenty days ago would have disallowed entry to: *jo bhi mujhe dekh raha hai, in chaaron ko dekhne de. Inhe choot mat lagne de.* She got both fingertips wet in the leaf's wake; the flame stayed small and did not gutter.

*Rudra* — did not make a wish. He had been raised by drill-command to fan wishes out evenly through the count; but tonight he let one coin of a thought drop, silently, into the only wishing-well of a city that used them up for food: *itni raat tak rahe. Bas. Itni raat tak.*

They let the five bowls go; the little flotilla of them pushed off the stones and stood into the current and sailed away down through the dead lamps like goldfish down a drain the size of the ocean.

"Ek second," said Aadhya suddenly, softly, pulling all their attention back out of the dark from wherever she had withdrawn to consider. She had a difference to note. "Rudra — dekh. Hum log jahan bhi move ho rahe hai na — udhar udhar diye ki taraf bheed jama ho rahi hai. Dekh — dekho. 'Passage' wali cheez —"

Rudra looked.

He had not put it into order until she spoke it; then he knew he had been tracking it for the last twenty minutes: the crowds, when they gathered, drifted over the fairground in slow blind clots; but along the ghatside tonight the clots had seams; along the seams, like thread going under black cloth, a second motion — the diya-lines still lit, interrupted at fixed intervals where people were not stepping, leaving vacancies like buckteeth in the light — and along those lanes of dark the dying lights were not simply going out. They were going out *faster.*

And the vacancies were at the six points he knew.

And under his collar, his mother's taped lines turned over faintly and slept again, as if she had nodded at the catalogue.

"Order hai isme," he said — low, to Veer, who had come up behind. "Battiyan nahi bujh rahi — *kheenchi jaa rahi hain.* Lane se. Point se."

"Ira ko bata," said Veer.

"Sabko bata raha hoon," he said, and turned.

Ira was already looking at him with both fists dug deep into the windcheater pockets, a mouth already full of whatever arithmetic today had handed her — the night the city should have held its greatest single deposit of fire-and-prayer, and the earnings known to be ending before their normal period — she had not yet even reached for paper; she was simply, hard-eyed, *standing in the answer.*

"Gyarah se ghata kar," she said, "ab *aath* din bache hain. Aaj ke baad aur kam. Aaj raat unhone inn sab se hi bhar liya." And she took out her hand at last, and rubbed at her eyes with her whole wrist, the way teenaged mothers do to break intimacy with their tiredness — and across the river, in the dark, the last unaccompanied cone of a vessel-lamp drifted over Manikarnika and sank soundlessly through nothing specific, the way gold coins sink without ceremony through the black lanes of the underworld.

They stood together in the leftover heat of five hundred thousand lamps, and did not say anything more, because nobody needed to, not tonight and not again: because *they were four*, and four, tonight, was a number nobody had assigned them and they had decided — separately and entirely — to be.

---

# CHAPTER 14 — THE BOAT RACE

The poster went up on a Thursday, pasted with rice-glue to the notice wall of Assi's chai shop between a lost-goat announcement and a tuition-class schedule, lettered by hand in the definitive red of events that have happened every year for longer than anyone has been checking:

**GANGA NAUKA DAUD — RAJGHAT SE PANCHGANGA TAK — SHANIVAR DOPAHAR 4 BAJE — ENTRY ₹200 — INAAM: ₹5001 + TROPHY + PURANE CHAMPION KA NAMAN**

Under it someone had ballpointed, without malice: *iss saal bharosa mat karna, bukhaar se zyada tez hai.*

Veer circled the date in the group chat with a photograph and a single message — **main aur tu** — and the argument lasted well into the night, because Rudra wanted it on record that rowing was a sport of synchronization and not heroism, and Veer wanted it on record that fighting a pahalwan in a fairground had been technically Rudra's fault for allowing him to get bored, and both of them, at bottom, needed very badly to do the simplest possible thing with their bodies in public for once, without anything glowing anywhere.

A boat race was exactly, blessedly, a thing with rules.

The girls were appointed by mutual tyranny to Management: strategy, registration, betting-tier surveillance (Ira), and morale (Aadhya, who claimed to despise all competitive sport and could name the batting order of four international sides historically).

"They're doing it for *themselves*," Ira explained to the chai-shop wall, as if charging admission to her own resignation. "Hamara boat hum jitayenge. Dekh lena. Lekin ek minute ka bhi umas nahi rahega beech mein."

The registration was conducted beneath the chai-shop's awning at a folding table run by Munna Majhi the elder — seventy-something, a torso like an oar, a face cross-hatched by sixty ghat-winters, famous as the man who had once piloted a whole wedding party through the flood of '78 without losing so much as one packet of chura. He looked the two of them up and down — the mountain boy and the wrapped knuckles — with the serenity of a man assessing secondhand furniture.

"Do sau," he said. "Naam?"

"Sawant. Pathak. Saath mein likho," said Veer.

"Do ladke hote toh paise oopar se lagte. Teen sau."

"Uncle, do sau toh poster pe—"

"Poster professional ke liye hai. Tum dono college waale ho. College waalon se zyada liya jaata hai kyunki tum boat ko jhooti umeed dete ho." But the old man was smiling through the haggle in the way of a man who had decided fifty years ago that the youth was part of the scenery he was sworn to like anyway; he took the notes from Veer's roll one by one, wet his thumb, counted to exactly two hundred, and struck the entry into the tin. "Register likhna padega. Bhaari boat mat lena. Jalpari bhaari hai — sahab-log ko nahi dete." He dipped the iron-nibbed pen into campaign-blue ink and wrote their names in with square strokes, and then, dry, still not looking up: "Suno. Subah do aadmi aaye the. Motorbike waale. Pooch rahe the ghat pe us raat kaun tha — jab khambha gira. Naam-muhalla-prize detail. Maine kaha dekha nahi." He looked up at last, one eye wide and belonging to caution, the other narrow and belonging to memory. "College wale kaam padhai karte ho yeh bhi?"

"Kabhi-kabhi," Rudra said, pleasantly, in the courtyard voice. "Unhone kisaan-kapat pahna tha kya?"

"Windcheater. Grey. Aur pair mein aisa boot jo bhaiya log nahi pahante." The old man swiped the register shut on their names. "Jeeto ya haaro — shaam ke pehle ghar nikal jaana. Chaar ghat pe paanch cheez daud rahi hai aajkal sivaye paani ke. Tum dono ko chhaap ke rakhna, shaniwar ke pehle bhi chalta hai."

They took the boat key from his hand without looking at each other. Down on the water, the afternoon's early practice runs were crashing the peace in ecstasy — hulls cracking over each other's wakes, oars up, boys standing to whoop.

"Thank you, Munna-ji," said Veer.

"Hmm," said the old man, and looked out at his river as a man looks at a beloved and increasingly unreliable cousin. "Jalpari ka khayal rakhna. Bhaari hai par wafadaar hai. Jaise boodha chowkidaar."

---

The day before the race they had an hour alone with Jalpari, for induction, because Veer held the view — entirely correct — that you do not begin a relationship with a machine on the morning it decides your reputation.

She lay at her chain off the Assi steps in the Friday late light, twenty-two feet of old river-timber, broad amidships, painted river-duck brown with a brass patch near the bow bearing her name in Devanagari and beneath the name, in painted numerals half-erased by seasons, a phone number for someone who had died. Veer climbed down first, barefoot, performed his own and everybody's checks — once around the waterline with his palm; once around the oar-locks listening for crack-song; bailing out the afternoon's ingress with the lota kept under the stern thwart for exactly that purpose — and only then did Rudra hand down the cardboard box of supplies: the electric tape, the sit-upon cushions produced mysteriously from someone's house, the sugar-sack of spare rope, four paracetamol and a pair of sturdy words of encouragement Ira had snipped off a previous year's science-college poster because she had judged them needed here.

In one hour they were at work — Veer caulking the bow's leak with the tar-soap donated by the chai-shop owner downstairs, Aadhya re-drilling the loose rowlock with her bicycle multi-tool and two bolts, Ira rechecking everything twice with a protractor as if she disputed geometry's permanence on principle. There was sweet disagreement. There were cut fingers and apology-band-aids.

At one point Veer stood on the thwart in the half-darkness with his arms out, narrating the principles of keel balance to an audience of three tired friends and seven moored hulks, and Aadhya, paint on her chin, said fondly: "Mummy kehti hain — jo ladka boat ko jitna pyaar se dekh raha hai, wohi naukari se dekhna chahiye tha—"

"Naukri boring hai," Veer replied, serene, from his pedestal. "Boat mein naav hai."

When they finished — *approximately* when they finished: nineteen minutes after Veer announced completion — Jalpari lay on her own shadow in no pain, the bilge dry, the locks true, and she'd taken aboard, invisible to any eye, her full complement: four hours of their work, their banter sitting like dust-pearls in her seams, their shared clumsy hopes already taking passenger-space on every thwart. She did not leak a cupful that evening. The next morning she leaked exactly enough to make it literary.

Saturday, four o'clock: the river had turned out.

Race days on the Ganga at Varanasi are as old as oars; the municipality came in later and licensed them. The start line lay north off Rajghat, where the current was widest and most honest — a stretch of brown-green water that gave any rower exactly as much river as his education deserved — with the finish buoy a white-painted oil drum bobbing opposite Panchganga, a route of perhaps two kilometres, downstream, diagonally hugging the ghatside so the crowd could follow along the steps like a standing ovation in motion.

Seventeen boats had entered. By hydrological legacy they divided, as they divided every year, into three unchangeable nations: the **boatmen's sons**, lean, gorgeously economical boys of the river who had been pulling oars since they were eight and whose strokes were as standardized as scripture (entry of Munna Majhi's people, the reigning champions for four years in their red trim boat); the **gym's rearguard**, broad and desperate, alumni of every akhara and cable-gym in Sigra and Lanka, strong as oxcarts and precisely as hydrodynamic; and the **others** — college boys, a pair of widowed enthusiasts, one team of boys from the polytechnic who had shown up in matching jerseys with *GANGA WARRIORS* printed on the back, who would capsize within sight of the starting rope, ritually, as they did every year.

The boat Veer had rented was old, twenty-two feet of heavy river-made timber named *Jalpari* on a patch of brass, and she leaked with the serene persistence of long experience.

"Ye doobi hui boat hai," Ira pronounced, verifications completed. "Rent wapas le lo."

"Doobi hui boat se hi jeette hain," Veer said serenely, settling his whole converted throne into the bow thwart and adjusting decibels by way of testing the oar-locks. "Fast boat waala drama pata hai kya hota hai? Jeetne pe log shak karte hain. Hum thoda poorane, mazboot, sarkari, seedha-nikamme falsely aahat wale hain."

"Five hundred aur ek rupee ka trophy ke liye ye sab speech de raha hai," muttered Aadhya.

"Society ke liye cup hai yeh!" announced Veer. Utter conviction. Somewhere underneath the conviction: that thing — the gold, the horizon-spanning bigness — which had, three weeks in, stopped answering his door and begun, instead, to *listen from the kitchen.* He could feel it in his wrist today, in the low sunshine like permanent feverishness. It had the decency — he and it had come to arrangements, he'd discovered — to keep entirely silent if he did not *ask* it things. Today he was going to do some rowing there, with it, within it, intending not to ask; that was the plan. He was, indisputably, the amateur champion of plans.

Rudra, in the stern seat, looked astern — composed, contented. "Ready?"

"Ready."

Ira stood on the topmost step with both windcheater pockets heavier than the windcheater, and shouted down the standard of her blessing: "Dono, laziness mat karna, pani heavy hai, tairne ki koshish mat karna, bas stroke—"

"— aur tu kya kar rahi hogi udhar chaar logon ki report file kar ke," Rudra yelled back pleasantly, and pushed off, and the river took them.

---

The start's drum had echoed twice off the facades before the two of them exhaled.

"Utpat," Aadhya pronounced, standing at the crowd's edge on tiptoe — and then, with something small and satisfied written along her mouth: "Chai piyogi? Mere taraf se."

Ira nodded once and followed — down the terraces toward the chai stall, without conversation, in the new quality of walk the last ten days had fabricated for them out of opposing materials: two women in parallel, neither asking the other's pace to change.

The chai came. They sat on the overlook stones above the river-lane and watched small shapes in the thread of light inscribe some old contention onto the afternoon.

"Tum log aise bahar bahut aate ho?" Ira said presently, data-gathering despite herself.

"Aate toh the," Aadhya said. "Ghoomne ke bahane se nahi — ek doosre ko dekhne ke bahane se." She scratched her nail along the step's old grain, half-listening to the crowd thunder as somebody went topsy-turvy into the shallows. "Mere ghar mein gaya-trip maana hai. Toh main jhooth bolti hoon — 'zoo ja rahi hoon,' 'library ja rahi hoon' — aur phir raat ko apni ungliyan ginti hoon." A long mouthful of chai, suspiciously examined. "Bachpan se mujhe kissi bhi din mein ek hi lamha mila hai jismein gate nahi tha. Wo ped ke andar wala — jahan adda jamake khadi ho sakti thi. Zameen wala." Her glance touched Ira's, checking for mockery, found none. "Ab zameen mujhe sun rahi hai waapis. Tum madad kar rahi ho — pata hai mujhe. Main aur tum dono apna poora nahi batate, kyunki kuch baatein bolte hi khatarnak ho jaati hain. Par hum dono ko shak nahi hota ek doosre pe. Bas itna hi chahti thi."

"Sun rahi hoon," said Ira. And then, with the exact kindness of a surveyor taking a witness-stone within reach: "Theek hai. Main bhi tumse poora nahi bolti. Ek din bolongi, jab proof haath mein hoga. Aaj nahi — kyunki aaj usne paani pe haath rakh diya hai, aur neeche wala dekhta hai ki maine dekha ya nahi." She drained the plastic cup. "Ghabrana mat. Race dekh le pehle. Yeh dono jeetne waale hain, aur mujhe statistics mein interest nahi."

"Hmm," said Aadhya, and for one minute they were — elbows on knees, completely full — two people who had never had friends before, watching their boys win twenty metres at a time.

---

The race went out — the way all boat races go out — drastically wide of anybody's plan.

The start rope snapped, the drum struck from the starter's rowboat, and the first hundred metres of the Ganga became, in seconds, a boiling traffic court. The polytechnic team pulled portside with the perfect synchronized incompetence they had trained all year, bore down onto the reigning champions' red trim boat like a runaway metro, missed by an oar's width, over-corrected — *splash* — and found water. The crowd, who came for nothing else, went up in a single huge libelous rejoicing.

Veer howled with laughter and lost the stroke count.

"Count pe ab tera future hai!" Rudra shouted from the stern, over the noise, dragging them back onto four-four breath with the throttle of his own two arms — and by the manner of all such recoveries, in powered boats or lives, the two of them found it at the same stroke: metronome-breath, eyes up, discipline bootstrapped from eleven years of courtyard drills, Veer feeling the load through his forearm and converting it the way a schoolboy converts stolen mangoes into afternoon; and they went along the lane of water harder and faster, harder and faster, passing the gym's ox-crews one by one (rancid and jealous cries in their wake, bets wavering, small fortunes knees-first down), the ghat-steps' crowd pacing them along the shore like a moving wedding.

At the halfway mark — the Narad Ghat alignment, where the shore wears its one slip of old temple-flag — they were third. Ahead, the champions' red trim, and one lean boat of the old khidki crews whose boys were known for racing anything, over anything, in any season. The red trim's two oarsmen, Munna Majhi's twin sons, pulled in deep fine malice, like rowing was pension they'd rather die than give up, and Veer, six seats' worth of mountain, looked over his shoulder, once, at Rudra, and said, with an enormous grin he could not suppress into the shape of any approved family speech:

"Toh le jaaun na inhe!"

"Le jaa." Rudra was laughing despite his ribs, despite the tape at his collarbone, despite even the coal of him banking slow and interested at the circus of human daring being done around it. "Count pe rakh. Chaar-chaar. Aane de inko — pata chalna chahiye unko apna level —"

But the river, un-licensed and possessed of its own entries, got there first.

---

It began as a wrongness in Rudra's own wake.

The wake behind a river-boat follows physics with the loyalty of a wounded soldier; it should fan out V-shaped, two shoulders of brown water peeling off in magnified homage. Their wake today — as Rudra glimpsed it over his shoulder between strokes, face tightening within himself — *sank.* It went from them aft and flattened discreetly into the river's surface as if taking instructions; and a long slack line of calm followed their boat, over the pulled water, as if the river had been ironed where they passed. When he took his next stroke, on the blade's exit — the water under the blade resisted *upward* — against the blade's own recovery — and pushed him gently but unmistakably *astern,* and under his taped sternum that seam stood up at forty million volts of attention, and it was all he could do in that half-second not to yell.

The bottom of the river was looking *at* their boat.

He remembered this wake. He had given this wake to it once before, on a dark night, burning. It had put its mouth, however softly, on Manikarnika and bidden him count. It recognized him now — or, no: not *him*; it recognized **the thing** — the furnace he carried. It was bumping its nose along his hull plates, doggish, meek, enormous, *recalling.*

"Rudra," Veer said, over his shoulder, voice low — eyes forward, hauling. "Ye kaisa gadbad lag raha hai paani. Jaise — *keechad*."

"Kheench raha hai woh cheez," Rudra heard himself answer — level, thrilled-terrified steady — "rok raha hai hume. Competition mein bhi nahi chhodta. **Stroke count dhayaan rakh. Veer — bas chalta rahe.**"

---

And Ira, on the ghat, gasped.

She had followed them by eye from the bank with the race's artillery column of spectators, but instruments kept finding in her hand their own fascinations — the compass had wound itself throughout the start so softly that she'd thought of casing it to its felt pouch; but now, in dead dry air, it was revolving. It was doing the slow willful inspection tour of the compass rose, like an astronaut re-learning which home was. She raised it to the water then. Bright as hook.

Ganga was lying beneath the race: the great darbari water, grandmother-water, mother-water of history — and under it, at forty metres down, a parallel river ran, recent, purpose-made, darkened; it had rope-lines converging under the exact mid-point of the race course; it doubled, sometimes trebled where the pride of seventeen boats' best strokes lashed it; and it gave nothing back. The surface of the official river glittered, sunned itself, accepted adoration. The second river tasted the boat where its shadow darkened the top of it, climbed it thirstily, *sipped,* dropped back into inventory.

The river was being skimmed, and the roadmap of the skimming, in broad daylight, among the boats of celebrating boys, was the Festival's own: six soft tapping-places — like cups taken up by waiters at a wedding — with one drinking-knot off Manikarnika that she *knew,* that she had drawn from a dead man's dark memory with lamp-lit guilt; it had flattened at last by the Dream-side of the walls she had made around it; it had grown *greedy.*

She took the camera of her phone and got, in eight seconds, four photographs of what no camera could photograph — the angle of the rowers' shadows over the race course's surface, clearly showing sheen; the compass needle swinging free across bearing-worn cardinal; the river. Her hands, even for the festival's sake, did not tremble very much. Upstream, where the banyan's ruin still stood wrapped in municipal red cloth like a wounded sentinel, the crows, off for long, all came back at once.

---

On the river, Veer jerked his oar out a half-beat early, sun in his teeth, thought of his mother's flour-smell and any god, and brought the whole craft's speed down off a count with a new motion nobody taught him; somewhere aft he heard Rudra grunt — heard the throttled volume of a rest-day exercise pushing its way back up the boy's spine — and with the whole blaze of fury-feel in him he shouted in a voice of disproportioned happiness —

"Tight! Left! *Stroke!*—"

The boat lifted — a that-sound that is not the water, that is not air; a wet plain *snarl* ran along their keel underside from bow to stern as if a long dog brushed beneath the boards — and they went half-a-boat faster, passing the champions' red trim at the final bend with three-quarters of a length of open river — and crossed the finish drum-buoy at Panchganga into the groan and roar of thousands, and Munna Majhi's twins, who flunked exams with equal patience every year and did not intend to learn, pulled their dying strokes into the crowd-afield like men told good news.

Veer, still inside the noise, dropped the oar's worn handles, caught a whole rack of breath in one drag, and lay entirely backwards across Jalpari's thwart like a wet piece of sacking. Tufts of broken sky lay above him. Somewhere the crowd-kingdom was absolutely that — blankets and clapping — and Rudra, still upright and shaking in every piled-up joint, looked down at the river that ran along its old channels as though nothing had happened.

The wake behind them flattened politely. Then, one hundred feet aft, it stopped moving.

"Chal," Rudra said, a very long way from the register of heroes, and began to steer them, slowly, with people arguing in dozens of directions on the shore, towards the landing-steps; and each row of water coming up against Jalpari's flank touched her as if in sermons — subsided — and retired.

On the steps, the sport that had been played on that water for a hundred years concluded in the hundred-years-old way: without podiums. Munna Majhi's twins climbed out of the red trim boat, spat expertly over opposite shoulders, shook the winners' hands with the complete warmth of the defeated — "Agle saal yahin. Yahi boat. Yaad rakhna." — and were gone into the crowd before the trophy arrived, because losing, if you keep its hours correct, is simply an appointment for next year.

The prize came down through a relay of khaki-shirted organizers: five thousand one rupees in an envelope sealed with cellotape, and the trophy, plastic-and-gold, handed over with both hands by the councillor's nephew, everybody stiff as a wedding photograph. "Haath mein lo, haath!" hissed somebody's uncle from the second step, and Veer took it with both of his — the photo flashed — and for the record of the district's paper-of-note there they stood, two champions grinning with their foreheads shining, a pink bear balloon tied accidentally permanent to Veer's wrist giving the shot its immortal official weirdness, at the edge of the winning-boat two girls looking levelly at the camera in completely different register of not-smiling.

"Champions se nivedan," the announcer's loudspeaker clucked bewildered bilingual, "ki ve log apna ghar-pata stage pe darj karwayen" — which produced general volleys of unrepeatable shore-commentary and the first honest laughter of the day.
