"Chalo ghar," Ira called from the high step.

"Ghar chalte hain," Munna-ji agreed from his table, and looked out of his one wide eye at the two boys and past them at the water, at the two rivers lying braided under the dusk, and then up into the sky above the city as if consulting a senior. "Jaldi. Aaj paani bahut *jaag* raha hai."

And nobody who stood there that evening could afterwards have explained why they would remember it as the last warm ordinary dusk — the crowd thinning into the lanes with folded poster-boards, Jalpari riding at her chain among the moored hulks, the lamp-lighters coming out with their long poles like the setting-out of a smaller, insubordinate heaven upon the town.

---

The trophy was plastic and gold-plated in the tradition of everywhere; it had a plastic boat on it. It went home on Veer's lap in the auto, upon his enormous knees, and he held it with the ginger meticulousness of a christening uncle, only saying, once every minute or so, "Behti hui cheez ke upar se jeeta hoon — *jeeta hoon bhai*," unable to do anything else at all with the inside of himself without losing a structured plan of being a person.

It was Aadhya, crammed along with the rest of them into the auto's back seat, who — after watching Ira silently add and subtract line-angles with her eyes the whole way, crossing and uncrossing two rivers only she could see — said into the engine-noise:

"Kal sab milte hain, chai pe. Maine sun liya tum dono ka bachon-wala bhaasha khelna race ke peechhe. Detail mein. *Bataenge.* Sab."

Her voice didn't crack; it carried the complete anti-moral of the hardened local, the girl who had attended prayers with demons in their heart and then gone to buy potatoes. The auto jounced; a small ache ran from the driver's brake through everyone's bones.

And Veer held up the trophy against the streetlight, so the cheap gold flooded them all for one passing second in waves of imitation splendour they did not mind at all — they were too tired, too rowed-out, too *alive* to mind. At the end of the day it was, properly, the first object their four lives had won together, and even Ira leaned around it, once, briefly, a satisfied pride-drunk parenthesis of a person:

"Paanch hazaar ek rupaye," Veer reminded the window, happily, for the eighth time. "Aur *naman*."

"Trophy apne baap ka?" said Rudra.

"Trophy APNA hai, bhai. Kal chhapne waali hai photo — dekh lena akhbaar mein—"

"Mandir arti nahi," Ira corrected without looking up, "marathon coverage hota hai usko."

"Jo bhi. Kal tak poora Nagar Nigam poochhega. *Kaun hai yeh ladke — jo boat wo chala rahe the paani bhaichara—*"

---

That night, after a dinner she ate standing and unremembered, Ira opened the notebook onto one whole page and drew the two rivers.

The first — surface, shown to the spectator — she drew level, in clean ruled strips, the way the official charts had it. Unwavering. Sacred. Zero.

Under it she drew, with two alternating rows of grey storm-hatching, the second river — the one that ran no barges, the one that lay directly beneath the real one's light: its side-gutters opening onto six little inductor-points, and at the seventh — out of scale, lower than truth could be drawn — she hatched the mouth.

She dated the drawing, top right corner, in her most impersonal hand. She marked it:

*SUBJECT: river rendered by halves. OBSERVATION: aaj pehli baar dhoop mein kheencha. It is pacing up its strength.*

Below that she wrote, pencil wearing through the soft paper:

**7 din.**

And then — because her hand had finally been told what kind of arithmetic was imminent, and the arithmetic did not deserve the dignity of more ink than this — just, alone in the middle of the page:

**7.**

---

# CHAPTER 15 — THE LIBRARY

The morning of it, at home, had insisted on being ordinary first.

Breakfast. Her father's chair — pulled out, sat in, asked over: an accountant of familial habits, measuring her silences by teaspoons. Her mother, who for three weeks had managed a tense up-curve towards entente — *zoo se phone aaya tha, college jaa rahi ho na* — brought the dal to table with her own unusual stint of looking directly, and said, standing over her daughter's chair with both House-lamps lit:

"Chaudah din ho gaye us raat ko."

"Haan, Ammu."

"Bina raat ke kuch bhi khaya kiye ho tum?" It was a sentence almost the same shape as her habitually careful ones, and at the same time a precisely and intricately formulated sentence that had, visibly, been rehearsed; its edges wavered wherever it had had to carry a weight. "Subah se bhuk lagi hogi. Pakode bana rahi hoon thodi der mein. Tum college se aa jao toh dono saath khayenge."

"Aa jaungi, Ammu. Paanch tak." She took this as well as its promise allowed her. Her mother nodded tersely, and the whole silent ever-understood treaty of mornings was, by all surface measures, at end.

Her father, from the courtyard's other doorway, in a morning-calm voice too consistent with his normal volume for anyone who did not know the family: "Nayi kitaab kharidoge toh bill le aana. Hisaab complete rahega."

It was its own translation: *tumhe pata hai, humein pata hai; hamein bill chahiye, tumhe cover chahiye.* But what stayed with her all day was the way, without ever checking, without ever even looking at her, both parents had each in their own idiom promised to keep the door open as long as the secret kept itself — the way houses can practice fine border-mercy without any treaty-paper at all. "Papa—" she had to stop. "Haan. Bill le aaungi." The gate clicked its usual dignity behind her.

"Tab se kabhi dikhti ho ruk ke," her mother added at the door, in the tone of somebody closing inventory. She put down the empty bowl and looked at Aadhya's hands. Only that: at the hands. Then, entirely lightly, in the direction of the windows: "Padhna mat zyada raat ko. Aankhein kharab hoti hain. Aaj jaldi aa jana, padhayi ke saath-saath khaane ka bhi time table banao."

Aadhya went, with something like a long-held breath finally released, and knew — as she had known since that night — that the mothers of this city can be fluent in their daughters without translation, and that her mother had offered a treaty of secret-keeping, singularly and unmistakably deliberate. She touched her mother's hand once, leaving; the hand only returned the pressure; neither of them had spoken of it at all.

The 8:40 shared-auto carried her down the avenue like nothing special — three girls behind handkerchiefs, a procession of bicycle bells, dust and sun.

---

The Central Library stood on the campus' far side like the serious elder of all its buildings — tall teak stacks, fans hanging off rods like sleeping fruit-bats, clerestory lights gone umber through nine monsoons of uncleaned panes, dust ruling every surface with the unchallenged tenure of a founding family. The present government of it was administered from the issue desk by Shastri-ji — officially retired, officially re-engaged, in actuality irreplaceable — a man with a white parted beard and a cataloguer's spinal curve, known on campus to have memorized the location of every book in the building in protest against the computer.

Aadhya came in on a Wednesday afternoon with her roll-bag and her reasons and signed the register, under discipline, in a column of other people's Wednesdays.

She had decided, on Sunday night, lying awake, that she was done being the only person on the crew who studied nothing. Veer had his arm. Rudra had his tapes. Ira had her whole mad attic-instrument-room. Aadhya meant this with no vanity: she wanted, ten nights after her leaf turned grey-headed at the banyan's roots, to have *data* — on record, in some authoritative neutral voice printed decades before she existed — about what a tree remembers when it dies standing; about the way the city's underground was plumbed; about the ring. About anything.

The banyan had made her a promise she could not name. The library, she reasoned, was where promises get filed.

---

Botany first — the respectable runway. She spent the first hour among hardbound monographs and journals in the Plant Sciences alcove, looking for anything at all on *Ficus benghalensis* and sudden necrosis; found, as she suspected, decades of work on roots and none on ghosts. The genus-entries said the same calm things — aerial prop roots, canopies of acres, tremendous longevity — and she turned pages feeling the dry opacity of scholarship answer her, and she could not have said exactly when she first felt it. Somewhere between hour one and hour two, in the alcove's deep quiet, she became aware that she was leaning — that her body had, unasked, begun pressing its weight into the shelf's flank as if into friendly bark.

She pulled herself upright, embarrassed. The shelf's wood was kind as always, inanimate. Nobody had been back here among the low cabinets since the term's last exam; the old stacks smelled of night-glue and ingested rain.

On impulse — because she had learned, over eleven strange nights, that the only tool she owned was attention — she put her palm flat against the inside upright of the racked shelf and *asked.* Not aloud. Not even in words: the way she'd asked the marigold once, a year ago, in the mela-line of memory when a crushed flower half stood up under her thumb — with presence, with the offer of her pulse as an ear.

The wood answered. Faintly. Ridiculously.

It informed her that it had once been a tree forty miles east, carted in as logs on bullock-wagon roads that had become tramlines that had become nothing; that it had spent its own youth shaking in wind it missed like a marriage; that it was, as shelving, exactly as happy as it could afford to be, in a world made of its body's purposes; and that against the East wall (her heart knocked) there was a shelf where a book had been opened so often at two particular gatherings that the spine still kept the memory of a reader's hands, eleven years of warmth pressed into the hinge like oil into a door-frame.

She stood with her palm resting flat on teak and did not let herself weep because there was a reader-girl two alcoves over and she refused to be *that* girl in the quiet section.

She tried Indology.

There, the card catalog — a wooden cabinet older than her grandmother, brass tab-handles worn pale — brought up what she had secretly been hunting: a whole nest of references on the **Pancha Koshi Yatra**. Books from another century when pilgrim-geography was still a science men confessed to publicly: an 1876 route-survey with unfolding linen plates; a Vishwanath-era miscellany in two bindings; an octavo bound in red with the ghost-of-the-colophon smell of the good stuff; a thin green folio with the District Board seal on its spine — the encyclopoedia of this region's charitability, temple administration, walking customs, well-maintained since by people who wore sweaters.

She took them to the alcove's long table. She had the feeling — foolish, narcotic — that the room had pulled in closer to listen.

For the next two hours she read with the ferocity of a person researching her own illness in a hospital corridor. The 1876 route-survey, opened to its linen plate, gave her the first clean picture: the circuit's five kos, the dharamshalas at the cardinal stands, the halting-stones where processions changed their shoulders — and, running faintly under the walker's tracks in the plate's faded blue, *the wells*. Not water wells: the old time-marks of the circuit, halting-niches of a different plumbing entirely, each drawn as a small lotus-notch at the plate's margin, each accompanied by the surveyor's bored pencil: *tank, dry; tank, dubious; tank, visited only by certain families.* Six of them stood, on the plate, at exact intervals around the circumference, and the seventh stood at its heart — she put her ruler-straight stare against it and verified, mark by mark, the same seven that Ira had ringed in red across three weeks of compass-drift: N-3 among them, sitting where the campus lawns remembered a great old tree.

The miscellany's second binding told her who had *tended* the stations. That was the section with the pages missing — and now she understood why the excisions were cleanest there: it was the registry of the *keepers* — which family watered which niche, which line of widows had fed which halting-point since before the Company, whose diwans had signed the temple accounts in the famine years, and (she discovered by measuring the size of the gap with one finger along the spine) roughly forty further pages on the *history* of one such line of keepers — a line that in this registered copy ceased, exactly, to exist at all.

Of the name of that line, every copy of every volume retained precisely nothing. Where it should have stood, the binding's heart opened on the brown canyon of tape.

She wrote the date-patterns down. She copied the marginals letter by letter, tiny ergonomic script, her father's hand over her shoulder. She wrote, at the bottom of her own page, slower: *Somebody spent sixty years removing a name from the shelves. Somebody else spent those same years learning where the holes were. And on the books' spines the blade-hands came back to keep the hole neat — three weeks ago — when a lamp cracked at a ghat and the city began, every night, to go gently more dim.*

And below that, inside a square bracket, in her own hand, before her nerve could repeal it:

*[Is this what the tree saw, when it looked down into the ground?]*

---

The Pancha Koshi entries were themselves. Perfectly ordinary, perfectly devout: the circuit, the five kos, the halting-points where pilgrims took the night's shelter (the colonial surveyor's spellings mangled like pilgrims' feet on stones); the famous old fact that half the circumference lay off-road even in 1876 because the route pre-dated roads; and column by column, the unchanging round phrases about *accumulated merit* describing what the yatra stored where it walked.

She wrote, dizzy with small gratitude, with both hands.

Three references in, the mischief began.

She had noticed the pattern as a coldness before she noticed it as a fact. Book four — the octavo — opened shyly, on pasted endpapers left over from reigns. The section on the five-kos circuit was there. The section on the sacred geography of the region's lineages was there — **in theory.** Thirty pages were missing. Not torn in anger, not budget-cut: *removed* — excised — with fine straight blades, exactly to the innermost 2mm of the spine, like teeth drilled out by someone who cared about mouths. Where those pages should have opened there was a two-page trench of quiet, and on the remaining stub-leaf, in penciled minuscule hand, half-erased by someone else's rubber years ago, a column of marginalia bent to accommodate itself:

*…par itihaas koi ghar nahi hota jis mein sabke naam likhe hon…*

*…yatra the nadi ke niche dhara chhodti hai…*

*…parampara se bada keval ek bar jo asli ho chuka ho…*

And below them, at the bottom of the stub — in a different hand: darker pencil, later, energetic enough to pressure through onto the second stub —

*…iska poora naam mat dhundo. Isme faayda nahi hai…*

She sat for a full minute with her finger resting halfway across those words, looking down through them with all her attention, like water looking through ice.

Book five had the same surgery. Book six, the green District Board folio, same. In each, the excision was exact to the same two gatherings: the lineage-section of the old geographies, and the compound gazette of the ring's holy tree-stations — who tended which, in whose family's keeping. In each, someone's hand had closed each wound with brown library tape, in years when tape was not issued, by hands which knew the alcove's job.

Someone had gone, with plain industry, through *every copy* of the region's old pieced-together sacred geography, and removed the same pages from all of them.

Her hands had gone cold somewhere in the middle. She registered this the way another woman registers rain — academically, from behind glass — and kept going back through the alcove, volume by volume. The excision was not despair, not vandalism. Vandalism jabs. This was a scalpel's trace: sixty pages removed from copy after copy after copy, and each wound finished with archive-grade attention, and each tape-seal a slightly different width — *not one year's work. Several years*. Whoever did it had come back, again and again, improving, rehearsing concealment when concealment was no longer concealable; they had, in places, replaced what they took with cut-down leaves from condemned duplicates, so that page-counts would pass a hurried audit, and only the truly patient would ever find the joints where the paper's age betrayed the swap. After the third book she understood the shape of the thief's attention: it was not stealing treasure. It was *censoring a lineage* — removing the location-record of a stream of nursed memory so thoroughly that even the librarians forgot there had ever been water there.

She worked through two hours of this as if walking a long road at night, eyes grounded on the stones immediately ahead, careful not to look up at the distance of it all. If she looked up — she knew — she would be overtaken by the biggest thing her hands had not yet found the courage to hold: the realization that *someone had known there was knowledge worth this much surgery*, generations before any coil-holding men in grey windcheaters came asking about a family's history with the nerve to bend over her roll-bag outside a college gate.

She was not the first person to search these shelves. That was explicable. What she was was the *only other,* and her predecessors — plural — had left, unmistakably, the signs of their passing: anachronistic rubber-bands gone to petrified rope; a child's homework-page marker wedged into a 1931 finance report at exactly the relevant table; shavings of blade-cut page-edge caked yellow-brown along two of the spines — holding, still, after all these waiting years, the faintest exhausted smokiness of incense. Whoever owned these earlier hands had not trusted librarians with where they had been. They had not even trusted each other.

And under the last stub of the last folio, deep into the crease, there was a librarian's pencil-line note, on the origins-page, right against the table of contents — in that darker, later hand — three words:

**Stop looking.**

---

Twenty minutes later, heart finally consenting to slow to a jog, she went back through the register copies at the issue desk — the visitor-books of decades — and found them in order:

Nineteen years ago: an officer's hand, long and elegant, blue ink, the name entered eleven times at three-month intervals — then, at some final spring date, simply stopping, the way a heart stops when its owner's work moves elsewhere. The hand was the near-brother of a handwriting she knew from her own father's letters; near enough to make her wrist move in sympathy before her mind understood why. But the register's name was not his. Another man's. A name she did not know, filed against the same stacks, the same alcove, the same requests.

Before him — a generation earlier — a second visitor: the pencil-neat hand of the stub-marginals themselves, the same cadence, cleared through the same desk. Water had damaged two of those entries. On the third, the blue-black letters floated loose but legible. She read what she could and closed the file gently, the way you close a door on a room where somebody else's grandfather is sleeping.

And the last name — the one that stayed with her — was dated twenty-one days ago. A Monday. Cramped modern handwriting, no indigo, no fountain pen, ballpoint's shallow trench. Two weeks after the burning night at the ghats. Logged twice again in the fortnight since. Always the alcove. Always Indology. Never issuing — only reading, only cutting.

*Who else,* she wondered, the cold climbing her in slow administrative layers, *in this city brings a blade to a library?*

She put her palm flat against a shelf upright — the way, lately, she was learning one did, to check. The old wood was drowsy and kind; there was gratitude banked in this building the way it was banked in prayer-halls — three generations of opened books and wandering readers stamping the air of the alcove with their appetites — and under the gratitude, ever so faint under the floor's two tons of sleeping records, she felt the other presence also: the drilled agency of the blade-hands, still fresh, like chalk-lines on a floor where a body had been.

She pulled her hand back before the shelves could begin to answer in detail, folded every paper she had copied into her roll-bag, and walked the length of the silent reading room with the exact, unhurried calm of a person who has rehearsed, all week, not running.

---

Back at the issue desk, she took a year's borrowing file squarely in both arms.

The library was emptying around her in the daily ritual — readers folding gone light into their bags, stools being hooked under tables with the settled choreography of long tenancy — while outside, the campus honked and went by with the luck of buses. Shastri-ji looked up because his catalog of gestures was large enough to include reader-departures.

"Almirah B," she said, as innocently as she could arrange. "Wo purane gazetteers — unke reference mein ek naam tha. *Shastri* likha tha notes mein. Aap jaante honge."

The old librarian looked at her for a while over his spectacles with the deliberation of a man whose job, vocationally, was to distinguish one edition from another. Then he smiled, a true one, sad at the edges.

"Gazetteer aaj pandrah saal mein teesra insaan khole hai," he said. "Pehle Pandey sahab — jo ab sheher mein nahi hain; unke kiiye khud hi utha ke diya tha table. Phir ek aur sahab — unka naam nahi bataya jaa sakta, unhone likh ke diya tha ki nahi batana. Dono ne wahi pages maange the jo aaj aapko milte nahi." He tapped the register's closed cover twice, forefinger only, like a man tapping the lid of a well to check if things echo. "Beta. Kabhi-kabhi books ke liye tape theek hota hai. Logon ke liye nahi. Jo nikaala gaya, uske aadhe gharon ke bache ab bhi is sheher mein hain. Aisa mat sochna ki kaagaz tote gaye toh baat tootti hai."

She stood with her roll-bag hugged, and her heart going deliberately at the doorway of every cold idea possible, and asked the only safe question available:

"Un dusre sahab ko main pehchaanungi kaise?"

"Pehchaan rahi ho tumhe," said Shastri-ji gently, snapping the register shut on her name like a boltsman going home. "Kal aa jaana. Kal bhi kholuunga." A pause; the old face did something complicated, and decided. "Meri apni diary hai. Chhattis saal yahan bitaye hain — jo mitaaya jaata tha, jo shabd jhanke kaate jaate the, main raat ko apni diary mein utaar leta tha. Tape ki bhi umar nahi hoti zyada. Kaagaz phool jaata hai, shabd mera reh jaata hai." His smile creased the ends of his eyes wide. "Kal do baje. Lekin ek sharat — kisi ko mat batana. *Kisi ko.* Is sheher mein jo log sach ki hifazat karte hain, ve haar baithe aadmi jaise chalte hain — aur mujhe aise hi jeena aaya."

Aadhya did not thank him. She bowed — a motion from nowhere, a muscle of the correct kind — and went out into the moth-coloured hallway with the day's heat still held in the stones, and the first feather-touch of hope at her ribs.

The hope kept for seven seconds.

At the gate's book-return bin she stopped dead: leaning against the pillar beside it, reading the newspaper back-to-front in an acrylic gesture, was a man in a grey windcheater — too bald, too weather-safe for the mosquito hour, who collected his weight with his shoulders as if he frequently carried something in his pockets which she very well knew. He did not look up. He did not, as far as her terrified eyes measured, *need* to.

She struck north instantly, up the principal avenue of students — alive, peopled, fully sunlit — and a shared auto bore her off along the great lanes home.

At the turning she permitted herself one glance back. The man had not moved at all; the newspaper was being read with total absorption, back page first, a posture sustained with theatrical discipline past all reason of a man's comfort, past even the property of patience — and only then, from the moving conveyance, did it dawn on her that the newspaper's sheet — the front side visible only beyond his shoulder — displayed a page of the paper whose morning headline she had read over the poha: *GHAT STAMBHA GIRAA: JAANCH JAARI*. He had opened the paper to the *last* page, to read it, and he was not reading the last page.

She got the auto's number from the numberplate box of her proper instincts, filed it in the deepest pocket of her routine, and did not think of it again in the auto, not even once, her pulse and the vehicle's rattle keeping one strictly separate account; the driver's mirror was full of the road behind and had nothing in it; but for the five kilometres home she kept herself from turning around in any case, a posture of an inch of the head that cost her an hour of peace later.

---

The Central Library's best lamps went out at six. Shastri-ji, his day's administration finally completed, walked from the issue desk to the alcove he remembered being prepared to like in 1991, put his hand in a specific place on the Catalogue cabinet, took out a rubber-banded notebook no wider than a newspaper column, and wrote in it one line in his customary Devanagari — very old, very short:

*Aaj teesri aayi. Sahas hai. Ankhon mein sawaal hai, haar mein nahi. Uparwala saath de.*

---

She rode back to Lanka in a shared auto with her roll-bag against her chest like a baby during an emergency landing, and with every lurch of its banking the evening's cargo rearranged itself inside her: the torn books; the same excised gatherings in copy after copy; the two earlier readers going three-monthly to the same well; the third one — three weeks ago, ballpoint — cutting as if the books were vegetables and dinner was at last due; *Stop looking*, one hand; and her own hand, steadying itself on the auto's frame-rail, going cold all through.

At her street-corner, paying the driver, she found the second note.

It was folded twice, taped to the underside of her roll-bag's flap — placed there sometime between the alcove and the bus, by hands she had sat twenty feet from for an hour and had never once noticed — and it said, in the darker, later pencil:

**Stop looking. Main dekh rahi hoon.**

She stood on her own pavement in the day's last violence of birdsong, with her own gate in view and her mother's balcony chair in it, and she made herself a promise cheaper and heavier than any other she had sworn that month: that she would not tell her mother about the library, and that she would — immediately, tonight, before courage had time to open account — tell the other three *everything*.

And below the pavement's four mineral inches of skin, under the auto's humming diesel trail and the tailings of the evening's last traffic, the old city went on with the day's final settling of its warmth — some of it blessedly borne by her neighbourhood, much of it older gratitude, all of it being carried down, slowly, through the counting stations, on somebody's measured orders.

---

In her room that night she did two slow, practical things.

First: she sat herself under the desk lamp with a square of cotton cloth and inspected her own hands. She had washed them ten thousand times in her ordinary life — after trains, after hospital visits, after mela food — but tonight it was inventory, not hygiene; her palms under the lamplight like a pair of confiscated instruments. She looked a long time at their fine lines. She looked for any seed of stranger-colour under the skin and found nothing. She looked at the thumb that had left a green shadow on bark one night, and said aloud to no one:

"Thoda sabr karo. Kuch seekh lunga— *seekh lungi* pehle."

Then she took out her practical file and a pencil, and spent the next hours making her Day's work look like homework: a clean ruled index of everything she had copied from the torn books — neat, cross-referenced, exactly as a student files notes against a viva. If anyone asked: Pancha Koshi project. Pilgrimage routes of the old city. Geography elective. The evidence smiled back innocently off the page; but inside its marginal numbers, inside the gaps of a photocopied plate, it silently carried the trench-map of what had been stolen, by whom, over how many years.

Around eleven her mother came in with warm milk — the eleven-o'clock milk, oldest treaty in the building — and did not ask anything. Aadhya covered the two names with a page-marker, took the katori with due seriousness, and sat drinking in long thinking sips while her mother, pausing once at the door, said:

"Kal college hai?"

"Shanivaar hai. Library jaa rahi hoon. Ek pustak waale Dada hain wahan — purane gazetteer ke boss — unke paas kuch hai. Kal poochungi."

Her mother looked at her once, sidelong, measure and mercy combined, and left the door arranged in the house's old shared silence.

Aadhya turned the lamp low and slept. For the first time in weeks of being marked, she chose not to listen for what was moving under the city — chose it deliberately, like closing a window — and slept the unreasonable sleep of someone who had, at last, work to do.

---

# CHAPTER 16 — THE ROOFTOP

Veer had inherited, by birth and by luck and by the mysterious diplomacy of his mother, the use of the highest roof on his lane — the terrace above the old neighbours' wing, where the water tank squatted like a bald owlet, where a forty-year-old TV antenna stood bent double against the sky, and where, at sunset, the whole city seemed to postpone itself, the way rooms pause when a door is opening somewhere else.

His mother had spent the day constructing pakoras with the entrenched resolve of a woman funding an expedition. "Chaar baje aayenge?" she narrated without looking up — "Main bahar nahi ja rahi. Tum log upar baithte jao, aur sambhal ke." She'd sent up a steel tray — the pakora pyramid, four katoris of green chutney squeezed from nothing, a jug of nimbu-shikanji, and on the tray's exact compass-centre, one saucer of salt that she rotated, arbitrarily suspiciously superstitiously, to true north. "Nazar laga mat dikhana un charon ko," she warned him, slapping away his sneaking hand. "Bohot achhe lagte hain wo log."

"Ammi—"

"Pakode garam rehne chahiye."

They came up at half past five, with two packets of roasted corn bought on the corner-steps as a teenagers' tithe, and Aadhya hugging her roll-bag against the roof's squabbling evening wind, and Ira locking the trapdoor behind them — a habit she had imported, directly and undisguisedly, from the month she had spent believing the Durgakund door was not fully able to keep anything in or out. Nobody minded. Nobody locked the other one with any different care.

The view, when they dismounted the final awkward terrace-door step and dispersed along the coping into their now-definite places — Veer at the tank, Aadhya at the north parapet, Ira at the corner where the sun went down — was the usual private miracle of a city in which you could stand still. To the north, temple-tops and rose-dust air and one triple-cornered old kiosk sheening; northeast, in rinsed lines, the river sliding stored and lazy gold westwards, carrying drained prayer-smoke on its back; and along the vast flat galaxy of roofs around them, other families coming out to beat their rugs or to fly a last evening kite where it caught the last usable wind, children mouse-brown with play. Everywhere the sounds: women calling in children, the city's night-detail preparing for route.

Aadhya told them about the library.

She told it flat, the way you recount a stranger's misfortune — the excisions, the two earlier names at intervals of a generation, the third name with its ballpoint three weeks old, the notes — and she handed around the two paper slips like a teacher distributing bad results. They passed the pencil-fragments from hand to hand. *Stop looking*, read each of them in turn, with scrupulous fairness; and the second one: *Main dekh rahi hoon.*

"Itni si cheez mein itna data hai," said Ira, pinching the second note's edge up to the last of the light. "Pehla note — chhote pen se, dabey hue, purana mood, *warning*. Doosra note — naya pressure, seedha, saaf — *protest*. 'Main dekh rahi hoon' — matlab jo likh rahi hai wo humein rok nahi rahi, wo khud dekh rahi hai humari jagah." Her eyes stayed on the paper as if daring it to confess further. "Lekin pages kaatne waala koi aur hai. Blade tez hai, professional haath." She held the slip steady against the dying light. "Katne waala alag. Rokne waali alag. Rokne waali likhti hai 'main dekh rahi hoon' — matlab wo ab bhi *wahan hai*. Roz ya kabhi-kabhi. Par dekhti zaroor hai."

"Jo dekh rahi hai, wo humari taraf hai?" Veer asked.

"Pata nahi," Ira said. "Par humein bola nahi gaya police ko. Roka gaya sirf. Kabhi-kabhi rokna bhi madad hoti hai."

Rudra had been quiet through all of it — knuckles wrapped, palms cupped together — and now, without moving, he said:

"Teesra naam. Register wala. Gyarah dafa likhne waala. Uska naam kya tha?"

His voice did not rise, and nobody answered him straight, because the answer was sitting already in each of their ribs. Ira set the slip down on the parapet with the cautious flat care of a pathologist laying out one exhibit.

"Unnees saal pehle," she said. "Pehli entry. Blue ink. Officer jaisa haath. Naam —" she shook her head once — "naam ka pehla akshar *R* hai. Bas itna bacha hai. Aage ka portion paani ne khaa liya." She let the words sit. "Do saal woh aata raha. Phir likhna band kar diya."

Nobody had a verdict on that. Down the parapet Aadhya watched Rudra's face perform, without a single word, the whole thing it had so many times promised her in courtyard code that it would never perform: the silent item-by-item topple of the masonry called *nineteen years*; the eyes shuttering from inside; the breath — in, four — the breath — hold, four — and the re-settling. Nobody said anything for ten whole breaths.

The sun detached itself from the last ledge west of them and dimmed to gratefuller color.

In that minute of dimming, Ira did what Ira did. The wrist-strip came out of her sleeve's pinned margin without looking; the compass surfaced from her pocket on the blind confidence of habit; she took one reading of each — once at the sky, once toward the street below them, once *down* — and stowed them away, and said nothing for a minute.

"Kitna?" Veer asked finally, with the blunt courage of a child asking the dentist where it will hurt.

"Chhe din," Ira said. "Mela ke waqt maine kaha tha aath. Ab usi rate se bhi tez — *chhe*." She opened the notebook to the gridded page and tilted it so all of them could see: the seven circled points around the cup-shape, the days ruled down the margin like a school timetable, each date given its figure. "Aarti waali raat: gyarah. Mela ke din: aath. Aaj: chhe. Rate tez ho raha hai. Chhote taps barah baje se ab dupahar ko bhi chalte hain. Jo pehle sirf raaton mein pee rhe the — ab wo din mein bhi mod sakte hain."

The four of them looked at the page the way people at a play look at the rain beginning over the stage.

"Toh cheezein delay nahi karti," Aadhya said.

"Delay nahi karti," said Ira. "Khushi ke din sabse bada collection hota hai. Ye formula yaad rakh lo. Mela ka din sabse tez gaya. Race ka din doosre number pe. Aarti teesre." She closed the notebook with its small leather slap. "Isliye aaj ka din count karta hai. Humne aaj ek dusre se apna darr baanta hai — ye bhi ek diya hai isi din ka — dekh lena, yeh tapakega kal raat. Kal se kaam."

Everyone heard the tank give its hushed steel gurgle, like something clearing its throat to agree.

Then Veer clapped his knees and stood, huge, relieved to be able to give anything to anyone. "Toh ab — koi meeting-log madad ki? Chai? Kuch bhi? Kyunki agar main aur ek second yahan beykaar baitha raha toh main tank ke upar chadh jaaunga —"

"Baith jaa," said Aadhya, not ungently, "dramebaaz."

— and after a while it was decided, formally, the way the four of them decided everything: by silence going unpunished just a little too long. Veer dragged the two cane chairs and the khaat-stool from the corner; Aadhya put the library-bag against the antenna's knee and sat on the parapet-coping with her legs dangling over two storeys of air as if air were handrails; Ira took the sundown corner with her back to the light like a lookout; Rudra leaned against the cool curve of the tank and let the steel hold some of his evening for him.

A normal sunset kept them company. Off to the northeast the city articulated its evening devotions in layers — bells from the Sankat Mochan side, a scooter-wallah's argument with his own brakes, the smells of six dinners beginning. Something neighbours would later describe as "bahut shaanti lag rahi thi bheed mein" — and under it all, the by-now familiar second transit of the dusk: the far dominion of harvested light doing its daily accounts.

It was Ira who began the real business. She did it with a quality of academic formaldehyde, as if conducting an examination no one was required to pass:

"Aaj se hum chaaron ke rules mein ek add hota hai," she told the corn packet. "Ek-ek baat. *Darr.* Sabse bada waala. Chaaron bolenge. Main shuru karti hoon."

"Ye kya group therapy game hai," Rudra said, alarmed.

"Ruk ja. Bolne de," said Aadhya. She was looking out at the thinning evening; the mela's memory still stood folded over the ghats — last night's diya-lines gone to trampled mud at their stations. "Bolo. Sun rahi hoon."

So Ira — archivist of tremors, self-appointed keeper of everything measurable — spoke facing the broken antenna, into its five bent arms, as into a conference table:

"Mujhe apne sapno ke *sach* hone se darr lagta hai." She steadied the words with the care of someone pouring into a narrow mouth. "Literal. Raaton waale. Bachpan se ek hi hai — ek andhera kamra, ek kursi, ek aadmi baitha hua ghutne pe talwaar liye, aur kisi ki aankhein jo dekh rahi hoti hain ki main dekh rahi hoon. Main hamesha sochti thi — ye dimaag ka pressure hai, orphanage ka residue, ghar ka intezaar. Par hafte bhar pehle mera dream aur mera compass dono ek hi cheez dikhane lage." A breath. "Sapne ka dar isliye nahi ki ve sach honge. Isliye ki ve *plan* hain. Kisi ka. Aur plan complete hote hi mera main-hona khatam ho jayega — main sirf uska naam rah jaoungi." She nodded once, recorder gone flat. "Likh lena. Yehi hai mera sabse bada. Agla bole."

Silence — the good kind — held the roof.

Then Veer rolled his corn-packet's sleeve in both fists and, like a man volunteering for roll-call, said:

"Mera sabse simple hai. *Useless reh jaana.*" A flat half-laugh, unloading. "Sab kuch ho raha hai — tum teeno ke andar kuch jagah banti jaa rahi hai — aur main... main wahan khada hoon photo mein. Bada aadmi. Lambe haath. Pillar pe haath rakh diya us raat — luck thi. Lekin agli baar? Agli baar agar wo luck na aayi — agar meri muthi phir *wahi* nahi hui jab zaroorat padi — toh main kya hoon? Khaana laane waala. Auto pakadne waala. Maa ke ghar ka lamp." He stopped; when he began again, his voice had gone somewhere knees-first into work. "Log kehte hain darr maut ka hota hai. Mujhe maut se darr nahi hai. Mujhe *faltu* se darr hai. Ki ek din tum log kisi aisi jagah khade hoge jahan bandook nahi flag kaam aayega — aur main wahan bas... *haath jod ke khada ho jaunga.* Vo din mere liye maut hai. Bas itna hi."

He finished red and looked immediately sorry for having taken a whole minute of the sunset. Nobody permitted him to regret it.

Under his rolled sleeve, along the old burn, the tan-gold lines flexed once as he unclenched his fist, and slept.

Aadhya took a while. Down in the last lane a cow looked over a wall at nothing; nobody waited on anyone.

"Mera darr aur bhi gadbad hai," she said. "Mujhe nahi lagta ki cheezein bigad jayengi. Mujhe lagta hai ki *main* bigad dungi — apne haathon se. Us raat ped ke paas jo mere haath jale — logon ne kaha kitna khoobsurat, kitna hara — par main sirf ye sochti hoon: jo cheez ped ke andar ghus sakti hai, wo insaan ke andar bhi ghus sakti hai. Ek din haath mila rahi thi apni classmate ko — usne touch kiya aur mujhse zyada usko achha laga — wo chill gayi itni achhi feeling mein, aur main raat bhar jaagi ki maine use *inject* toh nahi kar diya kuch?" She held her two hands out into the sunset, palms up, empty as caught birds being released. "Isliye ab main kisi se haath nahi milati. Isliye main pooja mein piche khadi hoti hoon. Mera sabse bada darr ye hai ki kabhi — galti se, mohabbat se, maa ko gale lagate waqt — meri ye cheez kisi apne ko khaa jaye aur mujhe pata bhi na chale ki kis waqt gate khula."

Her voice stayed level to the end of it, and the end of it undid her anyway: she wept — soundlessly, untheatrically, the weeping of a person whose books keep to their own accounting — and Ira's hand found her shoulder blade before either of the boys saw reason to move, flat-palmed, applying the kind of arithmetic a body can answer.

For a long time none of them attempted any language at all.

Then Rudra stood forward off the tank, and faced the whole small sunset, and delivered his, flat, clinically:

"Mujhe apne papa banne ka darr hai."

He let the sentence occupy the air. The building two lanes over sounded its brass plate against a steel vessel, like a clock.

"Roz subah dekhta hoon unhe aangan mein, chaar baje, roz — aur har saal thoda kam aadmi dikhayi deta hai, thoda zyada farz." He kept his eyes on the far roofs, where the TV antennas stood in their flocks like dead vegetation. "Pandrah saal pit-te hue, aur ek din bhi unhone yeh nahin bataya ki *kyun*. Main toota raha, woh jodta raha training se. Ab andar waali cheez phat rahi hai toh pehli khabar manzil se pehle meri zubaan pe likhi mili — *fever tha, theek hoon, kuch nahi hai.* Kis se seekha? Unhi se." He swallowed. "Toh mera darr ye nahi ki main bura banunga. Darr ye hai ki main unke jaisa ho jaunga — sahi rehte hue bhi akela, pilate bhi raho aur door bhi rakho. Ki ek din tum teeno ko meri jhooti OK-gaadi aise dekhoge jaise deewar dekhte ho. Aur poochna band kar doge."

He breathed out through the nose, once, and found the end of it: "Sabse zyada darr mujhe is baat ka hai ki ek din tum mera hisaab lena chhod doge. Ki tum heel samjhoge ke *ye toh ab aise hi hai.* Poochna mat chhodna. Mere andar kuch hai jo bola nahi jaata — tum bolna. Hamesha bolna. Ussi mein zinda rahoonga."

The sun came down like a struck cymbal into the city's west of roofs.

Nobody said *nahi yaar*, which was, right then, a mercy rare enough to be holy. The four-held thing by the tank simply stood in silence. Aadhya pressed once at the corners of her eyes with the heel of her wrist; Veer sat with his temple on his fist, the trophy of the race week standing somewhere below in his mother's puja-corner like a rumor of him; Ira, crouched by the tank's curve, scratched so small a pencil-line across her knee-level notebook that no one heard it go down.

— and Veer said, when the pause had been kind long enough:

"Toh isliye aaj hum ek niyaam banate hain. Sirf ek."

He sat up. There was in his whole frame then an uncharacteristic stillness, the gravity of the big sincere; all three of them straightened out of their slants to receive it.

"Peer promise. Ek dusre se. Kabhi bhi, kisi bhi din, andar ka darr agar daba ke rakha — toh bolna padega. Aur dusra —" he turned the flat of his palm at each of them in turn, careful, and included, "—dusra jhooth ka jawaab nahi dega. Koi bolega 'theek hoon, sab normal hai' — toh teen puchenge: *sach?* aur jawaab mein jhooth nahi chalega. Aadmi apne andar ki deewar ko girte hue pakadna chahta ho toh baaki teen rok sakte hain. Bas. Kaho."

The dusk slowly gathered its brightness up off the gulleys of roofs.

"Nahi normal hai," said Aadhya, testing the new phrase, finding it fit her. And one by one, in the laid-back tin hum of the lanes below, each voice came up:

"Sach puchenge. — Nahi normal." — Veer.

"Poochenge roz. — Nahi normal." — Ira.

"Nahi normal hai," Rudra said last, quietly, as something settled in his sternum — visibly gentled, listening; as if, under the tape and the stitches and the whole conspiracy of other people's protection, somebody had finally knocked from the outside and been told *come in.*

It was Ira who laughed first — a small round sound, thoughtless, out-loud, getting away from her like a parrot off its rope; then Aadhya, one arm in the air defending the flood in her face; then Rudra, losing the battle of his mouth-corners against his will in the half-dark; then Veer, who was to blame for all of it, whistled once in salute of the finished sun. The whole roof faced west for it, old antenna bent like something hailing home. The neighbourhood heard the clink of four cold drinks set down on a water tank, the ungoverned brightness of four nineteen-year-olds laughing down a day, and in rooms below, half a dozen people turned their heads toward it, and passed on, and smiled, and ached invisibly, with the old utterly particular grief of overheard youth.
After a while, over the pakora-tray's wreckage, they made the one operational decision of the evening.

"Kal se," Ira said, "raat ki ghumne ki list banegi. Do-do ka schedule. Ghat chabootra, kabhi Assi, kabhi Panchganga." She let the list settle in the air like a chalk-drawn circle. "Koi ladwai nahi. Koi naa-gaali nahi. Sirf dekhna. Sirf likhna. Cheez jab skill karti hai na — ka, kab, kis taraf se — mujhe dekhna hai. Aage jo bhi hona chahta hai, mere hisaab se vomit hi nahi hua chahta — poora prepared controller hua chahta hai."

"Toh ladki kehti hai planning, tum shaam ghumai bologi," said Veer, with a large grin entirely of relief. "Main ready hoon."

"Mummy ko bol dungi extra classes," said Aadhya. "Woh ab mujhpe —" she stopped, looked at the empty stairhead, translated a thought: "—bharosa kar rahi hain bina proof ke. Kal pooch bhi leti hain school mein agar uthaana ho toh uthaana padega. Figure out kar lungi."

"Ground rules," said Rudra.

"Ground rules abhi bane hain," Ira said. "Bas ek jo chahiye mujhe kal pencil mein—"

"Jo?"

"Bhaago mat," she said, quietly. Address to none of them, and all of them. "Jab bhi jo bhi hota hai — jal raha ho, bhaago, ya jhooth bolo mat bas — bhaago mat. Log jo bhaagte hain wo girte hain theli ki taraf. Chale hue jaate hain."

She nodded once, at the tank's edge.

There was a silence with a note of murmurs — then Rudra asked,

"Doosra?"

"Doosra tu pahle se jaanta hai," Ira said. "'Nahi normal hai.' Tum bolnoge na hamesha?"

"Bolega," Rudra said.

And because ceremonies, half-finished, smart like half-set bones, Veer stood up in the middle of their crew and raised his steel tumbler of nimbu-shikanji to the roof's sky and to nothing visible in the west.

"Toh formal kar dete hain," he announced. "Panchon ke saamne."

Ira tilted her head. "Panchon? Paanchwa kaun?"

"Tere padosi mein hai." He nodded, one small chin-dip, at her pocket where the compass lay against the heart-line. "Toh." The tumbler swung to the antenna: "Chaar log. Ek nazariya. Ek sheher. Aur ek cheez jiska naam abhi tak kisi ne nahi diya." No four steel tumblers on a rooftop have ever needed a linguist to add up to a toast. "Kal se — jab tak dar hai, jab tak naadaan hai, jab tak bhaagne ka mann hai — chaaron apni asli aankh se dekhna. Us hisaab se bolna. Bagair faltu ke."

"Veer bhai," Rudra said, dry as a ledger, "faltu nahi chahiye."

"Wahi toh keh raha hoon," said Veer, wounded. "Bagair *flatus* ke."

"Yeh shabd kahan se late ho tum?" Ira demanded.

"Bangalore. Meri mausi—"

"Accept," said Ira.

"Accept," said Aadhya, catching the last thread of the joke.

"Arre bhai —" Rudra raised his glass, having already lost the veto to the three voices on his roof — "Accept."

The four steel tumblers clicked on their rims over the city, all round, each edge touching each, in the dusk. The shikanji was evening-warm by then, faintly grainy, ice long surrendered; and they drank it standing, four of them on a rented roof, like people sworn in under a tent of evening; and if any other roof on the street mistook the small quiet orchestra of it for boys humming farewells in a neem-shadowed quarter, let it: some oaths read better small.

Below and west, through the cloud's last veil, a light came on in a roof-house near the college — went off — came on — off — *twice*, measured, like somebody passing a window twice to count the lane. Ira, watching without being watched, glanced at her wrist and said, too low to belong to any toast:

"Woh register waala saaf nahi hai. Kal se tum do apana yahee chakkar—"

She stopped. The light stayed off. Nobody else had seen it. The tank gurgled again, consenting to the whole arrangement of the evening, and the four glasses went empty with a last small hydrological clink, four lives raising the smallest possible toast entirely to itself.

---

Dismount was nine, across the hatch, down the tilted staircase where every resident for thirty years had put a hand on the same rail. At the bottom the lane began: dinner's smells, the music-system family adjusting one hour less loud, the carrom board going past all accounting below a porch tube-light.

They stood in the very middle of it for a while as a single unit, because the evening had got so freighted with plans and ceremonies that nobody could find who should signal the dispersal. Veer solved it, in his fashion: got the bicycle out of its lock and turned it into the send-off for all of them:

"Chal — kal paanch baje milte hain. College ke peeche wali chai pe. Aur bhaagne ka nahi, haan?" — thigh over the bar, one foot's flirting pedal — "Jitna zor se hum apas mein baant rahe hain na, utna hi bayan ho raha hai ki cheez hamari taraf tez aa rahi hai."

Then he was off, chain-song going cheerful down the lane.

Ira went opposite-wise: slight quick figure, one hand raised behind her in goodbye without turning, half her brain already counting something in units only she collected.

Aadhya and Rudra walked the last two turns to the rickshaw stand in the quiet of a hundred little homes' supper-endings. On the bench, two boys too young for the hour flicked a cigarette's ember back and forth like a counter; the sign-painter's shutter stood half-down; the milk-seller counted change for the record. She climbed into the auto sideways before it started and gave him back the whole evening in one look:

"Jo tune aaj bola, Rudra — ki poochna mat chhodna — woh sun ke mujhe bahut bada thehrav mila. Poochte rahenge. Chaahe tu jawab de, chaahe chup kar de."

"Kal paanch baje," he said. "College ke peeche wali chai. Adrak double."

"Baat yahin theek hai, double se," she said, and the auto took her away with its patient sputter, which sounded at that hour like the whole lane saying its own goodnight.

---

Home. Gate, lamp, door. He did not pass the storeroom this time: he stopped, raised three wrapped fingers, and knocked twice on the centre panel, despite the hour.

Inside: a settling sound, leather and tools remembering their arrangement. Then: "Aaja."

On the threshold, framed in it, Rudra said to the man who sat working an awl through old strap-leather in the room's collected dusk:

"Kal se main raat ko bahar ghumunga. Veer ke saath. Ghat ki taraf. Bas — socha bata doon."

The awl came out of the leather and paused. Ram did not lift his eyes; he did not need to. His stillness was all in the perfect lowered hang of the hand.

"Jaa," he said at last.

"Aur kuch?" Rudra asked — because the whole day's interrogation had taught him the inventory of his father's permissions, and he expected a list.

The awl went back in. "Prashant ke ladke se mat ladna," his father said — the neighbourhood's dusty smuggler-son, rumour of cricket-bets and quarrel. Then, lower, after the kind of silence in which a man rehearses a larger sentence and puts it back in the drawer: "…aur mere bina mat karna woh."

"Woh kya?" Rudra said.

"Seene par tape laga ke bahar jaane wala kaam," said Ram, precisely, without needing to be told.

The air between them did the thing that air in that house had been doing since before Rudra knew its name: it filled with nineteen years of rescue and went out the door on its own.

"Theek hai," said Rudra. "Ghar pe jaldi aa jaunga."

The awl paused only once more — a man's full allowance of emotion — and went on with the strap.

"Rukmini ke saath dinner pe ruk jaana," Ram said at the moment of his son's exit's first step, almost gently, in the dialect they'd worn all their lives: *eat with your mother.*

"Haan ji," said Rudra.

---

Upstairs the house closed itself like a practicing installation. And Rudra fell asleep that night with one palm flat against the taped chest, practising not being lonely, while two rooms down a lullaby without words climbed thread by thread up the dark well of the staircase and lay, at the top of it, like a folded blanket nobody was any longer certain had not been there since the house began.

They gave the thing no name; the thing was forming nonetheless; and below them the lanes sang up their habitual colours, and beyond the river — measured in her room far away with the dampness at her wrist cooling westwards — the days left said six, and held.

---

# CHAPTER 17 — THE RAKSHAS

Friday came up out of the river-fog like a creditor's knock.

Ira was at the chai stall at five exactly — college-peeche-wali, where the adrak went in double and the biscuit tin had rusted in a decorative way — notebook already open on the tin table, held flat by a stone. The other three arrived in ones and twos, out of breath, out of excuses, entirely present. The stall's radio burbled the local evening news: a second water-main failure, this one on the west end. Nothing visible. Everything on record.

"Paanch," Ira said, in place of greeting.

Nobody asked paanch-kya. Not any more.

"Kal raat," Ira said, flipping a page, "jab hum terrace pe the — toast, glass, glass ka chhin — maine kahan tha ki woh bhi ek diya hai. Subah tak kheench liya gaya. Aaj subah naya count liya." She touched the number in the margin with two fingers, the way you touch a letter from someone who cannot write back. "Chhe se seedha — *paanch.*"

"Ek hi raat mein ek din gira?" Veer put his elbow down slow.

"Tum chaaron jab ek saath sach bolte ho na," said Ira, "toh ek jagah bahut roshni ek saath jal jaati hai." She drew the stub of pencil across the notebook — the city's circle, seven points, and the heavy dot at the heart. "Unko wohi chahiye. Mela ke din double gaya tha. Kal raat poora ek din zar gaya. *Khushi sabse amir anna hai unki kheti mein.*"

"Toh ab? Khush mat ho?" Aadhya asked, dry.

"Nahi." Ira shut the notebook. "Khushi rok nahi sakte — rokna bhi nahi chahiye, varna jeet ke baad zinda kaise raho ge. Lekin *ginti* toh chalni chahiye. Har din subah main measure lungi. Tumlog apne-apne kaam jaldi-se-jaldi pakad lo. Har chore who thing — jaldi, aur wapas." Her eyes went once to Rudra's kurta — to the taped chest under it — and away, clean as a scalpel. "Aur jo chipaya hua hai raaz, usse dekhte raho. Akele mat dekho."

The chai came. Steam went up. Below the table, out of the stall's little light, the evening opened its first door.

Aadhya had carried something since Wednesday and could not carry it alone any more. "Kal do baje main Shastri-ji se mili," she said. "Unhone apni diary dikhai. Chhattis saal ka apna register — jo kaata gaya sarkari kaagaz se, woh unhone raat raat bhar utara. Ek naam baar-baar aata hai. *Ek widow ka.* Uski line ne stations sambhale — sau saal tak. Aur phir poora naam hi kaat diya gaya, jagah-jagah." She laid her ruled page flat; on its corner, in an old blue hand, the diary-margin sentence she had copied: *last survivor's family — address destroyed.* "Register mein teesri entry — pehli baar likhne waala — nineteen saal pehle. Officer jaisa haath. Naam ka pehla akshar *R*."

She said it without looking at Rudra, and Rudra did not move, and the other two watched both of them with the care of people who have promised to ask the truth and are paying an instalment early.

"Aur mujhe follow kiya ja raha hai," Aadhya added, levelly. "College gate. Akhbaar peeche se padhne waala."

"Sirf tumhe nahi," Rudra said. He did not point. They followed his eyes: across the lane, the same grey windcheater, today employed as a rickshaw-repair man, a wrench leaning unused in one patient hand. "Kal se hum inse chhupte nahi. Chhupna unka kaam hai — humara nahi. Dekhenge seedha. Bhaagne ka nahi. Aur kisi ko chot nahi — apni jaan bachani hai, bas."

"Bas," Ira agreed. "Apni jaan. Ghar waqt pe. Sab ko sab bataana."

They drank. The sun slid lower. Somewhere under the table, under the street, under the whole tired beautiful city, the heart knock the crews had learned to hear gave its nine-second note, a fraction louder than yesterday, and none of them said so aloud.

---

The city's darkness arrived between two chutneys. Rudra went home; he had given his word. Rukmini sat by the window with the kanjeevaram pulled over her shoulders against the cooling air, reading the week's folded paper, her sore foot resting on a stack of folded boat-blankets that still smelled vaguely of the race.

"Mainaaj aath tak aa jaunga," Rudra told her.

She nodded without looking up, considered the paper's wedding announcements again, and said, "Jamun ka paudha aa raha hai." She had wanted, since before her operation, a small fruit tree for the courtyard's blind corner. "Shaam-shakkar waala nahi. Asli jamun. Bholenath wale laaye hain mandi se, kal lagana."

"Haan," he said. And there was no strangeness in the courtyard at all, just a woman and a boy and the leaves that were coming.

Ram was upstairs at the storeroom lamp. The lane's evening hush had settled onto the doors as though an invisible army of sweepers had passed. Rudra washed, ate standing against the wall, listening to the downstairs clock hum its way toward the hour of its own nightly prayer.

At seven-forty he stood in the storeroom doorway, hands loosely together, saying nothing.

"Jaa," Ram said, without lifting his head from the awl. "Le apna sab saath."

And so the chest-tape went on, in the two-minute laying of cool neem it always was, thong and diagonal wrap drawn with the practiced cruelty of mercy across the heat that had been building under his ribs all day. His father's hands were steady as instruments. When it was done, the eyes met once, and in them was neither question nor permission — only the private arithmetic of a man who had survived four decades of knowing, and who had promised himself he would never explain. They said their ordinary small goodnight; and the boy went out into the lane with his heart wrapped exactly as it was safe for his heart to be wrapped, and it occurred to him — as it had begun to occur to him regularly now — that someday soon, some night, the tape would not be enough, and what would happen then was a question his father's shoulders were carrying on both sides of the door.

"Jaana," Ram said after him — barely voice.

Rudra went. And the door shut behind him in its accustomed neutral way, which was as close to a blessing as that doorway manufactured.

---

Ira and Veer occupied the Panchganga chabootra by eight. The ghat's nameplate stood in moonlight, black script on bleached stone, and the river below rode its evening way in long frothy pools, each lung of the bank breathing in and out behind its ruined towers. At the corner of the dusk the ghat assembled its usual orchestra — the tin whistles of birds who never accepted the city's clocks, the slow knocking of footsteps on old stone, the snapped-then-relit sparks of a belated aarti drifting down on the air like a rumour. And beneath it all, beneath every sense the naked ear could teach them, the old bones of the laid-earth lines gave their small insistent tock — at intervals of nine seconds, one two, as though deep below something were trying to come awake off their side's schedule and on no one's permission.

"Itna bhaari hai aaj," Ira said, mostly to her wrist-strip. "Kaafaa nahi raha. Bhaari."

"Kya fir se wo jagah hai?" Veer asked. Yehi hai na — Panchganga."

"Yehi tha. Yehi se hi." She put the strip back on her wrist, knotting it tighter than before. "Jagaah waali ghat. Sadaa se pehla."

Others used the stair: a bent old figure slid triple-bowed into a sitting niche; a woman with a hen in a basket went the way she had come, her eyes on Veer's arm a half-second too long for comfort but exactly the length people saw things. They kept the peace. Twice more the earth gave its underground punctuation, the second time soft enough that Veer could not tell argument from instruction. It landed in the knot under his chest and became, for the briefest second, something he would have called kind, if nothing else could have been.

Then came the sound of mud, laid down heavily on the steps above the water in a way that meant *somebody was very wet.* Veer heard it the way his mind remembered hearing an outrider approach: outside, at a distance, in the reeds across the water's longer pace.

Except that what came out of the reeds could not have been someone.

He could not move a foot. These night-things do not live on walking; they live on stillness, on the pauses of everyone who sees them. It came on along the bank-shoulder like a refugee from the entire condemned neighbourhood of daylight. It was rolling its head feebly against the wind like a drunk ignoring stars. There was a face.

The face was old in a way he could not have named — not old at the elbows, not old in the gums; old like brick in the sun, old like ash from a decree. It was not staring at them so much as it was letting them have the first look, the way one allows the people of the neighbourhood the first sight of a stranger one owns.

It leaned over and smiled, or what was left for it to smile smiled, for the dented. The whole left cheek was gone through the skin down to something clean, and through the opening Veer could see that the skull behind was not quite close to a shape.

"Baanch raha," it said then, in something close to Bhojpuri-ribbed old speech — a voice whose middle register was all worm-eaten and whose vowel boxes seemed to have been reknotted language by the unwise — "maine tumhare raaz suna hai. Hum baar-aankhon ko is sheher mein. Charon. Charon ki aankhen bhari hui hain. Kya tum sochte ho ki main itni door se aaya hoon tumse sirf *prem* se baat karne?"

And then it came forward, lazily, with the slow exhausted economy of a very old animal-god who no longer has the convention of human pride in attendance, its ruined legs moving in burst-cloth with the rhythmic **swish of blown ash for feet**; its face black-hinge-arguable under the convenient dark; its eyes grey as moth-ashes.

Veer heard himself say, entirely unwisely, "Jhuke re—"

"Veer nahi," said Ira in the steadiest voice she had ever owned. And then down the wind, from Assi's direction, a far-call and a far-answer in a pause, and Ira's hand shot flat onto the notebook's page as she counted two seconds of the thing's attention and matched them against the route of her own panic. "Wo campus jaa raha hai," she said. "Bhaago mat. Yaad rahe — bhaago mat — toh chalo—"

and the thing laughed at the top of the Panchganga stair for one ancient second and turned — and went. Sheer vacancy followed like a struck bow.

---

By eight twenty-three the college's western gates had shut their thin cricket-less night, and the thing that had come up along the river arrived under the library arches as naturally as a wall walks.

It had gone around the city to reach them.

Aadhya, who was standing in the quadrangle with her arms full of borrowed translation-pages, never knew who screamed first. She knew that she heard her own name screamed by a girl in the corridor, turned, and came face-on to the thing hovering over the same patch of grass Shastri-ji had tended every Tuesday and Saturday since before she'd been born. She gave herself no explanation for how quickly she was moving; she was moving quickly for no reason her body refused to accept. She ran between the columns without a word and put herself directly in its path.

It paid her the compliment of attention. Its neck came up, full-face, teeth, and its mouth moved.

"Aaiye," it said, in its rotted polite cadence. "Mera neta." It took one slow step, and the grass under its assenting foot turned white — a ring of white that bled outward and outward with no botanical logic, and kept growing, feet to yards, until even Aadhya standing at the quadrangle's centre felt the earth *compel* feet to frontage. "Chhattis saal se tere paas kuch hai — le aao."

The pale ring hit the edge of the quad. Her hands were already raised.

She did not think. There was no thought and no thought-spared. Her palms closed on nothing and the whole vegetable account of the quadrangle's kingdom whisked into her tightening fists with a speed no gardener on this planet had ever possessed: scattered green beads burnt and flew from her fingers like sparks beaten from cold iron, whirling, sticking, returning from invisible wires dense in spatial weight and dark in colour and only partially intelligent — and then they collided with the ground's green and did not ask anything.

The grass of the quadrangle flew forward at her command — not fast grass, not wind-tossed grass: a quick-flung sheet of thousand-year green, torn up from its laziness into purpose.

It hit the thing's half-human chest and did not push it. It *grabbed.* Newly sprouted — impossibly sprouted — shoots of pea-vine and ashoka and twenty feet of jasmine tendon sank split rootlets around the black-cowled tangle of the thing, whose startled many-mouths drew in a deep red oblique of reflex; and then the ropes flowered over it shoulder to chin: a woven green fabric the size of a gate, winding and tightening, hauling life out of lawn after lawn in three seconds of one sustained gasp —

Aadhya stood at its source, arms straightened stiff as a pyramid's flag, and neither knew nor wanted to know: her nails bled; her face was pale.

The thing swung. Ropes coiled, peeled, split ivy; it fed on one strand of jasmine and recoiled mid-scream (a scream of grass screaming back), and it came again, and she wrapped it again, twice as hard. Veer vaulted over a low hedge and hit the ground carrying her weight before she could fall; somewhere behind her Ira had both hands to her own mouth counting in tandem seconds.

Then out of nowhere — down the three-hundred-year-old stair toward the college's shattered gate — Rudra came.

He came through them. The tape was heavier than usual across his ribs that evening, riding with body's promised laughter; afterward he could not have told if a wave of fury had passed under it, or whether he had brought it himself in his hands. He ran barefoot across the lawn-footing, striding once mad under lamplight, climbed the vine's own ascending green ramp without bearing any particular course, cut the air to the thing's left flank at a hanging angle, and hit it with all of himself, from the same side and in the same direction:

*his whole body, knotted in land-and-ring, and a scream no word ever owned.*

The impact carried it through the veil of green she massed between her people, tore it out of the staff-and-rope wrap, and threw it across the quad to the foot of a lamp-post. It landed spun, and Rudra hit it again — in the head at the edge of its temples, twice as hard as it knew, and his hands broke nothing at all each time, trembled from the nerves that his skin refused to accept as bruised — and again, and again.

Between the second and third strikes the air around him went colourless. The tape on his chest peeled backwards under the mutiny of how much he was trying not to give; then split, not wildly this time but *flat,* wide, and brilliant — flowing into his wide-open palm — and the restless bright that neither sky nor earth could contain now spat outward, striking no thing and no shadow, covering everything at once that this huge erupting unprotected brightness could choose: the bottom of his fist ploughed the dirt and carried the entity splashing, gasping, six feet down into the quad's mud.

Not cast. Not pinned. Driven, by root and land together.

The black outline of it was gone.

Its eyes rallied dimly — a couple of new mouths for how many servants above it had fed at this trough over its long life, no one could tell — and in the last slow ratio of joint to breath it found the grass again: one complete looking-out of a non-lidded intelligence, paused. Its chest and throat arched.

And the entire dying shadow said low, sagaciously, in a Bhojpuri two generations older than Rudra, with an amen-smile leaking dark from its lips — each word carrying, exactly, the weight of every question the boy had been forbidden for nineteen years to ask:

*"You don't even know what you are, Asuran child."*

The breath shed. The shadow of the body unraveled, not with a battle-cry. Only the dead *use* of the words remained in the air, ground out to a grain, ground in under the brown quad.

Then the air came back.

---

Aadhya's vines were already withering where the grass had turned white around the thing's first footfall; the rest subsided tenderly, letting tendrils fall back into soil as if ashamed of having trusted. The quad rang empty.

Rudra stood over the sunken earth with his hands shaking in the hot air and turned slowly in place. Three faces looked back at him. Nobody had words; the twenty-second pause passed, as it passes when a thing is finished and nobody yet knows what it was called.

He could assemble only one sentence out of the scene:

*Asuran child.*

His ears rang with the ancient vowel-colour of it. Under the college's broken night, with half the quadrangle's grass folded back where the thing had passed, he saw how quickly the evening had collapsed from private to public theatre. In twelve heartbeats the black outline in the mud began a patient downward migration — sunken, dripped away, folded into the ground through some wormhole of its own knowing — and was gone before anyone could have stopped it or named it.

"Kya tha," Rudra said at last. "Kya tha woh *aakhri line* —"

"Theek mat socho," said Aadhya. She was sitting on her own splayed hands on the lowest stair, hair plastered to one cheek, refusing every hand Veer offered her. The skin-pads of her fingers were bleeding where the vine-threads had drawn their toll, and she was not crying; she was watching the place where the earth refused to give the thing a decent grave. "Kal sochenge. Aaj — saans."

"Ira kahan hai."

"Yahan hoon," said Ira, eyes still shut, on the second stair. She stood at last. Her face was not a witness's face. It was the face of a clerk who has watched which bricks of a bridge blew. "Rudra. *Tape.*"

Not a question. A count. He looked down: somewhere between the second and third strike the chest-tape had come undone — not torn by the enemy, unwound from inside, a string loose of its own fatigue — and the chest beneath had been bare to everything for three full seconds.

"Maine koshish ki thi —" he began.

"Tune tape ke bina *saara* kavach zameen pe gira diya," she said, steady and exact. "Aur neeche kuch sunta rehta hai hamesha. Meri wrist-strip — dekh."

She held it out. The strip was still warm. The dot of darkness she marked nightly had swelled by a ring she had never marked before: not an eighth-station dot. A radiance-ring, the record of something large answering from underneath, the way a well answers a dropped stone.

"Kal se," Ira said, to all of them, to the dark, to herself — "hum estimate nahi karte apni sides. Kal se hum apne aap ko *measure* karte hain. Rules same hain. Bhaago mat. Sach bolna. Apni jaan." She looked around slowly, once, at each of them. "Aur ek naya — *koi bhi dheere se nahi dekh sakta ki uski body kya bol rahi hai. Koi bhi akela nahi bolega apne mooh mein uski aakhri baat.*"

Veer made a rumbling assent without opening his folded arms.

She opened the notebook's back page and wrote, with unusual slowness, in a hand she would deny later:

*Rudra ne tape ke bina chaaron ko bachaya. Zameen ne dekh liya. Meri strip ne sun liya. Darwaza neeche bhi sun raha hai. Main darwaza nahi kholungi khud.*

She shut it before the earth could file a comment.

Veer did a slow circuit of the quad alone while the others steadied themselves. At the white ring's edge he crouched, touched the dead grass — no warmth, no cold, no pull in his wrist: it was *finished*. It was the first thing of the enemy's making he had seen in weeks that finished instead of collecting, and the difference between the two verbs shook him worse than the fight.

He came back and reported it in his own unconsulted vocabulary: "Jo kha liya — uska hisaab khatam kar diya. Wapas kuch nahi aayega udhar se. Main poora ghoom aaya. Sab diye hue the."

"Tabhi toh," Ira said quietly, "shuru mein kam, baad mein zyada." Nobody translated it. All four understood in the body: *the taps yield most when the accounts are freshest*.

Then Aadhya held up her hands in the lamplight. The knuckle-pads had stopped bleeding. Around her smallest finger, pressed beneath the skin like a welt seen through glass, ran a thread of colour the exact warm gold of a marigold petal. She turned the finger slowly. Nobody had words for it. Ira wrote it down anyway, with the time.


---

They separated late and in pairs, leaving behind an empty quadrangle where the brown earth was slowly furling itself over the hollow, and where a hundred pale new grass-threads were already replacing the ruined lawn in obedience to tomorrow's timetables, as though tonight had taught nobody anything except, perhaps, grass.

Aadhya sat on Veer's cycle-carrier and held on with both hands and said not a word for nine minutes. Ira rode ahead on her rusted machine, veering badly at crossings, stopping herself, counting under her breath. Between them they offered two jokes in exchange, broken-off, kindly; each was declined politely by the evening.

Rudra walked. He walked through the unlit alley and across the bridge the boat-race had contested with torches and neon sails, and did not run anywhere — there was no surface left in the city tonight on which to run that would not have witnessed whatever his ribcage was doing under the new skin.

He said it once, aloud, on the bridge, at the empty water:

"You don't even know what you are" — and then, in the tone a man uses to weigh mustard seeds — "kya hoon main…"

Nothing answered. He was not sure whether he had wanted it to.

Below him the river negotiated its channels. Somewhere far and damp, in the grid of the city's underground, the old seven marks burned faintly at their posts like seven coals that had forgotten what fire was, and the newest warmth — the pale ring his friend's hands had left in a college lawn — subsided last and slowest into the soil, admitting to itself it might outlive the pause.

Then he went home, alone, at walking pace, as if at a funeral nobody had announced.

Two turns from home, Ram Yadav's door opened on a lantern held by a hand more steady than any face at eleven at night should have to be greeted by.

He said, to nobody in particular, into the vestibule's air: "Rukmini. Paani nikalo."

The father stood a long way inside the doorway holding the lantern where it belonged, and watched his son come up the lane: shirt torn at the diagonal where the wrap had burst, wrists bruised dark along their flex-lines, eyes carrying too much that had already been spent successfully. The veteran's gaze made one full transit over the body — the way it always did — and asked nothing with it. *How* was not a currency that traded at that door.

"Aye," said Ram at last, to the lane and the night beyond it: "Dubara mat karna — mere bina."

He let his son pass.

"Main theek hoon," Rudra said, into the cold stair air.

"Andar," said Ram, finishing it.

Rukmini rose without sound. She came up with a tray — cold well-water, rock-salt on the rim of a steel glass — and looked at her son's chest the way field surgeons look: unloaded, unstartled, every bruise assigned its shelf in some account only mothers keep permanently updated. Rudra drank in two slow drafts, and the evening's unmatched labour finally went out of him in one long exhale. His mother touched his forehead once with the back of two fingers and let it go.

"Kal jamun lagaenge," she said, looking at the tray, not at his face. "Subah. Aap dono. Main rukne waali nahi hoon."

Behind them, Ram watched the lane until the moon leaned out from the neighbour's awning and corrected its station. He put both hands behind his back. Nineteen years of practiced peace gathered in his shoulders and reached, as from a great height, one sentence's worth of descent; the rope of it tugged once, twice; nothing arrived. The father gave no word except the one the doorway had been built around, the boy's whole life:

"Jaana."

Upstairs, the house's lamps went down one by one in their usual order — kitchen, gallery, storeroom — and the last lamp, the one in his father's hand, held out longest, as lamps do when the thing they are guarding is walking away from them up a flight of stairs.

---

Below the whole city the old line took its measure of the night. Seven faint coals along the ancient ring burned at their posts — and an eighth warmth, newest by exactly one evening, subsided slowly into a college lawn and joined their count without being invited, the way a new star files itself quietly in an old constellation. What had been a ring of seven was, for one night, a ring with a hollow at its heart no longer empty: refilled without permission, fed without ceremony, noticed nowhere.

*You don't even know what you are.*

Half a month ago he could have thought those words and stayed himself. He no longer could.

---

# CHAPTER 18 — THE WORD "KUL"

The college, in the morning, was completely itself. This was the most frightening thing about Saturday.

Sweepers swept the quadrangle. The peons cocooned dead leaves into gunny sacks with their morning grumbles; two girl-students arrived early for library seats, complaining about the mid-semester time-table; the guard at the west gate was arguing with the milk van about parking. The pale ring in the grass had left, on drying, only a faint salt-coloured circumference — the sort of blemish the city produced every monsoon and the municipality never treated. Nobody from the hostels had heard anything. Nobody at the chowki had filed anything. By ten AM a gardener with a rake had redistributed the topsoil over the sunken hollow so evenly that the earth itself appeared to have decided to settle the case out of court.

The four of them arrived before the gardener's second pass, at dawn, and thus got their one chance at the hollow before the city administered it.

They stood around it in the grey light in the formation they always took now without discussing it — Ira at the compass bearing, Veer with his body between the others and the lane, Aadhya crouched nearest the earth, Rudra with his hands held away from everything, carefully, as if his hands were tools that had to stay on their hooks until told otherwise.

"Neeche khula tha aur phir mudh gaya," Ira said quietly, notebook out. "Yahan jo gehraayi dikhi thi raat ko — ab woh nahi hai. Mitti *wapas le aayi* apni gehraayi. Yeh normal nahi hai. Jameen audit karti hai, apne hisaab se."
